# Second Serve: Arc 3 chapters (Tokyo expedition and summer camp)

## Chapter 30: Red pen

Tanaka was already on the step when Sho came up the hill on Monday, sitting on it with the key on its red string hanging from 1 finger and his chin on his fist, watching the bottom of the path.

When Sho came round the corner by the shrine Tanaka stood up and put the key in the lock and turned it. *Clack.*

It was 4:56. The air was already warm, the 1st properly warm morning of the summer, and the sky over the school had gone pale and clean, with 1 long thin cloud lying over the hills behind the gym.

"Early," said Sho.

"EARLIER THAN YOU," said Tanaka, and pushed the door open with his whole arm.

They got the net out of the storage room. It was a 2-person job, and for a week the net had stayed rolled up on its pole in the corner, because Tanaka had been on his own. Tanaka took the left post and Sho took the right, and they walked it out across the floor between them, the net sagging in the middle like a hammock. The left post's crank stuck halfway, the way it always did, and Tanaka hit it with the heel of his hand and it went. Sho held the post steady while Tanaka cranked. The cable came up tight. Tanaka slapped the tape with his flat hand, and it hummed.

"Light," said Tanaka. "Coach said light."

"Light."

They passed. Back and forth across the net, slow, the ball going up and down between them in the blue light from the high windows. Tanaka in his FIGHTING SPIRIT shirt with the backward R, yelling "NICE!" when Sho passed it to him and "MINE!" when it came back, as if there were anybody else in the gym it could belong to.

After a while Tanaka started hitting it at him instead, slow down-balls, 1 a little to Sho's left, then a little to his right, then 1 short, dropping in front of him. Sho got his feet to all of them and his arms under all of them, and the ball went up, 1 after another, and came down in Tanaka's hands on the other side of the net.

At 5:40 Tanaka caught the ball and stood holding it.

"Your eyes."

"Fine."

"Fine fine? Or fine like you said with the nose?"

"Fine fine."

Tanaka gave him the intimidation face, which on Tanaka also meant he was thinking. Then he nodded once, hard, threw the ball up, and spiked it at the floor by Sho's feet, a little harder than before. Sho was already there. It went straight up off his arms, high and clean, and came down into Tanaka's hands.

"Okay," said Tanaka. "Good."

---

At the start of practice that afternoon, before warm-up, Ukai stood in front of the club with his clipboard.

"Monday," he said. "Exams. Who's in trouble."

Nobody moved.

"I said Monday. It's Monday. Who's in trouble."

Slowly, from the middle of the half circle, Hinata's hand went up. A beat later, from a different part of the half circle, Kageyama's hand went up.

Then Noya's. Then, after a long silence, Tanaka's, at shoulder height, as if he were hoping it wouldn't count if it didn't go all the way.

"Which subjects," said Ukai.

"All of them," said Hinata.

"English," said Kageyama. "And the rest."

"English," said Noya. "And math. And Classical. And maybe history."

Ukai looked at Tanaka.

"Classical," said Tanaka, to the floor. "I got 33 last time."

Down the half circle, somebody made a small sound. Tanaka's head came round toward the back of the room, toward Sho, with his eyes narrowed. Sho kept his face exactly where it was.

"33's a pass," said Ukai. "Barely. Who are you studying with."

"Noya. At my place." Tanaka scratched his head. "My sister says she'll make us. She says if we don't pass she'll drive us to Tokyo herself, and we'll have to sit in the back with the dog."

"Fine. And you 2." Ukai looked at Hinata and Kageyama, a few places apart, both staring at the floor. "Who are you studying with?"

Hinata opened his mouth. Kageyama opened his. They both shut them again.

"Sort it out," said Ukai. "By tomorrow." He turned a page on his clipboard. "Warm up."

---

The receive drill was the one they did every day. Ukai on a chair on the far side of the net with a cart of balls, serving floats. The receivers coming through in a line, Daichi, Noya, Asahi, Tanaka, Ennoshita, Narita, Kinoshita, round and round, and the pass going up to Suga at the net, who caught it and dropped it in a basket.

"Kiyose." Ukai pointed at the floor beside the end line, behind the receivers. "There. You call. Who passed, and if it's good. Every 3rd turn you take 1 yourself, light."

"Yes."

Ukai served. Daichi passed.

"Daichi-san. Good."

Noya. "Noya-san. Good."

Asahi. "Asahi-san. A bit high."

Tanaka. The ball came off his arms, and Sho heard it go wrong before he saw it, the flat hard note it made when it hit the bone, and his eyes went to Tanaka's left arm, which was lower than his right, and then to the ball, which was going short.

"Tanaka-senpai. Left. Short."

Suga had to take a step in for it.

"I KNOW," said Tanaka.

"Keep calling," said Ukai, from the chair.

Ennoshita. "Good." Narita. The sound came, stiff and straight-armed, the same sound Asahi made, and Sho's eyes went to the arms that had made it, and it was Narita. "Narita-san. Good." Kinoshita. "Kinoshita-san. Short."

The line went round again, and again. Somewhere in the 3rd time round Sho noticed that Noya, after each of his own passes, was turning his head a fraction toward the back of the court. Waiting for the call. When it came, "Noya-san, good," he punched the air, small, by his hip.

Daichi kept his head still. After Sho said "Daichi-san, a bit fast," Daichi's next pass came up off his arms softer, with more air under it, and dropped into Suga's hands like something being set down on a table.

Tanaka, after his 3rd "Left," stood in the line and lifted his left arm and looked at it as if it belonged to someone else. When his turn came round again he bent lower, and the ball came off both arms evenly, and Sho said "Tanaka-senpai, good," and Tanaka yelled "HA!" so loud that Ukai's next serve went into the net.

"Oi."

"Sorry, Coach!"

Every 3rd turn Sho went to the front of the line and took a pass himself.

The 1st was easy, a float straight at him, and he passed it up to Suga, a little toward the antenna. The 2nd was the same. Then, on his 3rd turn, Ukai looked at him over the cart of balls a beat longer than usual before he tossed.

Sho watched him.

Ukai's toss went up, a little further to his right than the others had, and his shoulder opened as he swung, turning out toward the sideline. His hand came through the ball flat, and Sho's feet were already going, 1 step back and to the right, before the ball had crossed the net. The ball came over flat and fast toward the seam between him and Noya, dipping in the last 3 meters, and he was there, and it came into his arms, and he let it go up, high, soft, in the middle of the court.

Suga caught it without moving his feet.

"Nice," said Suga.

On the chair, Ukai had the next ball in his hand and wasn't tossing it.

"You went before it crossed," said Ukai.

"His shoulder opened, Coach. Yours. You turned it out."

Ukai looked at his own shoulder, as if it had told on him.

"Hm," he said. He tossed the next ball. "Again."

Sho went to the back of the line. At the end of the drill Ukai got down off the chair and came round the net with his clipboard. He'd been keeping a column of little ticks the whole time, Sho saw, between serves.

"How many'd you call wrong?"

"3. Maybe 4."

"I had you at 4." Ukai tucked the clipboard under his arm. "Tomorrow, same. Ears and eyes. You hear it, you look where it tells you."

He walked away across the court, and at the far end Sho heard him say, not loudly, to Takeda, who'd come in during the drill with a stack of papers, "Kid reads servers like a book," and Takeda say, "Is that good?" and Ukai say, "It's IQ. You can't teach it," and blow his whistle.

---

On Tuesday at lunch Sho went down to the 1st-year corridor to buy bread and stopped by the noticeboard halfway along it, because at the far end, by the window outside class 1-4, Tsukishima was leaning on the sill with his headphones round his neck and a carton of milk tea, and Yamaguchi was beside him eating an onigiri, and Hinata had just come up the stairs at the other end of the corridor at a run.

Hinata saw Tsukishima. He slowed down.

From the vending machines in the alcove halfway along, Kageyama came out with a carton of milk. He saw Tsukishima too, and stopped.

He saw Hinata. Hinata saw him.

Both of them turned and looked at Tsukishima.

Then they both started walking toward the window, Hinata from the stairs and Kageyama from the alcove, at almost exactly the same speed, each with his eyes fixed on Tsukishima and nowhere else.

Sho read the poster on the noticeboard. It was about the swimming pool.

Hinata got there 1 step ahead. He stopped in front of Tsukishima and bowed, deep, the whole way down. Kageyama arrived a step later, a meter away from Hinata, and stood there with the carton of milk in his hand and his mouth slightly open, and didn't bow.

"Please teach us!" said Hinata, to the floor. "For exams! Please!"

Tsukishima looked down at the top of Hinata's head. Then at Kageyama, who was still standing up straight.

"And the King?" said Tsukishima. "Is he asking too, or is he just here for the view?"

Kageyama's face went through several things. His jaw worked. The carton in his hand made a small crumpling noise.

"...Please," said Kageyama. It came out of him like a cork coming out of a bottle. Then he bowed too, abruptly, from the waist, a little too far, so that the milk carton nearly hit the floor. "Teach us. English. And the rest."

Yamaguchi had put his onigiri down on the windowsill. His shoulders were shaking.

"Tsukki," he said, not quite steadily. "They're asking nicely."

Tsukishima drank some of his milk tea. He looked out of the window at the field for a while, at the baseball team's net sagging in the heat.

"Library," he said at last. "After practice. Every day till exams." He looked down at the 2 bowed heads. "And if you 2 start fighting in the library, I'm leaving, and you can fail on your own."

"Yes!" said Hinata.

"...Yes," said Kageyama.

They came up out of the bow together, and both noticed, and both looked away from each other, and walked off in opposite directions, Hinata toward the stairs and Kageyama back to the vending machines, where he stood for a long time staring at the buttons as if he'd forgotten what milk was.

Sho went on down the corridor to buy his bread. As he passed the window Tsukishima's eyes moved to him, briefly, over the top of the milk tea, and moved away again.

---

On Wednesday after practice the library was full.

It was on the 3rd floor at the end of the corridor, and in exam season it was the most crowded room in the school. Every table was taken. The windows were open because the radiators were off and the heat had come in early, and outside in the cherry trees the 1st cicadas were starting, a dry rattling sound that came and went.

Suga had the last table by the window. He'd put his bag on the chair opposite to keep it, and moved it when Sho came in.

At a table on the far side of the room, under the clock, Tsukishima sat with a red pen in his hand and 2 practice tests in front of him, 1 from Hinata and 1 from Kageyama, which Hinata and Kageyama had pushed across to him from opposite ends of the table. Tsukishima was marking them. The red pen went down each page, and each time it moved Hinata flinched, and Kageyama didn't, and Yamaguchi, sitting next to Tsukishima, watched the pen and winced on their behalf.

"Center Exam," said Suga, tapping the thick blue book in front of him. "English. This one's going to kill me." He tapped the other book, older and thinner, with a paper cover gone soft. "And this one's yours."

*Classical Japanese Grammar: Complete Review.*

"I got 72 in Classical last year," said Suga. "So. English for Classical."

Sho sat down.

They did English 1st, because Suga had a practice test the next morning and said he was about 3 grammar points away from crying. Sho looked at the paper. Long reading passages, the kind he'd read a thousand of in the other life: scouting reports from Italy and Poland and Brazil run through bad translation software, which he'd then had to rewrite by hand into 3 lines a coach could read before a match. The grammar in Suga's book was ordinary. The trick, he'd learned in a room with the lights off, had never been the grammar.

"Don't read all of it straight off," said Sho. "Find the sentence that's doing the work."

"How do you find it?"

"It's usually the 1st or the last. Or the one with *however* in it. The rest is getting there." He put his finger on a paragraph about recycling in Sweden. "This one. *However, the program has struggled to...* That's the paragraph."

Suga read the sentence twice. He looked at the question under it, circled an answer, and turned to the answer key at the back.

"Huh," he said.

"Do the next one."

Suga did the next one. He read the paragraph through once, quickly, with his pencil moving down the margin, and stopped at a sentence near the end and read that one again, slowly, and circled his answer. Right. The one after that, he read and stopped and frowned, and read the 1st sentence again, and the last, and chose, and got it wrong, and swore at the book under his breath in the polite way he swore at things, and the 1st year at the next table looked round.

"*Although*," said Sho, pointing. "Not *however*. Same job."

Suga wrote it in the margin. *Although = however.* He did 6 more. He got 5.

"I watch a lot of movies," said Sho, before Suga could ask.

"Hm," said Suga, and slid the paper-covered book across the table.

Classical was worse.

Suga explained it with his hands. The verb endings that changed if the sentence was a question. The particle that meant 2 different things depending on what came after it. The way the subject vanished from sentences for whole paragraphs, and you were meant to know, from the honorifics on the verbs, whether it was the emperor or a lady-in-waiting doing the crying.

"This ending," said Suga, tapping it with his pencil, "means somebody important. So it's the emperor. It tells you who. You just have to hear it."

Sho looked at the sentence. *Ima wa mukashi...* He read it again under his breath, the endings, the shape of the sound of them.

"...The emperor's crying."

"The emperor's crying." Suga sat back. "Yes."

They went on. The light outside went from white to gold. Across the room Tsukishima finished marking and pushed the 2 tests back across the table, 1 to each end, and Hinata looked at his and put his forehead down on the table, and Kageyama looked at his and went very still, and Yamaguchi said "Tsukki, that's a lot of red," and Tsukishima said "That's their red. I just found it."

At 6:30, when the librarian started turning off the lights at the far end of the room, Suga took a sheet of paper out of the Classical book. It was a practice test, 1 page, photocopied, the kind the teachers handed out in exam week.

"Do this one," said Suga. "15 minutes. I'll mark it."

Sho did it. He did it the way he'd been doing the sentences all evening, out loud under his breath, listening to the endings. Some of them he heard. Some of them, halfway through a sentence, the subject slid away from him, and he couldn't tell whether it was the emperor or the lady or a monk who'd come in 2 lines ago, and he guessed.

At 15 minutes Suga held out his hand, and Sho gave him the paper.

Suga took a red pen out of his pencil case. He marked it the way Tsukishima had marked Hinata's, steadily, page down, the pen moving and stopping and moving. A tick. A tick. A line through a whole answer. Another line. A tick. A small circle round a verb ending, with a red arrow and a word next to it.

At the bottom of the page he wrote a number and put the pen down and turned the paper round.

*38.*

In red, circled.

Sho looked at it.

"It's 3 lower than last time," said Suga. "It's not a bad paper. This one's harder than the last test. But." He tapped the number, gently, with the back of the pen. "The line's 30. That's 8 away."

8 away. 8 points, out of 100, between him and a make-up test on a Saturday morning in Miyagi, while a bus went south to Tokyo without him.

He thought about the Nekoma page at the back of his notebook. *What does he see now?* He thought about a gym he'd never been in, and Kenma in it with no expression at all, waiting, holding the ball 1 beat too long for somebody who wasn't going to be there to look.

Across the room, Hinata had lifted his forehead off the table and was looking at his own red marks, and then, very slowly, picking up his pencil.

"Same time tomorrow," said Sho.

Suga raised an eyebrow. "It's Wednesdays."

"Tomorrow too," said Sho. "Please. And Friday. I'll do your English every day."

Suga looked at him, and at the red 38, and at him again.

"You really want Tokyo," said Suga.

"Yes," said Sho.

Suga capped the red pen.

"Tomorrow," he said. "Same table. Bring the emperor."

## Chapter 31: Villager B

On Thursday at lunch Kiyoko came down the 1st-year corridor, looking into the classrooms.

Sho was coming back up from the school store with a melon bread and a carton of milk, and he saw her as he came off the stairs. She came from the far end, from 1-1, slowly, with her bag on her shoulder and the light from the windows catching her glasses, and at each classroom door she paused and looked in, and moved on.

The corridor went quiet around her as she came. 3rd years didn't come to the 1st-year floor. Shimizu Kiyoko of the volleyball club was known by sight to every 1st year in the school, and none of them had ever expected her to look at them.

1-2. 1-3. At 1-4 Tsukishima, at the window with his milk tea, raised his eyebrows a fraction and lowered them again. Yamaguchi stood up straighter.

At 1-5 Kiyoko stopped. She looked past the door, down the corridor toward Sho, at 3 girls coming back from the vending machines with juice boxes. The 2 on the outside were talking. The 1 in the middle was listening, and laughing a beat after the others: a small girl with light hair tied up on 1 side, holding her juice box in both hands.

Yachi Hitoka. She sat in the front row of 1-5, by the door, and wrote everything down in small, neat handwriting, and had the top mark in the class on the last math test, and jumped when people said her name. In May she'd dropped her pencil case in this corridor and Sho had picked up 3 of the pens for her, and she'd said thank you so many times he'd started to wonder what he'd done.

Kiyoko stepped into the girls' path.

The 2 on the outside stopped. Yachi walked another half step before she noticed, and then stopped so suddenly the juice box squelched in her hands.

"Excuse me," said Kiyoko. "Are you a 1st year?"

"Y-yes?"

"Are you in a club?"

"N-no. Not yet. I mean, no. I'm not."

"Would you like to be a manager? For the boys' volleyball club."

Yachi looked up at her with her mouth a little open and the juice box forgotten. Her 2 friends had each taken a small step backward.

"Yes," said Yachi.

A beat later her face caught up with what she'd said.

"I mean... That is... What does a manager...?"

"Come and see," said Kiyoko. "After school. The Second Gymnasium." She inclined her head, very slightly. "Thank you."

She walked back up the corridor the way she'd come. When she'd turned the corner by the stairs the noise came back all at once, and Yachi's friends grabbed her by both arms.

"Hitoka! That was *Shimizu-senpai*!"

"I said yes." Yachi's voice had gone thin and high. "Why did I say yes? I don't know anything about volleyball. I don't know what a manager *does*. What if they make me carry things? What if I drop them? What if I drop something important and it breaks and they lose and they make me pay for it and I have to sell my..."

Her friends steered her into the classroom. On the way past, her eyes landed on Sho, standing aside in the doorway with his bread and his milk, and stopped.

"Kiyose-kun. You're in the volleyball club."

"Yes."

"Are they..." She held the juice box to her chest. "Are they scary?"

Sho thought about it.

"They're loud," he said. "Tanaka-senpai makes faces. He means them nicely. Hinata shouts about everything. Noya-senpai is short and shouts louder than Hinata." He thought some more. "Kiyoko-san's the quietest one there. When she says something, everybody stops."

Yachi looked at him for a while.

Then she bowed, quickly, a small bob from the waist, and let her friends pull her to her desk.

---

She came to the gym after school with Kiyoko, holding the strap of her school bag in both hands, and sat on the edge of the stage where Kiyoko pointed.

Sho saw her come in from the end line. He was where Ukai had put him on Monday, behind the receive line, calling the passes and taking every 3rd 1 himself.

"Noya-san. Good. Tanaka-senpai. Good."

On the stage Yachi jumped every time Tanaka yelled. Her eyes went from the receive drill to the far court, and stayed there.

On the far court, Hinata and Kageyama had a cart of balls between them.

They still weren't speaking. Ukai had put them on the far net together anyway, and Kageyama tossed and Hinata ran, and neither of them said a word. The 1st 2 went the old way. Hinata in the air before the ball left Kageyama's hands, his eyes shut, the ball arriving at his palm as if it had always been going there, the crack of it on the floor. On the stage Yachi leaned forward with her mouth open.

On the 3rd, Hinata kept his eyes open.

Sho saw it from the far end of the gym, because he'd been waiting for it. Hinata's eyes stayed wide all the way up, on the ball, and he was a fraction late, the quick already past the place where his hand arrived. He swung at the air. The ball went off the end of his fingers and over the antenna. He landed and stood looking at his own palm.

Kageyama took the next ball out of the cart without a word, and tossed it, the old way, fast. Hinata looked at it go past him. Then he ran for the next one, eyes shut, and it landed.

Yachi flinched at the crack and leaned forward again. To her it had been 2 of the most beautiful things she'd ever seen and a 3rd that went over the antenna, and she didn't know the difference yet.

Sho's turn came round. He went to the front of the line.

Ukai, on his chair on the far side of the net, picked a ball out of the cart and held it.

Before the toss Sho looked once at the other side of the net, all of it at once. Ukai's chair. The cart. Suga at the net with the basket by his feet. Ennoshita at the side, picking up a stray ball. The empty court where the blockers would be. He took it in 1 look and let it go, and then he was looking only at Ukai: the ball in his left hand, the right arm drawn back.

The toss went up, a little left of the last one. Ukai's chest stayed square. The arm came through straight.

*Straight down the middle. At me.*

Sho didn't move his feet. He let his weight sink and kept his arms together and still, and the ball came over the net flat and dipping, and it came to him, exactly where he was standing, and went up off his arms in a soft high curve into Suga's hands.

"Nice," said Suga.

"Daichi-san," said Sho, going back to the end line, because Daichi was already passing the next one. "Good."

At the water break Ennoshita came over with the basket of loose balls.

"Kiyose."

"Yes."

"You go still," said Ennoshita. "Right before. Every time."

Sho held the ball he'd been about to put in the basket.

"Someone said that once," he said.

"Thought so." Ennoshita took the ball out of his hand and put it in the basket. "I watch a lot, from the line."

He carried the basket off toward the cart.

Sho stood at the end of the court.

Oikawa had said it at the sink, after Seijoh. *You'd go still and look at them, and you'd already be moving.* But that had been the day the eye fired, the day of the strange one, with Oikawa's serve coming at him and his whole head full of light.

Today the eye hadn't fired. Today it had been an ordinary drill, an ordinary float, and he'd gone still anyway, every time, enough for Ennoshita to see it from the side of the court.

It was him now. His body had learned to stop and wait for the read, and it did it whether the eye came or not, and anybody who watched him for long enough could see it.

He went to the stage, to his bag at the far end from Yachi, and got the notebook out, and turned to the back, under the Nekoma box, under *What does he see now?*

*I go still before every read. With the eye or without. Every time. Ennoshita saw it from the line.*

And under that:

*So Kenma will.*

---

He did Classical with Suga every day after that, in the library, at the window table, the way he'd asked.

On Thursday it was 44. On Friday it was 47. Suga marked them in red and pushed them back across the table, and they did English for Suga after, every day, the trade going both ways, and Suga's practice scores went 8 out of 10, then 9.

On Friday, at lunch, Hinata came running down the 1st-year corridor shouting "YACHI-SAN!" at the top of his voice.

Yachi went out into the corridor white as paper, and the 2 of them stood by the window outside 1-5 for most of lunch: Hinata talking with both hands, Yachi listening with both hands clutched to her chest. Sho was at his desk at the back by the window, with his Classical book open and his bread, and Hinata came through the open door the way he came through most walls. Mostly Sho heard the shape of it, rising and falling, very fast.

Once a single word came through clearly, because Hinata shouted it.

"*VILLAGER!*"

Sho closed his Classical book, picked up his bread, and went to the library.

---

On Monday morning there was a poster on the noticeboard in the main corridor.

It was the size of 2 sheets of drawing paper side by side. A black crow with its wings wide, cut out of shadow against white, and behind it the outline of Karasuno's gym in thin, clean lines, and along the bottom in neat block letters *KARASUNO HIGH SCHOOL VOLLEYBALL CLUB*, and under that, smaller: *Please support our journey.* There was a donation box by the office and a list of the dates of the 2 trips.

People stopped to look at it all morning. Teachers stopped. Sho stopped on his way to homeroom and stood in front of it.

Every feather in the crow's wings had its own edge. Somebody had sat up late with a sharp knife.

In 1-5, Yachi was at her desk in the front row with her head down on her arms, and there was a small bandage on her left thumb. Her 2 friends were standing over her saying *Hitoka, everybody's looking at it, everybody*, and Yachi was saying into her arms, "Don't. Please. Thank you. Don't."

At practice that afternoon she came in with Kiyoko in a tracksuit a size too big for her, holding a clipboard to her chest. Before the warm-up she went to Takeda, who was by the door with his own notebook, and held out a sheet of paper with both hands, bowing over it.

It was the club registration form. At the bottom, in the box for a parent's seal, there was a red seal, pressed carefully, a little crooked.

Takeda took it in both hands and bowed back.

Then Daichi called everyone in and said, "This is Yachi Hitoka-san. She's going to be a manager," and Yachi bowed so low and so fast her clipboard hit her knees.

"I'm Yachi! I'll do my best! Please take care of me! I don't know anything! Sorry!"

Tanaka and Noya, side by side in the front row, looked at each other. Then at Yachi. Then at Kiyoko, standing beside her with her hands folded.

Their eyes filled up together.

"A 2nd... manager..." said Noya.

"Kiyoko-san brought us... a 2nd manager..." said Tanaka.

"Don't cry at her," said Daichi.

---

The exams started on Tuesday: 3 days of them, the 1st-semester end-of-term exams, 2 or 3 subjects every morning and then home at noon. The desks were pulled apart into rows. The blinds were half down against the July sun. The gym was locked all week.

On Tuesday Sho woke at 4:14 anyway. He lay in the dark and listened to the house, the water heater ticking, a car going past at the bottom of the street, and then got up and did 30 minutes of verb endings at his desk under the lamp. When he went downstairs his mother was already at the counter in her cardigan making onigiri, and there was a cup of tea poured and going cold at his place.

Math and English were on Tuesday. He finished both early and went through them twice and put his pencil down and looked out of the window at the hills behind the gym, which had turned the dark heavy green of real summer.

Classical was Thursday, 2nd period.

On Wednesday the exams finished at noon, and at 1 o'clock Suga was at the window table in the library with the old paper-covered book. His own exams were the same days as Sho's. His English was Thursday, the same as Sho's Classical. He'd come in anyway, on his own revision afternoon.

"Last one," said Suga, and slid a practice paper across. "Then you go home and sleep, and I go home and cry over English."

Sho did it. 15 minutes. Suga marked it in red.

*51.*

"Better," said Suga. "Still guessing when the subject slides." He turned the paper round and tapped a sentence. "This one. Who's speaking?"

Sho looked. "The monk."

"The lady. Look at the verb."

He looked at it. *Mōsu*. A humble verb, for speaking to someone above you.

"She's speaking to him," said Sho slowly. "She's lower than him. So she uses the humble one."

"So when the subject goes, look at the verb, and ask who's lower," said Suga. "Most of the time, the one using the humble word is the one doing it. Listen for who's bowing."

Sho looked at the sentence a while longer.

"Who's bowing," he said.

"Who's bowing." Suga capped the red pen and pushed his own blue book across the table. "Now. 10 minutes. Make me pass English."

---

The Classical exam was in 1-5's own room, with Ishikawa-sensei at the front with the papers face down in a stack and the blinds down on the left side, so that the light came in long gold bars across the floor and the backs of the desks.

Sho sat at the back by the window. In the front row by the door, Yachi had her pencil case open and 3 pencils lined up in a row, all sharpened to the same length, her bandaged thumb resting on the edge of the desk.

"Begin."

The papers turned over all round the room with a sound like birds getting up off a field.

The grammar section, he did the way Suga had taught him, the way he'd learned to hear a pass. He read each one under his breath with his lips barely moving, and listened to the shape of it, and the ending told him what it was. Mostly. 15 of them; 11 he was sure of; he put a small dot beside the other 4.

The passage was long. A courtier from the capital travelling to a temple in the mountains in winter, snow on the pines, a conversation with an old priest. In the 3rd sentence the subject slid away from him. Somebody was speaking to somebody, and the sentence didn't say who.

He sat with his pencil over the paper.

*Who's bowing.*

He read the verb again under his breath. *Mōshikeru.* The humble one. Somebody lower, speaking to somebody higher. In the world of the passage the courtier was a man from the capital, and the priest was an old man in a hut on a mountain.

So the priest was answering the courtier.

He wrote it down.

It went on like that for 40 minutes. Sometimes he heard it, right there, the way he heard Daichi's passes, solid and clean and the same every time. Sometimes the subject went and he listened for who was bowing, and sometimes that told him and sometimes it didn't, and then he guessed, and put a dot, and went on.

At 40 minutes he went back to his dots. He changed 2 of them.

"Pencils down."

The papers went forward along the rows, hand to hand. In the front row Yachi had already put her 3 pencils back in their case, side by side. She turned round in her chair and looked along the window row until she found him, and mouthed something.

He couldn't read it from there. She turned back round and put her hands in her lap.

---

The results came back on Friday, the last day of term, the day before the bus.

Math, 94. English, 98. Ishikawa-sensei came last, after lunch, and read out the names, and you went up and took yours and walked back to your desk with it folded so nobody could see the number.

He sat down at his desk by the window and unfolded it.

*58.* In red, in the top right corner, circled. And under it, in Ishikawa-sensei's small handwriting: *Subjects in the passage much improved.*

Who's bowing.

He folded it and put it in the inside pocket of his bag next to the permission form in its clear plastic folder, where Dad had zipped it shut nearly 2 weeks ago, and zipped the pocket shut again.

At the end of the day the door of 1-5 banged open and Tanaka came in, which 2nd years did not do, waving a test paper over his head like a flag.

"KIYOSE! 31!" He slapped it down on Sho's desk. *31*, in red, circled. "CLASSICAL! 31! I PASSED!"

Half of 1-5 had turned round to look at him. In the front row Yachi had gone rigid in her chair.

"Congratulations, senpai," said Sho.

"Noya got 34 in English! We're BOTH going! Saeko-nee made us do it till 2 in the morning!" Tanaka picked his paper back up and held it at arm's length, beaming at it. "31. Beautiful. What'd you get?"

"58."

Tanaka lowered the paper.

"...That's private," he said, and turned round and walked out again.

Sho picked up his bag and followed him out into the corridor. Tanaka was already gone, down the stairs, still yelling. The corridor was full of 1st years with their bags, the end-of-term noise, and Sho was at the stairs end of it when the sound started, from the doorway of 1-1, right beside him.

It was Hinata. It was a long, rising, falling wail, like a siren going past.

Hinata was standing in the doorway of 1-1 holding a test paper by 1 corner, the way you'd hold something dead. There was a number in the top corner of it, small and red and circled twice.

Further down the corridor, Kageyama came out of 1-3 with a paper in his hand, folded, held down by his side. He was walking slowly. He saw Hinata in the doorway of 1-1. Hinata saw him.

"...English," said Hinata.

"English," said Kageyama.

They stood there 10 meters apart, holding their papers, while everybody else went past them toward the stairs.

Sho stood at the top of the stairs with his bag on his shoulder and did the arithmetic, because he couldn't not.

Tomorrow at 6 in the morning the bus left for Tokyo. Tomorrow at 9, in this building, Hinata and Kageyama would be sitting a make-up test.

Tomorrow morning, against Fukurodani, or Nekoma, or whoever came 1st, Suga would set. There'd be no quick. There'd be no Hinata pulling 2 blockers across the net every time he ran. There'd be 2 empty places on the court in the front row, and Ukai would have to fill them with somebody.

**Continuity (ch 31)**

- Sho POV, Thursday of the week before exams to Friday of exam week. Title "Villager B" (canon translation; "Townsperson B" also used). The eye did not fire.
- **Canon kept (Kiyoko's ask, Yachi seeing the quick, Hinata's push, the poster, the parent's seal; staging and wording invented):** Kiyoko searched the 1st-year corridor at lunch; stopped Yachi coming back from the vending machines with 2 friends: "Are you a 1st year?... Are you in a club?... Would you like to be a manager? For the boys' volleyball club." "Yes." / "What does a manager...?" / "Come and see. After school. The Second Gymnasium." Yachi's worst-case spiral ("sell my..."). Thursday after school Yachi on the stage with Kiyoko. Friday lunch Hinata shouted "YACHI-SAN!" and talked to her by the window outside 1-5 for most of lunch; Sho heard 1 word, "*VILLAGER!*", and went to the library. Monday: Yachi's poster (black crow cut from shadow, Karasuno's gym in thin lines, *KARASUNO HIGH SCHOOL VOLLEYBALL CLUB / Please support our journey*, donation box by the office; every feather with its own edge); bandage on her left thumb. **Yachi gave Takeda her club registration form with a parent's red seal** (her mother's; not named). Daichi introduced her. Tanaka and Noya cried ("A 2nd... manager..."). "Don't cry at her."
- **Sho and Yachi:** classmates (she: front row by the door; he: back by the window); May, 3 pens. "Are they scary?" Sho: "They're loud. Tanaka-senpai makes faces. He means them nicely. Hinata shouts about everything. Noya-senpai is short and shouts louder than Hinata... Kiyoko-san's the quietest one there. When she says something, everybody stops." She bowed. After the Classical exam she mouthed something he couldn't read.
- **Hinata and Kageyama (still not speaking):** on the far court with a cart; 2 old quicks landed (eyes shut); on the 3rd Hinata kept his eyes open, was late, swung at air, ball over the antenna, looked at his palm; Kageyama tossed the next 1 the old way without a word. Yachi saw 2 beautiful quicks and didn't know the 3rd was a miss.
- **Sho's play (the 1-look snapshot debuts):** before Ukai's toss, 1 look at the whole far side (Ukai's chair, the cart, Suga, Ennoshita, the empty court), let go, then only Ukai: chest square, arm straight: *Straight down the middle. At me.* He didn't move his feet; the pass went soft and high to Suga ("Nice").
- **Ennoshita:** "You go still. Right before. Every time." Sho: "Someone said that once." "Thought so. I watch a lot, from the line." Sho's realization: Oikawa saw it on the day the eye fired; today it didn't fire, and he went still anyway: it's a habit of his body now, readable by anyone who watches long enough. Notebook (back page, under the Nekoma box): *I go still before every read. With the eye or without. Every time. Ennoshita saw it from the line. / So Kenma will.*
- **Daily Classical with Suga:** Thursday 44, Friday 47 (red pen), English for Suga after each (8/10, 9/10).
- **Exams:** 3 days, Tuesday to Thursday, mornings, home at noon; gym locked. Sho woke at 4:14 anyway; Mom's onigiri. Math and English Tuesday. Wednesday 1 p.m.: Suga, on his own revision afternoon (his English was Thursday), gave the last practice paper: **51**. Rule: "when the subject goes, look at the verb, and ask who's lower... Listen for who's bowing." (*mōsu*, humble verb of speaking).
- **The Classical exam (Thursday, 2nd period, 1-5; Ishikawa-sensei, invented):** grammar 11 of 15 sure; passage: a courtier from the capital at a mountain temple in winter, an old priest; the humble verb *mōshikeru* told him the priest was answering the courtier. Changed 2 answers.
- **Results (Friday, last day of term):** Math 94, English 98, **Classical 58** (*Subjects in the passage much improved.*). Put in the zipped inside pocket with the permission form (zipped by Dad nearly 2 weeks ago). **Tanaka: Classical 31, passed** ("Saeko-nee made us do it till 2 in the morning!" Saeko named for the 1st time). **Noya: English 34, passed.** Tanaka, on Sho's 58: "...That's private."
- **Canon kept:** Hinata and Kageyama failed (here: English; red, circled twice). In the corridor: "...English." "English." Bus to Tokyo at 6 a.m. Saturday; make-up test at 9 a.m.
- Hook (Sho's read): tomorrow Suga sets, no quick, 2 empty front-row places that Ukai has to fill.
- Next: ch 32 "Flying falls" (trip 1, Fukurodani Academy, Tokyo; the morning without Hinata and Kageyama; Sho plays real minutes and gets read by Akaashi; the eye fires on Bokuto, a little soreness gone by the next game; penalty dives; Saeko drives Hinata and Kageyama, theirs).
- No status card.

## Chapter 32: Flying falls

The bus was at the school gate at 5:40, white, with *Karasuno High School* on a card in the windscreen in Takeda's handwriting. The engine was running. The driver was a man of about 60 in a cap, reading a sports newspaper with his feet up on the dashboard.

It was barely light. The sky over the hills was the color of milk, and the street lamps along the school wall were still on, and a crow sat on top of the gate post watching them load the bags into the hold underneath. Sho had his bag on 1 shoulder and the bundle Mom had tied for him in the other hand: onigiri, 4 of them, and a frozen bottle of barley tea wrapped in a towel to keep them cold. In the inside pocket of his bag, zipped shut, the permission form in its clear plastic folder, and next to it, folded, a Classical test with *58* on it in red.

Everybody was there except 2.

Tanaka stood by the bus door with his arms folded, looking up the empty street toward the station, as if a car might come round the corner any minute. Noya stood beside him with his hands in his pockets, looking the same way.

"They'll be fine," said Tanaka, to the street. "They'll do the test, and they'll pass it, and Saeko-nee will bring them."

"Saeko-nee drives like a demon," said Noya.

"She drives like a demon," Tanaka agreed. "They'll be fine."

Daichi counted heads twice, the way he always did. Takeda counted them a 3rd time. Ukai came out of the convenience store across the road with a coffee and a bag of bread rolls and got on without counting anybody. Kiyoko sat at the front with the first-aid box on her knees. Yachi sat beside her with a clipboard she'd been given on Monday and hadn't put down since, reading the schedule for the 3rd time with her lips moving.

Sho got the window seat halfway down on the left. Yamaguchi sat next to him, and then got up again because Tsukishima was across the aisle and he wanted to tell him something, and then sat down again. The bus pulled away from the gate at 6:02. Through the back window Sho watched the school go, the gym roof, the cherry trees, the crow on the gate post, and then the road turned and it was gone.

2 seats near the front were empty. Nobody sat in them. Nobody put their bags on them.

---

It was nearly 5 hours to Tokyo.

Most of the bus slept for the 1st 2. Tanaka slept with his mouth open and his head on Noya's shoulder, and Noya slept with his head on the window. Asahi slept sitting perfectly upright with his arms folded, like a monk. Tsukishima had his headphones on and his eyes shut, and Sho couldn't tell if he was asleep. Yamaguchi slept against Sho's arm, which Sho let him do.

Sho watched the window.

The motorway went south through Fukushima and the hills folded away on both sides into paddies and forests and small towns with big shrines. Somewhere around Koriyama he realized he knew this road. Satoshi had done it at 20, on a university team bus going the other way, north, to a tournament in Sendai, the spring before the knee went. He'd sat by the window then, too. He'd watched the same hills go by, and thought about the match, and about his left knee, which had been sore for weeks by then, a little, nothing, nothing he was going to tell anybody about.

He looked at his own knee now, his left one, Sho's, under his track pants. It was fine. It had always been fine.

He took out the notebook and opened it on his knee, and turned to the 2 pages on Nekoma, and then to a blank one, and wrote at the top: *Fukurodani Academy Group. Day 1.* Then he sat with the pen and didn't write anything else, because he didn't know anything else yet. He'd know by tonight.

Past Utsunomiya the land flattened out. The buildings came closer together and got taller and grayer. Around 10 the bus woke up, all at once, the way buses did. Tanaka sat up and wiped his mouth and looked out of the window and said, "Is that Tokyo?"

"That's Saitama," said Ennoshita.

"It looks like Tokyo."

"It's Saitama."

They crossed a river that was wider than any river in Miyagi, brown and slow, with baseball pitches along its banks. Then it was Tokyo, and it went on and on, buildings and train lines and expressways stacked on top of each other, and everybody on the bus who'd been asleep was now pressed against a window.

---

Fukurodani Academy had a gymnasium bigger than Karasuno's First and Second Gymnasiums put together.

It was new, or newer, with a curved roof of white steel and glass and high windows all along 1 side, so that the light came down in bright bars across 2 full courts side by side. On 1 court a team in white and black was warming up. On the other, a team in red that Sho knew from May. The noise came off both courts at once and went up into the roof and came back down doubled: shoes squealing, balls slapping, someone laughing, a whistle, a voice shouting "HEY HEY HEY!" at a volume that made 2 of the Karasuno 1st years stop in the doorway.

Sho stopped in the doorway too.

He stood there with his bag on his shoulder and let it all come at him. Ears 1st, the way he'd been training. The pass on the near court, a solid pass off both arms. A set, then a spike, the crack of it doubled by the roof. Somebody's shoes. A pass on the far court, and another, and the roof took the sound of them and threw it back and mixed them together, near court and far court, the echo of 1 pass landing on top of the next one, until he couldn't tell which was which. He shut his eyes, the way he did on the step, and listened.

Everything. All at once. 2 courts and a roof full of echo, and no way to pick 1 pass out of it.

He opened his eyes. Behind him Ennoshita said, "You're in the door," and he moved out of it.

"Karasuno?" A tall man in a polo shirt with a whistle round his neck had come across from the near court. "Welcome. Fukurodani. You're on court 2 at 12, against Shinzen. Changing rooms are that way." He pointed. "Your coach?"

Ukai raised a hand.

"Nekomata-sensei says hello," said the man, and nodded at the red team. "He says you've met."

They had. Sho found Kenma without trying, at the back of Nekoma's line, hair half yellow and half dark, and looked away before Kenma could look back.

They changed. They came back out and warmed up on the side of court 2 while Nekoma finished on it. Sho stretched by the wall and watched the near court, where the team in white and black was running a hitting line.

Their setter was a 2nd year, or looked like it. Dark hair. Calm, almost sleepy face. He set every ball the same way, quiet hands, a small pause at the top, and the ball went where it went, and his face didn't change whether it was a good set or a bad one.

He had *5* on his back, black on white, the plain block numeral, a little faded from washing. Half the teams in Japan had a 5 on somebody. Satoshi had worn 5, at university, for 2 years, navy on white, with his name stitched on the sleeve of the team jacket.

His hand had gone to his left knee. He found it there, resting on the kneecap, and took it away.

"Kiyose," said Tsukishima, from further along the wall. "You're staring at their setter."

"He's good."

"Should I get you his autograph?" said Tsukishima, and went back to stretching his hamstrings.

On the near court the setter with 5 on his back set a ball high and outside to a hitter with gray and black hair sticking up in 2 points like horns, who went up into it with his whole body and hit it so hard it bounced off the floor and up into the windows and came back down onto the next court.

"HEY HEY HEY!" shouted the hitter, landing. "DID YOU SEE THAT, AKAASHI?"

"I set it, Bokuto-san," said the setter.

---

Sho started on the bench, with the spare towels folded on his knees.

Suga set. Narita took Hinata's place in the middle. Tsukishima was the other middle. Daichi, Asahi, Tanaka on the wings, Noya behind them. It was a normal lineup with a normal setter and no quick, and it played like one: solid, careful, a little slow. Suga's sets went high and outside and gave the hitters time, and Shinzen's blockers had time too, all the time they needed, because there was no little orange-haired boy running at them from the middle and pulling 2 of them across the net every time. They lined up in front of Asahi and Tanaka and waited.

Sho watched it from the bench, the way he'd watched for 9 years, seeing everything and touching none of it. By 12-17 he'd folded the top towel into a square the size of his palm.

Sho went in at 14-18 for Asahi, in the back row.

"Um. Borrow it longer this time," said Asahi, as they passed in the substitution zone, and squeezed Sho's shoulder on the way past, and went to the bench.

The noise was worse on the court. Ears were no good here. The roof took every pass and threw it back mixed with the pass from the other court, and the shouting from both benches, and the squeal of 24 pairs of shoes. He gave up on listening after 2 rallies, and just looked. 1 look at the far side before every serve: their setter, their front row, their libero, the gap. Then the server.

He passed 3 clean. He dug 1 off the block. He got set once, a high back-row ball on the right, from behind the 3-meter line, and hit it cross, and Shinzen's block was waiting for it, and it came back down at his feet.

Shinzen won 25-19.

"Penalty!" called the Fukurodani coach over his shoulder, from the near court.

Karasuno lined up on the end line.

"How many?" said Yamaguchi, who'd come off the bench to line up with them.

"1 length," said Daichi. "Every time we lose."

He went 1st. He threw himself forward onto the polished floor of Fukurodani Academy's gymnasium with his arms out, and his hands caught him, and he slid on his chest the length of his own body, and got up, and went again. Suga went next. Then Asahi, enormous and graceful, sliding like a seal. Then Tanaka, who yelled "FLYING FALL!" every single time he left the ground. Then Noya, who skimmed, as if he'd been born on a polished floor.

Sho went.

He ran 3 steps and threw himself forward, and the floor came up at him, polished and cool, and his hands caught it and his chest hit it and he slid, and the gym went past at the level of the floor, people's shoes, the bottom of the net, the lines. He got up. He went again.

On the 3rd one, halfway down the court, flat on his chest on the floor of a gym in Tokyo, he started laughing.

It came up out of him on its own. He was 15, and he was lying on his face on the floor of somebody else's gym, in Tokyo, in a team shirt, because he'd lost a game of volleyball that he'd played in. Satoshi had sat in a room with the lights off for 9 years and watched other people lose, and never once got to lie on the floor afterward with his face against the boards because he'd been out there.

He got up, still laughing, and threw himself forward again.

At the far end Tsukishima was standing up from his last one, brushing off his chest with both hands, and looking at Sho with his eyebrows raised over his glasses.

"You're enjoying this," said Tsukishima.

"Yes," said Sho.

Tsukishima looked at him a while longer. Then he went to get water.

---

The 2nd game was Fukurodani, at 12:50, on the near court.

Ukai started Sho.

He said it in the huddle without making anything of it, reading the lineup off his clipboard, and only paused when he got to the end. "Asahi, you're out this one. Kiyose, you're in. Same spot."

Asahi nodded. "Yes, Coach." He looked at Sho, and rubbed the back of his neck. "Um. Their 4. Bokuto. I watched him in warm-up. He hits cross more than line." He thought about it. "When he's happy."

"When he's happy," said Sho.

"You'll see," said Asahi.

Fukurodani came out onto the court in their white and black, Bokuto at the front of them, bouncing on his toes. "HEY! Karasuno! Kuroo says you've got a little one! A jumpy one! Where is he?"

"They're not here yet, Bokuto-san," said Akaashi behind him, in the same voice he'd used to say *I set it.*

Sho went to his spot at the back right. Before the 1st serve he looked once across the net and took in all of it: Akaashi at the right of the front row, hands loose at his sides; Bokuto on the left, up on his toes, grinning at nothing; their middle, tall and quiet; their libero, small and quick, bouncing on his heels. 1 look, and he let it go, and watched the server.

Fukurodani served. Daichi passed. Suga set Tanaka on the left, and Tanaka hit it hard down the line, and their libero dug it, and Akaashi got under it. Sho watched Akaashi's hands. They came up to the ball the same way they'd come up to every ball in warm-up, with no tell in the wrists and no lean in the hips, and the ball went high and outside to the left, to Bokuto.

Bokuto came in big and loud, every step of his approach slapping the floor, and went up high with his arm all the way back, and the gym went silent.

Sho knew the silence. It came the way it always came, without asking: the noise of both courts and the roof and the benches went far away all at once, as if someone had shut a door, and Bokuto became points of light hanging in the air above the net, shoulder and elbow and wrist and hips. The brightest was his right hand, burning white. A ghost peeled off it 1 blink early. Its wrist turned out toward the sideline, and the arm came through long.

Line. And from that height, with that much arm, deep. The back right corner, where Asahi had said he wouldn't hit when he was happy, and where Sho was standing.

He was already moving, 2 steps back and to the right, and down. He put his arms together where the ghost had pointed, and kept them there, and the sound came back all at once, the crack of Bokuto's hand on the ball and the roar off the Fukurodani bench, and the ball was already on him.

It was the hardest ball he'd ever had come at him. Harder than Iwaizumi. It hit his forearms like a kicked door and went straight up, high and wild, toward the side of the court, and Noya ran it down and scooped it over the net with 1 arm.

"NICE, KIYOSE!" Noya yelled, from the floor. "LEAVE THE REST TO ME!"

Fukurodani put it away on the next touch anyway. 1-0 to them.

On the far side of the net Bokuto had landed and was standing quite still, pointing at Sho.

"HEY!" said Bokuto. "13! You got it! You GOT it! That was my best one! Akaashi, did you see? He got my best one!"

"I saw, Bokuto-san," said Akaashi.

Sho went back to his spot. His eyes stung a little, the way your eyes sting after you've stared at something bright, a dull warmth at the back of them. He blinked twice and looked across the net while the Fukurodani server bounced the ball.

The server had the ball in his hands, and Akaashi was looking past him, across the net, at Sho. It was a short, plain look, the kind you'd give a word on a page you hadn't expected to find there. Then Akaashi turned back to the net and put his hand behind his back for the serve.

---

Sho saw the 1st change on the 4th rally.

Bokuto went up on the left, the same big approach. Sho was in the back right, ready for the line, and the ball went cross instead, hard, at Noya, who dug it and yelled about it.

He saw the 2nd change on the 6th. Fukurodani had the ball on a free ball, an easy pass, and every Karasuno blocker leaned toward Bokuto, and Akaashi set the middle instead, quick, into the floor in front of Daichi.

On the 9th Sho cheated. Bokuto was on the left again, and Sho took half a step toward the middle, for the cross, and Bokuto went line after all. Soft. A slow roll shot over the block, down the line, into the deep corner Sho had just stepped out of. Sho got 1 hand to it, flat on the floor, and it went out of the court off his knuckles.

He lay there and watched Akaashi walk back to his spot.

Then he got up, and on the next rally he watched the set.

It was a 9-year habit, from the room with the monitors. The setter tells you before the hitter does. And he saw it at once, now that he was looking at the right thing. When Akaashi wanted Bokuto to go cross, the set came a ball's width tighter to the net and a little further inside the antenna, and from there Bokuto's arm went cross by itself, because that was the only big swing the ball left him. When Akaashi wanted line, the set drifted out toward the antenna, and Bokuto's arm went down the line. Bokuto didn't have to know. Akaashi put the ball where he wanted, and Bokuto hit it there, happy, every time.

So that was how. The question was when. When had Akaashi decided where Sho would be?

At 6-11 Ukai called a timeout.

"How did you know line?" he said to Sho, in the huddle, before anything else. "On the 1st one."

"His wrist turned out. And he had a lot of arm left."

"Hm." Ukai looked at him for 1 beat longer than he looked at anybody else. "What else."

Sho told him in 3 lines, the way he used to write them for coaches to read before a match. "Akaashi-san sets him tighter when he wants cross. Out to the antenna when he wants line. Watch the set and you'll know before Bokuto-san does."

Ukai turned to his front row. "You heard. Tsukishima, you're on the set."

Tsukishima pushed his glasses up. "I was already on the set."

A few rotations later Sho came up to the front row and switched to the right side, across the net from Bokuto's spot. The 1st time Bokuto came at him, he read the set early, tight, cross, and he went up with Bokuto's approach, and hung there, and came down. Bokuto was still going up. Bokuto waited at the top of his jump until Sho's hands were on the way down, and hit the ball off the tips of Sho's fingers and out into the next court.

"HEY HEY HEY!" said Bokuto.

Sho stood at the net with his fingers stinging and looked at his own hands.

He'd been right about everything. The set, the cross, the angle. He'd been right too early.

On the next one he waited, and Bokuto hit over him anyway, because Bokuto was 2 years older and had about 20 centimeters of jump on him. But on the one after that he waited, and Tsukishima waited next to him, and the ball hit Tsukishima's hands and came straight back down onto Fukurodani's floor. Bokuto stood looking at it with his mouth open, and for the next 3 rallies he hit everything into the net, and Akaashi set the middle and the right side and said "Bokuto-san" twice, quietly, until he came back.

Back in the back row Sho called the short ones. "Short!" when Akaashi tipped, and Daichi was there. "Line!" once, and Suga was there. He didn't call Tsukishima's blocks. Tsukishima would have looked at him.

The sting at the back of his eyes stayed with him the whole game, faint and warm, and didn't get any worse.

They lost 25-17.

---

After the penalty, on the bench, with his chest still warm from the floor, Sho got his notebook out of his bag and wrote:

*Akaashi. #5. Read me in 1 dig. Stopped sending Bokuto's line to my corner. Never said a word to Bokuto. He steers with the set (tight = cross, out = line). When did he decide?*

He thought about it. Then he added:

*Everybody else watched the ball. He was watching me.*

He looked at that for a while. Then he wrote the line he'd been carrying since the timeout:

*Did he see me go still?*

He put the notebook back in his bag. On the court Ubugawa were warming up, and Ukai was already reading the next lineup off his clipboard, and Sho's name wasn't in it.

---

Saeko's car arrived at 3:10.

Sho was on the bench for the 3rd game, against Ubugawa, and heard it before anyone else did. A car engine somewhere outside the gym, revving far too hard, and then a squeal of tires on tarmac that went on for longer than tires should squeal. Then a car door slamming. Then another.

Then the side door of the gymnasium banged open, and Hinata came through it at a full sprint, in his school uniform, with his bag still on his back, and didn't stop running until he was at the edge of court 2, where he stood with his hands on his knees, gasping.

"WE... MADE IT!" he gasped, at the whole gym.

Behind him Kageyama walked in, a little green in the face, carrying his bag in 1 hand, and stopped just inside the door. He leaned against the wall. He looked as if he'd just stepped off a boat.

Behind Kageyama, in the doorway, there was a young woman with short blond hair and sunglasses pushed up on her head and car keys on her finger, grinning from ear to ear.

"RYU!" she shouted, across the whole gym. "I BROUGHT YOUR BABIES!"

On the court, waiting to receive, Tanaka put his face in his hands.

Ubugawa won 25-21. Karasuno threw themselves down the floor again. Sho's eyes, by then, felt like anybody else's.

Ukai let Hinata and Kageyama warm up, and put them in for the last game of the day, against Shinzen again. Sho watched from the bench as the quick came back onto the court for the 1st time that day: Hinata in the air before the ball left Kageyama's hands, the crack of it on the floor, Shinzen's blockers turning their heads 1 beat too late. Hinata and Kageyama still weren't speaking to each other. They ran the old quick, the one where Hinata shut his eyes, and it went in, and went in.

Shinzen won 25-23 on everything else.

Nekoma were waiting for the next court, sitting along the wall behind the Karasuno bench. Kenma was at the end of their row with a handheld game console in his lap, and he wasn't playing it. He was watching the court. He watched Hinata, the way everybody did, and then, for a few rallies, he watched Sho on the bench.

Sho felt it the way he'd felt Akaashi's look at 1 o'clock. He looked up and across, and Kenma's eyes had already gone down, the way they had in May in the handshake line, to Sho's feet, and stayed there a while, and went back to the game.

---

That night they slept on futons in a classroom on the 2nd floor of Fukurodani Academy, 13 futons in a row with the desks pushed to the walls, and the windows open on a Tokyo night that never got properly dark.

At 9:30 Sho went down to the courtyard with his phone, and sat on a bench under a light, and called home.

Mio answered on the 1st ring.

"You said every night," she said.

"It's night."

"It's 9:30. I've been waiting since 8." A rustle. The supermarket flyer, he guessed, unfolded on her knees. "Okay. Go. Who'd you play?"

He told her. Shinzen, 19 to 25. Fukurodani, 17 to 25. Ubugawa, 21 to 25. Shinzen again, after Hinata and Kageyama came, 23 to 25.

"You lost all of them," said Mio.

"All 4."

"So you lay on the floor 4 times."

"4 times." He told her about the flying falls, and that he'd laughed, and she said "Why would you laugh?" and he said "I don't know. It was fun," and the line went quiet long enough that he wondered if she was writing it down. He told her about Saeko-san, and the car, and Tanaka-senpai putting his face in his hands, and Mio laughed so hard she dropped the phone.

"Okay," she said, when she'd picked it up. "Eyes."

"Once," said Sho. "On their ace. Bokuto-san. He's really strong. I dug his best hit."

"And?"

"And my eyes stung a bit."

"Where?"

"Behind them. Like after you've been looking at a screen."

"How long?"

"Till the next game. Then it went."

"Less than Seijoh."

"Much less."

"Okay," said Mio. "I'm keeping score. What else."

"Their setter wears number 5."

"So?"

He looked at the light above the bench, where moths were going round it.

"Nothing," he said. "It's a good number. And he read me in 1 rally. He watched me dig 1 ball and then he never sent it to me again."

There was a pause on the line while Mio worked that out.

"Is that bad?"

"It's interesting," said Sho.

"That means bad."

"It means interesting." He watched the moths go round. "Tomorrow we play Nekoma. Their setter's the one who read me in May. In 4 rallies. Today Akaashi-san did it in 1."

"So tomorrow it'll be 0," said Mio.

"Maybe," he said.

"Then don't let him," said Mio. "Goodnight. Tell me tomorrow." And she hung up.

**Continuity (ch 32)**

- Sho POV, Saturday, trip 1, day 1 at Fukurodani Academy, Tokyo. The eye fired once (on Bokuto). Cost: a faint warm sting behind the eyes for the rest of that game, gone by the next (bible "Adapting"; user direction: no drama).
- Bus at the school gate 5:40, left 6:02, nearly 5 hours (driver about 60 in a cap). Kiyoko (first-aid box) and Yachi (clipboard, schedule) aboard. 2 empty seats near the front. Mom's bundle: 4 onigiri, frozen barley tea. Permission form and the Classical 58 in the zipped pocket. Tanaka/Noya: "Saeko-nee drives like a demon."
- **Satoshi memory:** at 20, the spring before the knee went, on a university team bus north to Sendai past Koriyama; knee sore for weeks, "nothing he was going to tell anybody about" (fits bible: 3 months of pain at 20). Notebook: *Fukurodani Academy Group. Day 1.* Tanaka: "Is that Tokyo?" Ennoshita: "That's Saitama."
- **Fukurodani's gym:** bigger than both Karasuno gyms; curved white steel and glass roof; 2 courts; echo. **Sound reads fail there** (passes blur); Sho switches to 1 look before every serve. A Fukurodani coach (polo, whistle): "Nekomata-sensei says hello... He says you've met." Nekoma already there; Sho sees Kenma and looks away first.
- **Akaashi #5** (Satoshi's university number, 2 years, navy on white). Sho's hand went to his left knee. Tsukishima: "You're staring at their setter." "He's good." "Should I get you his autograph?" Bokuto: "DID YOU SEE THAT, AKAASHI?" "I set it, Bokuto-san."
- **Game 1, Shinzen, lost 19-25 (12:00):** Suga (S), Narita and Tsukishima (MB), Daichi, Asahi, Tanaka, Noya (L). Sho on the bench folding a towel ("the way he'd watched for 9 years"). In at 14-18 for Asahi, back row. Asahi: "Um. Borrow it longer this time." (callback to ch 22 "Please let me borrow it for a bit"). 3 clean passes, 1 dig off the block, 1 back-row attack from the right blocked.
- **Flying falls** (Takeda explained them in ch 29): Yamaguchi "How many?" Daichi: "1 length. Every time we lose." Order Daichi, Suga, Asahi (like a seal), Tanaka ("FLYING FALL!"), Noya (skimmed), Sho. **Sho laughed on the floor** on his 3rd dive. Tsukishima: "You're enjoying this." "Yes."
- **Game 2, Fukurodani, lost 17-25 (12:50):** Ukai started Sho in Asahi's spot. Asahi (same year as Bokuto, no -san): "Um. Their 4. Bokuto. I watched him in warm-up. He hits cross more than line. When he's happy." Bokuto: "Kuroo says you've got a little one! A jumpy one!" Akaashi: "They're not here yet, Bokuto-san." **Eye fired on rally 1:** ghost's wrist turned out, arm long: line, deep, back right (against Asahi's scouting); Sho dug it wild; Noya scooped it over ("NICE, KIYOSE! LEAVE THE REST TO ME!"); Fukurodani scored. Bokuto: "13! You GOT it! That was my best one!" "I saw, Bokuto-san." Akaashi looked at Sho across the net.
- **Akaashi read Sho:** rally 4 cross at Noya; rally 6 free ball, middle quick; rally 9 Sho cheated toward cross, Bokuto rolled the line into the corner he'd left (1 hand, out). **Sho saw the mechanism** (analyst habit, watch the set): tight to the net and inside = cross; out to the antenna = line. Open question: *when* Akaashi decided.
- **Timeout at 6-11 (Den Den Mushi):** Ukai: "How did you know line?" "His wrist turned out. And he had a lot of arm left." "Hm." Sho's 3 lines: tight = cross, out = line, watch the set. Ukai: "Tsukishima, you're on the set." "I was already on the set."
- **Front row (right side vs Bokuto):** Sho read it right and jumped too early; Bokuto waited and hit off his fingertips ("He'd been right too early": seeds block timing). Next he waited and was hit over (Bokuto 2 years older, about 20 cm more jump); then Tsukishima stuffed Bokuto; Bokuto sulked 3 rallies, Akaashi said "Bokuto-san" twice. **Hawk-Eye calls** from the back row ("Short!", "Line!"); never calls Tsukishima's blocks.
- Notebook after the penalty: *Akaashi. #5. Read me in 1 dig. Stopped sending Bokuto's line to my corner. Never said a word to Bokuto. He steers with the set (tight = cross, out = line). When did he decide? / Everybody else watched the ball. He was watching me. / Did he see me go still?*
- **Game 3, Ubugawa, lost 21-25** (Sho benched). **3:10 Saeko's car (canon: Saeko drives them; staging invented):** squealing tires; Hinata sprinted in in uniform: "WE... MADE IT!"; Kageyama green, leaning on the wall; Saeko (short blond hair, sunglasses on her head, keys): "RYU! I BROUGHT YOUR BABIES!" Tanaka, waiting to receive, put his face in his hands.
- **Game 4, Shinzen again, lost 23-25** with Hinata and Kageyama in; still not speaking to each other; the old eyes-shut quick went in (nobody stops it yet; Lev's block is day 2). Canon: Karasuno lose every game on trip 1.
- **Kenma** along the wall waiting for the next court, console in his lap, not playing; watched Hinata, then Sho; eyes went to Sho's feet (May handshake line callback) and stayed a while.
- Night: 13 futons in a 2nd-floor classroom. **Mio call (9:30, courtyard bench, moths; she has the flyer):** "You lost all of them." "All 4." "So you lay on the floor 4 times." The laugh ("It was fun."). Saeko; Mio dropped the phone laughing. Eyes: "Once... stung a bit." "Where?" "Behind them. Like after you've been looking at a screen." "How long?" "Till the next game." "Less than Seijoh." "Much less." "I'm keeping score." Akaashi #5 ("It's a good number."). "He read me in 1 rally." "Is that bad?" "It's interesting." "That means bad." Kenma read him in May in 4 rallies. "So tomorrow it'll be 0." "Maybe." "Then don't let him."
- Next: ch 33 "Read" (Kenma POV, surprise POV 1): day 2 vs Nekoma; Kenma reads Sho's stillness; Lev's block on Hinata (Lev's); loss.
- No status card.

## Chapter 33: Read (Kenma)

The good spot in Fukurodani's gym was the corner behind court 2, between the folded-up scoreboard and the wall, where the morning sun didn't reach and nobody walked past on the way to the toilets. Kozume Kenma had found it yesterday almost as soon as he walked in. He was in it at 7:40 on Sunday with his back against the wall and his knees up and his console resting on them, playing nothing.

The game was paused on the save screen. It had been paused there since he sat down.

He was thinking about a 1st year from Karasuno who'd laughed on the floor.

It had happened yesterday, a little after 12, on the other court. Kenma had been waiting for Nekoma's turn, and Karasuno had lost to Shinzen and lined up on the end line for the penalty, and he'd watched them do it because it was there to be watched. The captain went 1st. Then the gray-haired setter, the one who'd set all morning because the real setter was somewhere else failing English. Then the big scary one who wasn't scary. Then the shaved one, yelling. Then their libero. Then 13.

13 had thrown himself down Fukurodani's floor, and got up, and thrown himself down again, and on the 3rd one, flat on his chest in the middle of the court, he'd started laughing.

Kenma had looked at it for a long time.

Penalties were effort you did for nothing. They were the part of the game where the game made you pay for losing, and afterward there was only more floor. Shoyo would laugh at a penalty; Shoyo laughed at everything that made him tired. This was 13. 13 was the one who wrote in a notebook.

Kenma had thought about it on the train home, and in the bath, and for some of the night, and he still didn't have it. He didn't like not having it. Not having something meant he'd keep thinking about it, and thinking about it was effort too.

"You're not playing," said Kuroo, from above him.

Kenma kept his eyes on the screen. Kuroo was the only person who could find the good spot in any gym without trying, because he'd been finding Kenma in good spots since they were 7.

"I'm thinking."

"About Shoyo?"

"No."

Kuroo sat down against the wall next to him, folding up his long legs, close enough that Kenma could feel the warmth of him through the sleeve of his jersey. He'd brought 2 cartons of apple juice. He put 1 on Kenma's console, on top of the paused game.

"About notebook," said Kuroo.

Kenma moved the juice off the screen.

"He laughed," Kenma said. "Yesterday. During the penalty."

"I saw." Kuroo stuck the straw into his own carton. "He was having a nice time."

"It was a penalty."

"Some people like it." Kuroo drank. "Your little Shoyo likes it."

"Shoyo's different."

"Mm." Kuroo looked out at court 2, where Fukurodani's 1st years were putting up the net and Bokuto was trying to help and making it worse. "What else have you got on him? Notebook."

Kenma thought about not answering. It was easier to answer Kuroo than to have Kuroo keep asking. It had always been easier.

"In May he looked at everything," he said. "His head went round like a fan. So I waited at the top and let him look, and put the ball where he'd just looked away from. Twice. 3 with the freeze. Then he stopped looking."

Kuroo grinned into his juice. He remembered. He remembered the walk to the station afterward, too, Kenma knew, and how quiet Kenma had been on it.

"Yesterday he looked once," Kenma said. "Before the serve. Everything at once, and then nothing, and then he just watched the ball." He turned the console over in his hands. "That's a lot to fix in 2 months."

"So he's better."

"He's better." Kenma looked at the paused screen. "And he does something now. I don't know what it is yet."

Kuroo looked down at him with interest, the way he looked at a spiker who'd just hit around his block.

"Akaashi read him," Kenma said. "Yesterday. In 1 ball."

"Akaashi reads everybody in 1 ball."

"Akaashi took 1 ball," said Kenma, "to find something I didn't see in a whole match in May." He put the straw in his mouth and didn't drink. "So it's new. Or I missed it."

Kuroo didn't pick either. Out on court 2 the net went up, a bit crooked, and Bokuto shouted "HEY HEY HEY!" at it as if it had done something brave, and Kuroo finished his juice and stood.

"Karasuno at 9:20," he said. "Coach wants us at 8:30. Shoyo's here, you know. They both are."

"I know."

"He didn't come and say hi."

"I know," said Kenma.

Kuroo stood there a little longer than he needed to. Then he went off to see what Lev had broken.

Kenma pressed the button. The save screen went away and the game came back, a small man standing in a field with a sword. Kenma didn't move him. When you let go of the controller, the small man didn't do anything. He just stood and breathed, his shoulders going up and down, ready for whatever you pressed next.

Kenma watched him breathe. Then he saved, and turned it off.

---

Shoyo still hadn't come to say hi by 8:30.

He was warming up on the far side of court 2 in Karasuno's black, jumping, landing, jumping, his face shut tight round something. Kageyama was tossing balls to himself against the wall at the other end, and the 2 of them were careful never to need the same part of the gym. Everybody could see it. Karasuno's captain could see it, and was rubbing the back of his neck. Even Lev could see it, and Lev saw almost nothing that wasn't a ball.

"Are they fighting?" Lev asked, loudly, at the water bottles. "The tiny one and the scary setter? Are they having a fight?"

"Shut up, Lev," said Yaku, and kicked him in the back of the knee.

Kenma watched Shoyo jump. He didn't know what the fight was about. He thought he could guess. Yesterday afternoon, in the last game, Shoyo's quick had been the old one, eyes shut, perfect. This morning, in warm-up, at the top of every jump, Shoyo's eyes were open, as if he wanted to watch the ball come to him. And a few of them had come wrong.

Shoyo wanted to see more. Kenma could see wanting that. Wanting it as badly as Shoyo did just looked tiring.

He looked along Karasuno's line for 13.

13 was at the end of it, near the wall, doing hamstring stretches with his eyes on court 1, where Fukurodani's setter was warming up. Akaashi. 13 was watching Akaashi's hands. Every set. When Akaashi turned to set backward, 13's head tilted a tiny bit, the way a cat's does at a noise in a wall.

He studied setters. Kenma had known that since May. He'd watched 13 write in that notebook from the bench, small fast writing without looking down at the page, and he'd known, the way you know when somebody behind you on the train is reading your screen, that some of it was about him.

Kenma didn't like being in somebody's notebook. A notebook about a player was a guide. Kenma had never used a guide in his life. You played a game to find out how it worked; if somebody else had already written down how it worked, there was nothing left for you to find. And being *in* a guide was worse. It meant somebody had solved you.

13 looked up from his stretch, across 2 courts, straight at Kenma's corner.

Kenma looked at the floor. When he looked back up, 13 had gone back to Akaashi.

---

Nekomata didn't say much in the huddle. He never did. He was old, and small, and he stood in the middle of them with his hands behind his back like a man waiting for a bus he didn't mind missing.

"Karasuno," he said. "Their setter and their little one aren't speaking. You'll see it on the quick." He smiled, all his wrinkles at once. "Don't help them. Don't hurt them either. Just play."

"Yes, sir!"

"And Lev."

"Yes!"

"Hands up early on the little one. Higher than you think. Your arms are longer than you think, too."

"I KNOW!" said Lev. "I'm very long! I'm going to block him today! The tiny one! You'll see!"

"Mm," said Nekomata, still smiling. "Then let's see it."

Coach Naoi read the lineup. Kenma, Kuroo, Lev, Yamamoto, Fukunaga, Kai, and Yaku in for whichever middle was in the back. Kenma heard his own name and went to his spot and stood in it.

Across the net, Karasuno's 6 were in: the scary setter, Shoyo, the tall blond one with glasses, the captain, the big ace with the bun, the shaved one. Their libero tapped in for the blond one at the back. 13 was on the bench at the end, sitting very straight, with a towel over his knees and a pencil behind his ear.

Next to him sat a small blond girl in a Karasuno tracksuit, who hadn't been there in May. She had a big silver camcorder in both hands, the old school kind, and she was pointing it at the court with her elbows tucked in and her tongue between her teeth, as if it might jump out of her hands and run away.

The 1st whistle went at 9:22.

---

The quick went wrong on the 3rd rally.

Kenma had been waiting for it. Karasuno's setter got a good pass and turned his hands toward the middle, and Shoyo was already in the air before the ball had left Kageyama's fingers, the way he always was. That was the trick of it. Shoyo flew to a spot and the ball was delivered there, and Shoyo never had to look, because the ball was always there.

But Shoyo was looking.

His eyes were open and on the ball. His arm was back. And Kenma, who'd watched this attack all through May's match, saw what was wrong before anybody: Shoyo's arm was waiting. It was waiting for the ball to arrive so he could see it arrive, and Kageyama's set wasn't built for anybody to wait, and the ball came through where Shoyo's hand had been a blink ago.

Shoyo's fingers clipped it. It went sideways off his hand, out, into the bench.

"Hey!" said Kageyama.

Shoyo landed and turned his back and didn't answer him.
