It happened again at 5-3. The 3rd time, at 8-5, Shoyo went up eyes open and the set was right there for once, a good one, and he hit it hard, and Lev was in front of him.

Lev had been saying all morning that he was going to block the tiny one. He'd said it to Yaku and to Inuoka and to the man who sold drinks at the gate. He'd played volleyball since spring and knew about as much about reading a setter as Kenma knew about running, and none of that mattered, because he'd gone up the moment Shoyo did, and his arms went up and up, longer than seemed fair, and Shoyo's spike hit the very top of Lev's right hand and went straight up off it. Yaku ran under it and dug it to the ceiling.

Lev landed. He looked at his hand.

"I TOUCHED IT!" Lev said, to the whole gym. "I TOUCHED THE TINY ONE'S SPIKE! YAKU-SAN! DID YOU SEE?"

"Play the ball, you idiot!" Yaku yelled, from the floor.

Kenma set Tora, high, and Tora hit it into Karasuno's floor. 9-5.

Then Kenma looked across the net at Shoyo, because he couldn't help it.

Shoyo was standing in the middle of his own court looking at Lev's hand. Kenma knew Shoyo's angry face, and his scared face, and his hungry face. This one was smaller and quieter, the face somebody makes at a door they'd thought was open.

Kenma turned back to the net. He wished, a little, that it was more fun to watch.

---

13 came in at 12-8, to serve.

Karasuno had just won a rally, and their rotation had brought the big ace round to the back, and Kenma saw the paddle go up at Karasuno's bench and the ace walk off. 13 walked on. The ace touched his shoulder on the way past, and 13 nodded to him. The pencil was gone from behind his ear.

Kenma had decided at 7:40 in the corner how he'd start: with the May trick. Wait at the top for 1 beat, let 13 look around, put the ball where he'd just looked away from. He didn't really think it would work anymore. He wanted to see it not work with his own eyes.

13 served a plain float at Yaku, which was a waste, because Yaku could pass anything. Fukunaga hit Kenma's 1st set into Karasuno's block, and Karasuno's captain dug the deflection, and Kageyama set the shaved one, Tanaka, who hit it hard cross, and Yaku dug it, of course, and it came up to Kenma in a nice slow arc.

Kenma got under it, and waited, as long as the ball would let him.

13's head didn't move. He was standing in the back row with his weight on the balls of his feet and his hands loose, and his eyes were on Kenma's hands and nothing else. The beat came and went, and 13 didn't spend it.

Kenma set Tora, because he had to set somebody. Tora hit it down the line. Karasuno's captain dug it, and the rally went on without anything interesting in it, until a Nekoma block touch went long. 12-9.

Fixed, then. Properly.

Kenma let out a breath through his nose. It felt a bit like annoyance. He sat up a little, too, which annoyance didn't usually make him do.

13's 2nd serve went to Kai, who passed it, and Tora put it away. 13-9. Karasuno would have to win a rally to get the ball back, and 13 stayed where he was, at the back, and on defense he drifted into the middle of the back row, the way Karasuno set up with him on, and stood there.

---

At 13-9 Tora went up on the left against Kageyama and Shoyo and hit a big cross over the top of them, hard, at the deep corner where Karasuno's libero was supposed to be.

The libero had gone forward for the tip. The deep corner was empty.

Kenma saw what happened next because he'd been watching 13 instead of Tora, and nobody else in the gym was doing that. Just before Tora's hand touched the ball, 13 stopped.

The whole of him stopped. He'd been moving slightly the way receivers moved, little shifts of weight, a twitch of the knees, and then all of it went out of him at once, and he stood there in the middle of the back row for a piece of a second as if somebody had let go of his controller.

The stop showed in his feet first. Before his face, before his hands. His feet went flat and quiet on the floor, both of them at once.

Then he went. Before Tora hit it. 2 steps back and to the left and down, into the exact place where the ball arrived, and the ball hit his forearms and went up, high, a little wild. Kageyama ran under it and set Shoyo, and Shoyo, eyes open, hit it into the net.

14-9. Kenma barely noticed.

He was looking at 13, getting up off the floor. 13 blinked twice, hard. He put his thumb in the corner of his right eye, pressed once, and took it away.

In May there'd been something like that too. After the freeze. 13 had sat on the bench with his hands flat on his knees and looked at nothing, and Kenma had decided then that whatever 13 did came with a cost. A skill with a cooldown. You use it, it goes gray for a while, and you're ordinary until it comes back. Every game had skills like that. You learned how long the cooldown was, and then you hit the player inside it.

So when the next ball came to him from Yaku, Kenma set Tora again, and Tora hit it into the middle of the back row, straight at 13, inside his cooldown.

13 dug it.

He was ordinary about it. No stop. No early. He just moved like a good receiver, saw Tora's shoulder and went where it pointed, and the ball came up to Kageyama clean, and Karasuno scored off it. 14-10.

13 had his hands on his knees, getting his breath back. His eyes were fine. Clear, and calm, and a little interested in the scoreboard.

No cooldown, then. Or one so short Kenma couldn't find it. In May it had taken him the rest of a set to come back.

Kenma found he didn't mind that as much as he'd expected to.

---

Nekoma took the next rally, and Karasuno's coach called a timeout at 15-10.

Nekoma went to their bench. Kenma sat down on the end of it with a towel and drank from his bottle and kept his eyes on the floor, which was how he listened best.

"Their 13," said Nekomata, to nobody in particular, though he was standing in front of Kenma when he said it. "Does he look tired to you?"

Kenma shook his head.

"Hm." Nekomata smiled at the floor. "Lookers get tired. Watching takes it out of you, and that one's watching very hard." He shrugged. "He'll get tired. Later. Not today, maybe."

Kenma drank. He thought Nekomata was a bit wrong this time, and he didn't want to say so, because saying so would mean explaining, and explaining 13 meant explaining something he'd only just started to understand.

He'd seen it twice now. The stop. It had happened yesterday too: he could see it now that he knew what it looked like, 13 in the back row against Fukurodani, Bokuto's arm going back, 13 going still for that little piece of time before he moved for the line. Akaashi must have seen it then. Akaashi saw everything in setters' time. And in May, in the handshake line, Kenma had looked at 13's feet without knowing why. He knew why now. The feet were where it started.

13 went still when he knew where the ball was going.

Guessers kept moving. Guessers shifted and hopped and leaned both ways, because they were waiting to find out. 13 stopped because he'd already found out. The stillness was the moment he decided. After it, he went where he'd decided, and he stayed decided.

Hiding where the ball was going was hard work, and 13 was good at seeing through it. Letting him decide was easy. Then you made him decide wrong.

Kenma lowered the bottle.

"Tora," he said.

Yamamoto turned round so fast he nearly fell over the bench. Kenma didn't talk in timeouts much. When he did, people turned round.

"Next time you're on the left," said Kenma, to the towel, "run in like you're getting it. Louder. Swing all the way. Like it's yours."

Tora looked at him. Then he grinned, a big wicked grin. "So it's not mine."

"Maybe not."

"Got it."

"Kuro. Kai." Kenma still didn't look up. "Run everything. Every time. Even when I'm looking at Tora."

Kuroo's eyebrows went up over the top of his bottle. Kai, who never got excited about anything, nodded once.

The whistle went.

---

Nekoma had rotated on the point before the timeout, and now Kenma was in the front row, on the right, with Tora on the left and Kuroo in the middle. Kai and Fukunaga were in the back with Yaku. Nekoma served.

The right ball came on the 1st rally. Yaku dug a hard one from the shaved one, high and slow, plenty of time, and it came down toward Kenma, and Tora came in from the left.

Tora was always loud. This time he was a stampede, every step slapping the floor, his arms swinging all the way back, his face lit up, his whole body yelling *mine, mine, MINE.* Kai came in from the back row behind him. Kuroo came in up the middle, fast and low, like a man who'd just remembered something. Kenma turned his shoulders to Tora, and looked at Tora, and let the ball come down into his hands at the angle you'd use for a big high set to the left. And he watched 13.

13 was watching his hands. Then, at the edge of it, 13's eyes went to Tora's run, the big loud arm, the shoulder coming round, and his feet went flat and quiet on the floor, and he stopped.

Kenma set the middle.

He pushed it short and quick to Kuroo, who was already in the air because Kenma had told him to be, and Kuroo met it at the top and hit it sharp into the middle of the back row, into the space 13 had just been standing in. 13 was already 2 steps toward the corner where Tora's cross would have gone, if there'd been a cross. Tora had swung his whole arm at nothing, all the way through, and landed laughing.

16-10.

13 stood in the back corner and looked at the place where the ball had landed.

Kenma had been ready for angry. 13 looked at the spot on the floor, and then up at Tora, still grinning, and then across the net at Kenma, straight at him, the way he'd looked at Akaashi's hands in warm-up, with his head tilted a little, as if somewhere in Kenma there was a wire making a noise.

Kenma turned back to the net.

It worked again on the next serve. Tora loud, 13 still, and this time the set went back to Kai, a pipe from the back row up the middle, and 13 was 2 steps the wrong way again, reaching for it with 1 hand, too late. 17-10.

It worked a 3rd time on the serve after that, and this time Kenma didn't set it at all. He let 13 go still on Tora, and then he jumped and pushed the ball over the net with 2 fingers into the front of Karasuno's court, the dump, the oldest trick he had. It dropped in front of Kageyama and Shoyo while both of them were turning to block Tora. Yesterday, against Fukurodani, 13 had called the short ones from the back row; Kenma had heard him across the gym. This one he didn't call. He was 2 steps deep, in Tora's corner, by then.

18-10.

Across the net 13 stood with his hands on his hips, breathing, looking at the floor where the dump had gone. Then he did something Kenma hadn't expected.

He started moving.

On the next rally 13 never stopped. He kept his feet going the whole time, little shuffles, a rock from heel to toe and back, his knees always bending, like a guesser. Like somebody trying very hard to be a guesser.

He'd felt it, then. He'd felt that he was being read through something, and he'd guessed what, or nearly. Kenma was a bit impressed by that.

But you couldn't see what 13 saw and move like a guesser at the same time. When Tora hit a plain cross straight at him, no trick at all, 13 was rocking back on his heels as the ball came, and got to it a beat late, and it went off his arms into the bench. 19-10.

The stillness was where all his early came from. He couldn't throw away 1 without the other.

13 looked down at his own feet.

He stayed on through 1 more of Karasuno's rotations, over to the left side of the back row. He kept rocking. He passed 1 serve well and 1 badly and dug 1 ball from Fukunaga that he'd have been early on an hour ago, and wasn't. Then Karasuno won a rally at 21-12, and the rotation carried his spot to the front, and the big ace came back on for him. 13 walked off, and sat down on the end of Karasuno's bench next to the girl with the camcorder, and put the towel over his knees, and got the pencil out from somewhere, and wrote.

---

Nekoma won 25-17.

Kenma's last set was a plain high ball Tora could hit, and Tora hit it so hard that Karasuno's libero dug it into the wall. Then the whistle went, and everyone shouted, and Lev shouted the loudest about his hand, and Kenma went and sat down on the end of the bench and put the towel over his head.

From under the towel he watched Karasuno line up on the end line for the penalty.

Shoyo went with his face shut tight. He threw himself down the floor as if the floor had done something to him, and got up, and went again, and kept his eyes off Kageyama, who was 2 people behind him doing the same thing with the same face.

13 ran 3 steps and threw himself down. Got up. Down again. On the 3rd one, halfway down the court, he laughed.

It was shorter than yesterday's. It came out of him once, into the floor, and he got up still smiling and went again.

Kenma watched him do it all the way to the far end. He'd lost. He'd been read and made a fool of 3 times in a row by somebody who'd spent the timeout sitting on a bench with a towel. He'd tried something and it had made him worse, and he'd had to come off. And he was lying on the floor laughing.

Kenma didn't have it. He was starting to think he wouldn't get it by watching.

---

The water fountains at Fukurodani were in a corridor outside the gym, 3 of them in a row, low and cold. Kenma liked the end 1. It had the coldest water and nobody used it because it was furthest from the door.

13 was using it.

He was bent over it, cupping water onto the back of his neck. When Kenma came round the corner he straightened up, and wiped his hand on his shorts, and stepped back from the fountain to let him have it, and stayed.

Kenma drank. It took as long as he could make it take.

"When did you see it?" said 13.

His voice was polite. A little amused, the way it had been in May when he'd said *Mostly*. Up close he was tall, nearly as tall as Kuroo, and he stood very easily, the way Nekomata stood, as if he'd been standing for a long time and had stopped thinking about it.

Kenma straightened up. He thought about not answering. 13 would stand there looking at him until he did.

"Yesterday," said Kenma. "Against Fukurodani."

"Did Akaashi-san see it first?"

"Probably."

13 nodded slowly. "I know I stop," he said. "Somebody on my team told me. I don't know what it looks like from over there."

Kenma looked down the corridor, toward the gym door. The small blond girl was standing in it with her clipboard under her arm and the camcorder held against her chest the way you'd hold a cat that might jump. She'd come out to find 13, Kenma thought, and lost her nerve at the door.

"She's been filming you," said Kenma. "All morning."

13 turned and looked at the girl. The girl jumped, and nearly dropped the camcorder, and hid behind the door.

When 13 turned back, he was looking at Kenma the way Kuroo did when Kenma finally said the thing Kuroo had been waiting all day for him to say. Kenma didn't know what to do with that, coming from somebody who wasn't Kuroo. So he stood there and let it be looked at.

"Thank you," said 13.

"That's so you'll stop looking at me," said Kenma.

"Okay."

Kenma started to go. Then he stopped, because he still didn't have it, and it was going to keep him awake again.

"You laughed," he said. "On the floor. Twice."

13 thought about it. Kenma could see him thinking, actually hunting for the true answer, which most people didn't bother to do.

"It was fun," said 13.

"It was a penalty."

"I know," said 13, and smiled, a small one, mostly with his eyes.

Then Lev's voice came down the corridor from the gym door, saying "KENMA-SAN! Yaku-san says we're on in 5 minutes and you have to come or he'll kick me!" and Kenma went, because it was easier.

Behind him he could feel 13 standing by the end fountain, looking at the girl with the camcorder.

---

They played Karasuno again at 2:10. Nekoma won that one 25-20. Shoyo and Kageyama were still not talking, and Shoyo's eyes were still open, and Lev touched 2 more of his quicks and screamed about both of them. 13 came in at the back for the ace again.

He didn't go still once. He didn't rock either. He stood with his feet quiet, an ordinary receiver's feet, and his eyes on Kenma's hands, and he dug what came straight at him and missed what Fukunaga hit behind him, and he was never early at all.

He was being careful. He was playing like an ordinary good receiver, on purpose, so there'd be nothing on him to read until he'd seen for himself what Kenma had been reading.

It was the most boring 13 had ever been.

---

Nekoma went home on the train at 6. They took up most of a carriage. Lev slept with his head against the window and his mouth open, and Yaku sat next to him looking as if he'd like to kick him and was too tired. Tora was telling Inuoka about the stampede, again.

Kenma sat at the end by the door with his console, playing the small man in the field.

Kuroo sat down next to him at Ochanomizu.

"Notebook," he said.

Kenma kept playing.

"You beat him 3 times in a row," said Kuroo. "With 1 timeout. Tora's never been so happy. What was it?"

"He stops," said Kenma. "Before he's right."

Kuroo turned that over. "And you made him right about the wrong guy."

"Yeah."

"And then?"

Kenma's small man walked into a cave. "Then he stopped stopping, and got worse. Then this afternoon he played like nobody."

"So you broke him."

The small man fought a bat. "His manager filmed the whole day," Kenma said. He didn't add that he'd been the one to tell 13 so. Kuroo would find out on his own; Kuroo always did. "He'll find it on the tape."

Kenma killed the bat. He let go of the controller, and the small man stood in the cave and breathed, shoulders going up and down, waiting.

He'd already thought about what came after the tape, somewhere between 2:10 and Ochanomizu. 13 would watch himself stop. Then he'd fix it, or he wouldn't. If he couldn't, he'd be a good receiver who studied setters, and there were lots of those, and Kenma could beat lots of those.

If he fixed it, though. If he turned up at the camp in Saitama with the stop gone, or doing something with it Kenma hadn't thought of yet.

Kenma would rather he fixed it. That was more interesting.

He pressed the button, and the small man went on into the dark.

**Continuity (ch 33)**

- **Kenma POV (surprise POV 1 of Arc 3; close third, past tense).** Kenma's images: the save screen, the guide ("being *in* a guide... meant somebody had solved you"), the cooldown, the small man who stands and breathes when you let go of the controller. Sho is "13" in Kenma's head; Kuroo calls him "notebook"; Kenma calls Kuroo "Kuro", Yamamoto "Tora", Hinata "Shoyo". No eye from inside; from outside: the stillness, 1 impossible-early dig, 1 thumb pressed to the right eye, no cooldown found.
- Sunday, trip 1, day 2 at Fukurodani. Kenma's corner behind court 2; game paused since he sat down. Kenma puzzled all night by #13 laughing in yesterday's penalty ("Penalties were effort you did for nothing").
- **Kuroo** (2 apple juices; finding Kenma's spots since they were 7): Kenma's file: May = head "like a fan", the waiting trick worked "Twice. 3 with the freeze"; yesterday = 1 look; "That's a lot to fix in 2 months." "Akaashi took 1 ball to find something I didn't see in a whole match in May." "Coach wants us at 8:30." Shoyo didn't come to say hi.
- **Warm-up:** Hinata and Kageyama avoid each other; Lev: "Are they fighting?" Yaku kicks him. Day 1's last game: Hinata's old eyes-shut quick; day 2 warm-up: eyes open (canon direction). #13 watched Akaashi's hands; looked at Kenma's corner; Kenma looked at the floor.
- **Nekomata huddle (invented wording):** "Don't help them. Don't hurt them either. Just play." To Lev: "Hands up early... Your arms are longer than you think, too." Lev: "I'm very long! I'm going to block him today!" Coach Naoi read the lineup: Kenma, Kuroo, Lev, Yamamoto, Fukunaga, Kai; Yaku for the back-row middle.
- **Yachi on Karasuno's bench with a big silver school camcorder (invented), next to Sho** from the 1st whistle.
- **Game 1 vs Karasuno, 9:22, Nekoma won 25-17 (scores Nekoma-first).** Hinata's eyes-open quick clipped into the bench ("Hey!"). Again at 5-3. **At 8-5 Lev's block touch on Hinata (Lev's, canon; his own boast and reach):** "I TOUCHED THE TINY ONE'S SPIKE!" "Play the ball, you idiot!" Tora 9-5. Hinata's face: "a door they'd thought was open."
- **Sho in at 12-8 to serve (back-row sub for Asahi; defends middle-back).** The May trick fails (head doesn't move): "Fixed, then. Properly." 12-9 on a Nekoma block touch; 2nd serve to Kai, Tora scores 13-9. **13-9: the stillness ("the controller let go"; it shows in the feet first) and the early dig** on Tora's cross to the empty deep corner (libero up for the tip); Hinata into the net, 14-9. Kenma tests the "cooldown" with the next ball: ordinary clean dig, 14-10. "No cooldown, then. Or one so short Kenma couldn't find it."
- **Ukai timeout 15-10.** Nekomata: "Lookers get tired... He'll get tired. Later. Not today, maybe." Kenma's theory: **13 goes still when he knows, then stays decided; guessers keep moving.** Feet callback (May handshake line). Orders: "Tora... run in like you're getting it. Louder. Swing all the way. Like it's yours." "So it's not mine." "Maybe not." "Kuro. Kai. Run everything. Every time."
- **The decoys (Kenma front row right, Tora left, Kuroo middle; Kai, Fukunaga, Yaku back; Nekoma serving):** 16-10 Kuroo's quick into the space Sho left; 17-10 Kai's pipe; 18-10 Kenma's dump in front of Kageyama and Hinata (Sho didn't call it). Sho looked at Kenma "as if somewhere in Kenma there was a wire making a noise." **Sho tried to move like a guesser** and was late on a plain cross, 19-10. "The stillness was where all his early came from." He stayed 1 more rotation, rocking; Asahi back on at 21-12. Sho wrote on the bench beside Yachi.
- **Penalty:** Hinata and Kageyama dive with shut faces, apart. Sho laughed again, shorter.
- **Water fountains (end 1):** "When did you see it?" "Yesterday. Against Fukurodani." "Did Akaashi-san see it first?" "Probably." **"I know I stop... Somebody on my team told me. I don't know what it looks like from over there."** Kenma: "She's been filming you. All morning." (Yachi hid behind the door.) "Thank you." "That's so you'll stop looking at me." "You laughed... Twice." "It was fun." "It was a penalty." "I know." Lev: "KENMA-SAN!..."
- **Game 2 vs Karasuno, 2:10, Nekoma won 25-20** (canon: Karasuno take 0 sets off Nekoma on trip 1). Lev touched 2 more quicks. Sho played careful, never still, never rocking, never early ("the most boring 13 had ever been").
- **Train home at 6; Kuroo sat down at Ochanomizu:** "He stops. Before he's right." "And you made him right about the wrong guy." "He'll find it on the tape." Kenma would rather he fixed it before the Saitama camp: "That was more interesting." Kenma hasn't told Kuroo he pointed Sho at the camcorder.
- Next: ch 34 "Tape" (Sho): Yachi's camcorder footage; Sho watches himself go still; Hinata and Kageyama's fight, Tanaka breaks it up (theirs); Mio starts her own notebook. Day 2's other games and Mio's call can be told there.
- No status card.

## Chapter 34: Tape

The bus left Fukurodani at 6:05 on Sunday evening, and for the 1st hour nobody said anything at all.

Most of them would sleep later. For now it was the quiet of a busload of people who'd lost 8 games in 2 days and done 8 lengths of a gym floor on their chests, and who had nothing to say about it that wouldn't make it worse. Tokyo went by the windows in orange lights and train bridges. Tanaka sat with his arms folded and his jaw set, looking at the seat in front of him. Noya had his knees up and his chin on them. Daichi and Suga, in the front row, were talking with their heads close together, the way they did when they were deciding something for everybody.

Hinata was in the 3rd row on the right, by the window, with his forehead against the glass.

Kageyama was in the back row on the left, as far from the 3rd row on the right as the bus allowed.

Sho had the window seat halfway down. Yamaguchi had fallen asleep against his arm at the 1st set of traffic lights, the only one on the bus who could. Sho let him be.

He had his notebook on his knee, open to the page with *Fukurodani Academy Group. Day 1.* at the top, the lines about Akaashi under it, and under those, from this morning, in the pencil he'd carried behind his ear onto the Nekoma bench, 3 more:

*Kenma saw it. Yesterday. Akaashi probably 1st.*

*3 in a row off the same run. I went still on Yamamoto every time. Kuroo, Kai, then the dump.*

*Tried to keep moving. Worse.*

He'd read the 3 lines maybe 20 times since 2:10. He knew what they said. He hadn't seen it.

He closed the notebook and looked out of the window.

---

The bus pulled into Nasukogen service area at 9:20, for the toilets and the vending machines. Ukai went straight to the smoking corner. Takeda went to buy everybody cold barley tea with his own money, which Daichi tried to stop and couldn't.

Sho walked to the end of the car park, where the light from the shop didn't reach and the trucks were parked in a long sleeping row with their engines ticking, and called home.

Mio answered before it rang.

"You're late."

"We're on the bus. It's 9:20."

"Yesterday was 9:30. Today's earlier. You're late for today." A rustle. The flyer, unfolding on her knees, cheap paper gone soft along its folds. "Okay. Go."

He told her. Nekoma, 17 to 25, in the morning. Ubugawa, 22 to 25. Nekoma again after lunch, 20 to 25. Fukurodani at the end, when everybody was tired, 16 to 25.

"You lost all of them again."

"All 4."

"That's 8." Her pencil scratched. "8 times on the floor."

"8."

"Did you laugh?"

He thought about it. "Once. In the morning."

"Only once?"

"I was busy in the afternoon."

"Busy with what?"

He leaned against a lamp post that had no bulb in it and looked at the trucks.

"Eyes," he said, because eyes came after the scores; that was her order. "Once, in the 1st game. A little. It went before the end of the next rally."

"Where?"

"Same place. Behind them."

"Less than yesterday."

"Less than yesterday."

Scratch. "Okay. Busy with what?"

"The setter from Nekoma," he said. "The one from May."

"The one who read you in 4." She remembered everything. "I said tomorrow it'd be 0."

"0," said Sho. "He'd already seen it yesterday. You were right."

"I said." She sounded pleased, and then, half a breath later, suspicious. "Seen what?"

Sho looked down at his own feet on the tarmac, in his trainers, in the dark.

"I stop," he said. "Just before I know where the ball's going. I go still. My feet go still 1st. He watches my feet, and when they stop, he knows I've decided, and he sends it somewhere else."

The line went quiet. A truck engine started somewhere down the row, ran, and stopped again.

"You do that in the yard," said Mio.

"What?"

"After dinner. When you think nobody's looking. You stand like that with your hands out and you don't move, and then you move." She said it the way she said 7 times 8 was 56. "I watch you from the kitchen. Mom said leave him alone."

He hadn't known anybody watched him in the yard. He stood there under the dead lamp post and didn't have anything to say to that.

"Dad folds the paper smaller when he's worried," said Mio. "You do your mouth when you're lying. I told the lady. And your feet." Scratch, scratch. "I'm writing it down."

"On the flyer?"

"The flyer's full. I'm getting a proper one. Like yours. I've got one from school with only 2 pages used, for science, and the science was bad. I'll tear them out." A pause. "What else."

"Our manager filmed me," said Sho. "Yachi-san. All day. I'm going to watch it on the bus."

"The doctor said an hour."

"I haven't used any today."

"And not before bed," said Mio. "She said that too. I was there."

"We don't get home till 12."

Mio thought about it. He could hear her thinking: a small breath through the nose.

"Then stop at 11," she said. "That's not bed. 11's the bus." Scratch. "I'm writing that down too. Goodnight. Tell me about the film."

She hung up. He stood there with the phone in his hand, and then went back toward the light of the shop, where Takeda was standing at the bus door with an armful of cold barley tea, trying to give the cans out without dropping any.

---

Yachi came down the aisle about 20 minutes after the bus pulled out again.

She came the way she did everything on the team so far, as if the floor of the bus might give way under her and she was trying to be light enough that it wouldn't. She had the camcorder in both hands, held against her chest, the big silver one with *KARASUNO H.S. AV ROOM* on a strip of label tape along the side. She stopped beside Sho's seat, looked at Yamaguchi asleep on his arm, then at Sho, then at the camcorder.

"Kiyose-kun," she whispered. "Um. I'm sorry."

"What for?"

"I filmed you." It came out fast, as if she'd practiced it. "Takeda-sensei gave me the camera so I could film the games for Coach, and everybody moves so fast, and every time I tried to film the ball it was gone. So I filmed you. This morning, and after lunch too. A lot." She took a breath. "I'm sorry."

"Why me?"

Yachi's face went red, then white, then red again.

"You were the easiest," she said, very small. "To keep in the picture. Everybody else runs everywhere. Hinata was out of the picture before I even pointed at him. But you stand still a lot. Just before things happen. So I could always find you again."

Sho laughed. It came out of him low, so as not to wake Yamaguchi, and kept going until his shoulders shook with it, and Yachi stared at him with her mouth open.

"Sorry," he said. "That's a very good reason. It's the best reason anybody's given me today." He held out his hands. "May I?"

She handed over the camcorder as if it might be sick on him. Then she showed him, with her finger hovering above the buttons, how to flip out the little screen, and play, and pause, and which was rewind, and that if you paused it and then pressed the fast-forward button it went 1 picture at a time.

"Frame advance," said Sho.

"Is that what it's called?"

"That's what it's called."

"How do you know that?"

He looked at the little rubber buttons, the play and the rewind worn shiny by other people's thumbs.

"I used to have one," he said.

She went back to her seat at the front, beside Kiyoko, who was asleep with her glasses folded in her lap. Sho moved Yamaguchi's head off his arm and onto the headrest, and Yamaguchi said something about pizza and didn't wake up.

He flipped out the screen. It was about the size of a business card.

He got his phone out and set an alarm for 11:00, because Mio had said 11 and written it down, and put the phone face down on his knee, and pressed play.

---

The 1st thing on the tape was Fukurodani's ceiling, very bright, for 4 seconds, while Yachi worked out which way up to hold it. Then the floor. Then a corner of Karasuno's bench, and Takeda's knee, and a water bottle. Then the court.

Then him.

He was on the bench, at the end, with the towel on his knees and the pencil behind his ear. The picture rocked a little with Yachi's breathing. He watched himself watch the game: a tall boy sitting very straight, younger than he felt, with Satoshi's old habit of keeping a pencil behind the ear.

The last tape of himself he'd ever watched was a university semifinal, the spring he was 20, and he'd turned it off halfway through. He'd watched other people every day after that, for 9 years. Something over 10,000 hours of them. He'd known what Kenma did with his hands at the top of a set before he'd ever met Kenma, because he'd known what 50 setters like Kenma did with theirs.

He pressed fast-forward. 12-8. The paddle. The ace walking off. Him walking on.

At 13-9 Yamamoto went up on the left, and on the screen, in the middle of the back row, the figure in black with *13* on its back stopped.

Sho paused it, and went forward 1 picture at a time.

There. Yamamoto's arm going back. The figure that was him had been doing what receivers do, little shifts, knees, weight to 1 foot and back. Then, between 1 picture and the next, his heels came down. Both of them, flat, together, as if somebody had cut the strings. Then his knees stopped, then his hands. His head had been still the whole time. His head was always still. 3 pictures of nothing at all, and then he went.

3 frames. About a 10th of a second.

He went back and watched it again, and again. Heels, knees, hands, 3 frames, go. From where Yachi had been sitting it looked like a dog that has heard its name.

He let the tape run and found the next one, and the next, and counted them in the margin, a line for each, the way he used to count a setter's choices for a coach who wanted it on 1 page by 8 a.m.

7 stops while he was on court in the morning, before he'd started trying to hide them.

He went back through the 7 and marked where the ball had gone. 4 times he'd gone where it went. 1 was the dig on Yamamoto's cross, the one with the warm sting afterward. The other 3 he hadn't even noticed at the time; they'd just been him, playing, a step early.

3 times he'd gone where it didn't go. Kuroo's quick, Kai's pipe, Kenma's dump. All after the timeout. All off the same loud run.

He slowed the 1st of the 3 right down.

Yamamoto coming in, enormous, arm back. Kenma under the ball with his shoulders square to Yamamoto. Across the net, Sho watching Kenma's hands, then a flick of his eyes to Yamamoto's arm, then his heels coming down. And Kenma, at the top, with the ball almost in his fingers, holding there 1 frame longer than he needed. Kenma's chin was low, tilted down under the net, toward Karasuno's back row.

Waiting for Sho's feet.

He watched it 4 times. Then he wrote:

*Heels down, both at once. Then knees, then hands. Head never moves. 3 frames, then I go.*

*Kenma waits at the top for my heels.*

Then he found the rocking. On the tiny screen the figure with 13 on its back bounced heel to toe, heel to toe, knees going, hands going, all the way through the rally, like a boy outside the headmaster's office who needed the toilet. When Yamamoto's plain cross came, the figure was going back onto its heels at the wrong moment, and reached, and missed.

The 3rd time through he had to put his hand over his mouth.

Then he skipped to the afternoon, the careful game. There was nothing to circle in it. The figure stood with quiet feet and watched the setter and dug what came at it and missed what didn't, and was never early, and never wrong, and never anything.

He knew that figure. He'd fast-forwarded past it 10,000 times. A tidy receiver on a team that lost, whom no coach would ever ask about on a Tuesday morning.

He paused it and found he was leaning closer.

The bus hummed. Outside, the fields had gone completely dark, with a town far off now and then in orange. Most of the bus was asleep at last. Somebody 2 rows back was breathing slow and heavy, Asahi probably. At the front, the only light was the small green clock above the driver's head.

11:04.

Sho looked at his own hands. The camcorder was held up close to his face. His thumb was on the rewind button. His shoulders were up round his ears, his back had come away from the seat, his neck was bent to the little screen, and his eyes were dry from not blinking.

He turned his phone over on his knee. The alarm had gone at 11:00. It said so on the screen: *Alarm dismissed.* He'd turned it off himself, with his thumb, and gone straight back to the tape, and he couldn't remember doing it.

A desk. A dark room. A thumb on a button and a clock in the corner of the monitor saying 3 o'clock, and then saying 3:14.

He took his thumb off the button.

He closed the screen and put the camcorder down on his knee and sat back until he could feel the seat along his whole back. He looked out of the window at the far orange of a town and then at the glass in front of his nose, and then at the town again. Near, far. Near, far. The way Dad held the string at the other end.

Then he opened the notebook and wrote, slowly, sitting back:

*7 stops. 4 right. 3 wrong. The 3 wrong were all Kenma, all after he'd seen it.*

*The stop is where the early comes from. Rocking kills it. Being careful kills it too.*

He looked at that until the letters stopped meaning anything. Then he wrote the line he'd been going round and round since the fountain:

*So keep it. Then what?*

Under it, smaller:

*11:04. Mio said 11.*

He closed the notebook on the pencil and put the camcorder in its case and didn't open either of them again. Across the aisle and down the bus, Yachi's head had fallen sideways onto Kiyoko's shoulder.

---

The bus came up the hill to Karasuno's gate at 11:52.

Parents' cars were parked along the school wall with their engines running, headlights laying long yellow paths down the road. Dad was standing under the 3rd lamp post, on foot, in his jacket, with his hands in his pockets, because the house was 10 minutes away and he'd never once driven anywhere he could walk.

"Mm," said Dad, when Sho came across the road.

"Hi, Dad."

Dad took the bag off his shoulder without asking and put it on his own. They walked. The street was empty and smelled of somebody's mosquito coil. At the corner by the shrine Dad said, "Mio waited up."

"It's midnight."

"Till 11:20."

The house was dark except for the living room. Mom was at the kitchen table in her cardigan with a cup of tea gone cold. She got up when they came in and put her hand on Sho's cheek, and looked at his face, and at his eyes, and then went to the stove to heat the miso soup she'd kept back.

Mio was on the sofa, asleep on her side under the brown blanket, 1 arm hanging off the edge and her mouth a little open. On the floor below her hand, where it had slid when she fell asleep, was a notebook.

Sho picked it up.

It was the cheap kind with a pale blue cover that every primary school child in Miyagi had a stack of. The 1st 2 pages had been torn out roughly, and the stubs stuck out of the spine like teeth. On the front, in marker pen, in big careful letters, Mio had written:

*SHO*

He opened it.

The 1st page had a heading underlined twice: *THINGS HE DOES.* Under it was a numbered list in pencil, in Mio's handwriting, which leaned forward as if it was in a hurry.

*1. Mouth (when lying). Since flu in February. Told the nurse lady.*

*2. Rubs his eyes when they hurt. Right one more. Says it's nothing.*

*3. Puts his hand on his knee when he is thinking about something sad. (Which knee? LEFT.)*

*4. Feet stop before he knows where the ball is going. (The Tokyo setter saw. I knew already. The yard.)*

Under that, a line, and a 2nd heading: *SCORE.*

*Floor: 8.*

*Eyes: Sat 1, Sun 1. Sat till next game. Sun 1 rally. LESS.*

*Laughed: Sat 1, Sun 1.*

*Film: stop at 11.*

At the bottom of the page, after a gap, in smaller writing, there was 1 more line:

*5. Says "nothing" when it's something.*

Sho stood in the living room in his socks with the notebook in his hands.

Number 3. He'd caught himself at it on Saturday, at Fukurodani, looking at a 5 on somebody else's back, and taken his hand away, and thought that was the end of it.

For 3 months, at 20, Satoshi had trained on a knee that hurt every single day. He'd put his hand on it on the bench, and on the bus, and in the changing room, over and over, and nobody on that team had ever written it down. He'd made sure nobody would look.

Behind him Mom set a bowl on the table. He closed the notebook and put it back on the floor exactly where it had fallen, by Mio's hand, cover up, *SHO* on top.

Then he got 1 arm under Mio's knees and 1 under her back and picked her up, blanket and all. She was heavier than the last time. She didn't wake. She made a small cross noise into his shoulder, the noise she made when Mom turned her light on in the mornings, and her hand closed on a fistful of his T-shirt and stayed there.

He carried her up the stairs 1 at a time, slowly, and put her down on her bed and pulled the covers up over the brown blanket, and unhooked her hand from his shirt, 1 finger at a time.

At the door he stopped.

"Number 5's right," he said, to the dark room.

Then he went down and ate his soup. Mom sat across from him with her cold tea and watched him eat, and when he'd finished she took the bowl and said, "Bath," and he went.

---

On Monday the gym smelled the way it always did, floor wax and old wood and the rubber of the balls, and he was surprised how much he'd missed it in 2 days.

Practice was flat. Everybody was tired somewhere past their legs. Ukai didn't push. He ran them through receive and serve and then an hour of 6-on-6, and stood at the side with his arms folded and the cigarette unlit behind his ear, and said less than usual.

He'd put Hinata and Kageyama on opposite teams.

Sho was on Hinata's. Suga was setting for them, and on the 4th rally Suga gave Hinata a quick, fast and flat, the way Kageyama's went, as close as Suga could make it. Hinata went up with his eyes wide open. Sho was in the back row and saw all of it: Hinata at the top, eyes open, arm back, waiting for the ball to arrive so he could see it arrive, and the ball arriving a fraction early, and Hinata's hand meeting it late. It went into the net.

Across the net, Kageyama's face stayed exactly as it was.

"Again," said Hinata, to Suga.

Suga set it again. Hinata went up with his eyes open again. The ball went into the block.

"Again."

Ukai let him do it 4 more times. Then he blew the whistle and moved everybody round, and didn't say why.

Sho had given Yachi the camcorder back before practice, at the club room door, with a proper bow. She'd dropped her clipboard. He'd picked it up, and thanked her, and told her she'd filmed exactly the right thing.

"I filmed you standing still," she'd said, as if he might not have noticed.

"That was the right thing."

"Oh." Then, carefully, testing a step: "Should I... film it again? This week? So you can see if it changes?"

He'd been about to ask.

"Yes," he'd said. "Please."

Yachi had gone pink to the ears and run into the gym, and filmed the whole 6-on-6, standing on the bench in the corner with her elbows tucked in.

---

At 7:30, after practice, Sho sat on the concrete step by the side door with the camcorder and the notebook and an alarm set for 8:15. The light over the side door had been broken since June. Yachi held the torch from Takeda's car over the page for him, a little too high, the way she held everything, while he counted stops in the margin.

Inside, the gym lights went off bank by bank. Somebody was still in there. The thud of a ball against a wall. Then the side door opened, and Kageyama came out with his bag on his shoulder, and Hinata came out after him, fast.

"Kageyama."

Kageyama kept walking.

"Kageyama! Just once more. With my eyes open. I'll get it this time."

"You won't," said Kageyama, still walking. "The quick works because you don't look. That's why it works."

"Then I'll make it work while I look!"

"It's impossible."

"You don't know that!"

"I know it." Kageyama stopped, at last, under the dead lamp by the bike racks, and turned round. "The set gets there before you can see it. That's what it is. If you wait to see it, it's gone. I'm not changing it."

"Then I'll never be able to fight by myself!" Hinata's voice cracked and went up high. "Every time I'm in the air I'm just waiting for you! I can't see the block! I can't see anything! I just hit where you put it! I want to see, Kageyama, I want to be able to see what's in front of me..."

"It doesn't need you to see!"

Beside Sho, Yachi had stopped breathing. The torch had gone still, pointing at the step.

Sho closed the camcorder. His hand had closed on the strap so hard his knuckles had gone white. He made it open.

Hinata grabbed the front of Kageyama's shirt.

Kageyama grabbed Hinata's arm. They stood there holding on, glaring, and Sho thought it might stop there. Then Kageyama shoved, and Hinata shoved back, and they went sideways together into the bike rack with a crash that shook every bike in it, and Hinata swung, and Kageyama caught it on the shoulder, and they were on the ground.

Sho was on his feet. He'd put the camcorder into Yachi's hands without deciding to and gone 2 steps off the concrete.

"OI!"

Tanaka came out of the dark from the club room at a flat run, in his school trousers and his practice shirt with his tie in his fist, and yelled once, across the whole car park. Then he was on them. He got 1 hand in the back of Hinata's collar and the other in the back of Kageyama's, and stood up with both of them, and pulled them apart like 2 cats, and held them at arm's length while they kicked.

"Enough," said Tanaka, and this time it was quiet.

"He won't even TRY..."

"I said enough." He gave Hinata's collar a small shake. "Not with your hands. You're no good to anybody with your hands broken. Either of you." He looked at Kageyama, who'd stopped struggling and was breathing hard, a red mark coming up on his cheekbone. "Kageyama. Go home."

Kageyama looked at him. At Hinata. At the ground.

Tanaka let go of his collar. Kageyama picked his bag up off the tarmac and walked out of the gate, very straight, the way he walked off a court after a loss.

Hinata stood there with Tanaka's hand still in the back of his shirt, shaking all over, the way your arms shake after you've carried something too heavy for too long.

Tanaka let go. Then he sat down on the kerb by the bike racks, heavily, and patted the concrete next to him.

"Sit," he said.

Hinata sat.

Tanaka left it at that. He sat next to Hinata on the kerb in the dark with his tie in his hand, looking at the gate.

Sho had stopped 2 steps out from the side door. He turned round and went back to the step and sat down next to Yachi, who was holding the camcorder against her chest so hard he thought the case might crack.

"Are they going to be okay?" Yachi whispered.

Sho looked at the 2 shapes on the kerb. Hinata had put his head down on his knees. Tanaka had put 1 hand on the back of Hinata's neck and left it there.

"They'll both be at practice tomorrow," said Sho. "Still angry."

"Is that good?"

"It's better than 1 of them not coming."

Yachi thought about that. Then she held the camcorder out to him.

"Do you want to finish counting?"

He took it, and opened the screen, and Monday's 6-on-6 came back up, frozen where he'd stopped it: a tiny figure in the back row with 13 on its back, heels coming down.

He pressed play, and counted.

---

Ukai came out of the club room at 8:10 with the keys and saw the kerb, and stopped.

He walked over there 1st. He stood above Tanaka and Hinata with his hands in his tracksuit pockets and said something too low for Sho to hear. Tanaka nodded. Ukai said, "Tanaka. Walk him home," at a normal volume, and Tanaka got up and picked up both bags, his own and Hinata's, and Hinata got up too, and they went out of the gate together, Hinata with his head down, Tanaka talking about nothing, loudly, about ramen.

Then Ukai came to the step.

"Kiyose." He looked at the camcorder, at the notebook, at the pencil marks down the margin. "What's that?"

"Stops, Coach."

"Stops."

"Before I know where it's going, I go still. Their setter in Tokyo saw it and waited for it." Sho turned the notebook so Ukai could read it in the torchlight. "4 out of 7 right on Sunday. The 3 wrong were all him. Today, 13 out of 14. But nobody today was looking for it."

Ukai read the margin all the way down, Sunday and Monday both. He stopped at the bottom of Sunday's page, where it said *So keep it. Then what?*, and stayed on it.

Then he took the cigarette from behind his ear and turned it over in his fingers, the way he did when he was deciding whether a thing was worth the trouble.

"Thursday," he said. "After practice. Bring that page."

"Where are we going?"

"My grandfather's." He put the cigarette back behind his ear and tapped the bottom line of the page once with 1 finger, *Then what?*, and went to lock the gym.

Yachi switched the torch off. In the dark, the page was just a pale shape on Sho's knee, and the last line on it was too small to read, and he didn't need to read it.

**Continuity (ch 34)**

- Sho POV, Sunday evening (trip 1 ends) to Monday night. No eye. No status card.
- **Bus home:** left Fukurodani 6:05. Hinata 3rd row right by the window; Kageyama back row left. Yamaguchi asleep on Sho's arm from the 1st traffic lights. Notebook lines from Sunday: *Kenma saw it. Yesterday. Akaashi probably 1st. / 3 in a row off the same run. I went still on Yamamoto every time. Kuroo, Kai, then the dump. / Tried to keep moving. Worse.*
- **Day 2 results (invented scores; canon: Karasuno lose the weekend):** Nekoma 17-25, Ubugawa 22-25, Nekoma 20-25, Fukurodani 16-25. Weekend: 8 games, 8 losses, 8 flying-fall lengths.
- **Nasukogen service area, 9:20, Mio call** (the flyer): "8 times on the floor." Laughed once. **Eyes: once, 1st game, gone before the end of the next rally ("Less than yesterday").** **Kenma: "0... You were right." "I said."** (pays her ch 32 line.) "I stop... My feet go still 1st." **Mio: "You do that in the yard. After dinner... I watch you from the kitchen. Mom said leave him alone."** (ch 23: the yard stance.) Dad folds the paper smaller when worried; Sho does his mouth when lying ("I told the lady"). Getting "a proper one. Like yours" (school notebook, 2 science pages torn out). **Doctor's rule (ch 28: an hour a day, not before bed): Mio: "Then stop at 11. That's not bed. 11's the bus."** Takeda bought cold barley tea for everybody.
- **Yachi and the camcorder (invented):** big silver *KARASUNO H.S. AV ROOM* camcorder, from Takeda, to film games for Ukai; she filmed Sho "This morning, and after lunch too" "because you stand still a lot. Just before things happen. So I could always find you again." Sho laughed. "Frame advance." "I used to have one."
- **The tape (alarm set for 11:00):** last tape of himself Satoshi watched: a university semifinal the spring he was 20, turned off halfway. Over 10,000 hours of other people. **The stop: heels down both at once, then knees, then hands; head never moves; 3 frames (~0.1 s at 30 fps), then he goes** ("like a dog that has heard its name"). **Sunday morning: 7 stops before he started hiding them: 4 right (the Yamamoto dig + 3 unnoticed), 3 wrong (Kuroo quick, Kai pipe, dump; all after the timeout, all off Yamamoto's run).** Kenma's chin low toward the back row, holding 1 frame at the top for Sho's heels. The rocking: "a boy outside the headmaster's office who needed the toilet." The careful afternoon game: "never early, and never wrong, and never anything."
- **11:04:** hunched, thumb on rewind; **the 11:00 alarm said *Alarm dismissed*; he'd turned it off himself without remembering** (Satoshi's 3:14 a.m. desk). Took his thumb off, sat back, near-far with the far lights (Dad's string). Notebook: *Heels down, both at once. Then knees, then hands. Head never moves. 3 frames, then I go. / Kenma waits at the top for my heels. / 7 stops. 4 right. 3 wrong. The 3 wrong were all Kenma, all after he'd seen it. / The stop is where the early comes from. Rocking kills it. Being careful kills it too. / So keep it. Then what? / 11:04. Mio said 11.*
- **Home:** bus at the gate 11:52; **Dad on foot** (never drives where he can walk), carried the bag; "Mio waited up." "Till 11:20." Mom (cardigan, cold tea) hand on his cheek, looked at his eyes, heated miso soup; after: "Bath."
- **Mio's notebook** (pale blue, 2 pages torn out, *SHO* in marker): *THINGS HE DOES. 1. Mouth (when lying). Since flu in February. Told the nurse lady. 2. Rubs his eyes when they hurt. Right one more. Says it's nothing. 3. Puts his hand on his knee when he is thinking about something sad. (Which knee? LEFT.) 4. Feet stop before he knows where the ball is going. (The Tokyo setter saw. I knew already. The yard.)* *SCORE. Floor: 8. Eyes: Sat 1, Sun 1. Sat till next game. Sun 1 rally. LESS. Laughed: Sat 1, Sun 1. Film: stop at 11.* *5. Says "nothing" when it's something.* Sho had caught his hand on his knee at Fukurodani (ch 32); Satoshi's 3 months of knee pain at 20 ("He'd made sure nobody would look"). Put it back where it fell; carried her to bed (her fist in his T-shirt): "Number 5's right."
- **Monday practice:** flat. Ukai put Hinata and Kageyama on opposite teams. Suga set Hinata a flat fast quick; **Hinata's eyes-open swings: into the net, into the block, 6 tries; Kageyama's face didn't move**; Ukai moved everybody round. Sho returned the camcorder with a bow; Yachi: "Should I... film it again? This week?" "Yes. Please." She filmed the 6-on-6 standing on the bench.
- **The fight (canon ch 82, Hinata's and Kageyama's; wording invented), 7:30 to 8:00 outside, side-door light broken since June:** Yachi holding Takeda's torch; Sho had an alarm for 8:15. Hinata: "Just once more. With my eyes open." Kageyama: "The quick works because you don't look... It's impossible... I'm not changing it." Hinata: "Then I'll never be able to fight by myself!... I want to see." "It doesn't need you to see!" Physical (shirt, shove, bike rack, ground). Sho's hand white on the strap; 2 steps off the step. **Tanaka: "OI!" at a run, then both collars: "Enough... Not with your hands... Kageyama. Go home."** Kageyama left (red mark on his cheekbone). Tanaka sat on the kerb with Hinata, hand on his neck. Sho went back to the step. Yachi: "Are they going to be okay?" "They'll both be at practice tomorrow. Still angry." "Is that good?" "It's better than 1 of them not coming."
- **Monday tape: 13 of 14 stops right** (nobody looking for it).
- **Ukai 8:10:** went to the kerb 1st ("Tanaka. Walk him home."; Tanaka carried both bags, talked loudly about ramen). Read Sho's margin; stopped on *So keep it. Then what?* **"Thursday. After practice. Bring that page." "Where are we going?" "My grandfather's."** Tapped *Then what?*
- Next: ch 35 "Ikkei" (Sho): Thursday at Ukai Ikkei's (Hinata's own tempo lessons with Ikkei are canon and separate; Keishin takes Hinata). Ikkei on looking at what can't happen; the stop. Kageyama back from Oikawa: "He asked if you still squint." Hinata and Kageyama not speaking (canon: about 3 weeks).

## Chapter 35: Ikkei

Hinata came back on Wednesday with a word.

Nobody knew where he'd been on Tuesday evening. He'd left practice early with Coach, the 2 of them getting into the shop's white van in the car park without explaining anything, and on Wednesday morning he came up the hill to the gym at 6:50 saying the word under his breath, over and over, like somebody trying to keep a phone number in his head.

"Tempo," said Hinata, to the gym door. "Tempo. Tempo."

"What's tempo?" said Noya.

"I don't know yet," said Hinata, and went in.

He didn't tell anybody else. He said the word all through Wednesday, to the ball, to the wall, to his own hands in the receive line. He jumped differently, too, in the spike drills, a little later than before, as if he was listening for something before he went. Sometimes it worked and mostly it didn't, and every time it didn't he went straight back to the end of the line without a word to anybody.

He and Kageyama still weren't speaking. They were at practice, both of them, every morning and every evening, just as Sho had told Yachi they'd be, except on Tuesday evening, when Kageyama had left early too, separately, 10 minutes after the van. They worked hard at not speaking. If Kageyama was setting at the near court, Hinata hit at the far one. If they passed each other at the water bottles, they both looked at the bottles.

Sho watched it the way you'd watch weather, and kept out of it.

---

On Thursday, at the evening water break, Kageyama came and stood next to him.

Sho was sitting against the wall under the window with his bottle. Kageyama stayed standing, with his own bottle in his hand, not drinking from it, looking out at the court where Tanaka and Noya were arguing about a line call.

Sho waited. Kageyama was 1 of those people you had to wait for.

"I saw Oikawa-san," said Kageyama at last.

"Oh?"

"On Tuesday. At the old Coach Ukai's class. The kids' one." Kageyama frowned at the court. "His nephew goes there. He was picking him up."

Tuesday. Sho kept his face where it was. He could see the shape of a story in how stiffly Kageyama was holding the bottle.

"He asked about you," said Kageyama.

"About me?"

"He asked if you still squint." Kageyama finally drank, a short angry swallow, as if the question had been his own fault. "I said I'd never noticed. He said, 'Typical.'"

Sho laughed before he could help it. Kageyama's ears went red.

"Do you?" said Kageyama.

"Squint?"

"Still."

Sho checked, the way he'd check a pass. In June, after Seijoh, he'd squinted at everything till the Wednesday clinic: menus, the board, Mio's handwriting. Now the board at the front of 1-5 was sharp from the back row. Akaashi's hands had been sharp across 2 courts.

"No," he said. "Not anymore."

Kageyama nodded slowly, as if he'd guessed and wanted it confirmed, and stayed where he was, looking at the court. Hinata was at the far end of it, alone, tossing a ball to himself and jumping for it with his eyes wide open, over and over, landing badly, going again.

"When he jumps like that," said Kageyama. He stopped. He started again, lower. "With his eyes open. You're in the back row. You see it." He didn't look at Sho. "What do you see?"

Sho looked at Hinata. He saw a great deal. He saw the arm waiting. He saw the head lifting a fraction late. He saw, as he'd seen on Monday from the back row, a boy trying to watch the ball arrive and a set that had been built to arrive before anyone could watch it. He could have put it in 3 lines on a page. He'd been doing that for other people's setters for 9 years.

"Too much," said Sho.

Kageyama turned his head and looked at him at last. "Too much what?"

Sho drank from his bottle.

Kageyama kept looking at him, his eyebrows pulling together the way they did when a set had gone somewhere he hadn't meant it to. Then he looked back at Hinata, jumping alone at the far end of the court with his eyes open.

"Tch," said Kageyama, to nobody, and went back to the court.

Ukai blew the whistle. Sho got up. On the way past the bench he saw Yachi on it, cross-legged, with the camcorder on her knee, and she gave him a small serious thumbs up, like somebody signaling from a submarine.

At 6:10, in the middle of a serve drill, Ukai said, "Kiyose. Get changed," the way he'd said it to Hinata on Tuesday, and went out to the van.

---

The van smelled of cigarettes and cardboard and the sweet chemical smell of the shop's air freshener, which was meant to be lemon and wasn't. There were boxes of instant noodles stacked in the back, and a crate of bottled tea, and a folded-up banner for the shop's summer sale that had slid off the pile onto the floor.

Ukai drove with 1 hand on the wheel and his window down and the cigarette, for once, actually lit, held out of the window between 2 fingers so the smoke went backward into Miyagi and not into Sho.

"I said after practice," he said, at the 1st set of lights. "He finishes at 7. So I lied. He's going to be rude, too."

"You said."

"I'm saying it again. He's rude to everybody. He was rude to the doctor who saved his life. He's rude to the kids, and the kids love it, which I don't understand." He tapped ash out of the window. "Don't take it personally."

"Okay."

"Or do. I don't care." The light went green. "Just don't argue with him. He likes it when you argue, and then you're there till 10."

Sho had the notebook on his knee, closed, with his thumb in the page.

"What happened to him?" he said. "Last year."

"Collapsed," he said. "In the gym. In the middle of a practice. He was 71, and he'd been coaching Karasuno like it was the national team, every day, 7 days a week, and his heart said no." He turned onto a narrower road between rice fields, gone blue-green in the evening. "Doctor told him to rest. Stay home. Watch television. Grow tomatoes."

"Does he?"

"He grows tomatoes," said Ukai. "And twice a week he coaches 8-year-olds in a community gym. That's his idea of rest." He flicked the cigarette end into an ashtray clipped to the dashboard. "He says the kids are easier on his heart than you lot were. The old you lot, I mean. My lot. I was 1 of the ones who wasn't easy on it."

Sho looked out of the window at the rice. A heron was standing in it, on 1 leg, absolutely still, watching the water.

"Why are you taking me?" he said.

Ukai didn't answer that 1 for longer. The road went over a small concrete bridge, and the van rattled.

"Because you wrote *Then what?* at the bottom of a page," he said, "and I don't know the answer. And he might."

---

The community gym was on the edge of a town Sho had never been to, a low building with a green roof next to a primary school and a playground and a vending machine glowing in the dusk. Its doors were open. The noise coming out of it was the noise of a lot of small children who'd been running for an hour and were nowhere near tired.

Inside, the gym was half the size of Karasuno's Second Gymnasium, with a lower ceiling, and a net set at the height for children, barely over Sho's head. About 15 kids between 7 and 10 were doing what was probably meant to be a receiving drill. 2 of them were receiving. The rest were chasing a ball that had got away into the corner, shrieking.

At the side of the court, on a folding chair, sat an old man with a whistle.

He was thin, much thinner than the man in the old team photo in Karasuno's club room, the one everybody looked at and nobody talked about. He had a blanket over his knees, in July, and a towel round his neck, and a face that looked as if it had been carved out of something hard and then left out in the weather for 70 years. He was watching the children chase the ball with an expression of total disgust.

"5!" he shouted, in a voice much bigger than his body. "5 of you after 1 ball! Is it a hamster? Is it going to escape? Kota! You're a receiver! Receivers stay home! STAY HOME!"

A small boy in a T-shirt 3 sizes too big ran back to his spot, grinning.

"Gramps," said Ukai, from the door.

The old man looked round. His eyes went to Keishin, and then past Keishin to Sho, and stayed there.

They were sharp eyes, set deep. They went over Sho once, quickly, head to feet: his height, his shoulders, his hands, his knees. Then they came back up and rested on his face. Sho had been looked at like that before, a few times, in the other life, by the 2 or 3 oldest coaches in the V.League, the ones who'd been scouting players since before Satoshi was born. It was the look of somebody pricing you.

"That's him?" said Ukai Ikkei.

"That's him."

"Hm." The old man turned back to the children. "Sit down. Both of you. Class finishes at 7. I'm not stopping for you."

They sat on the low bench by the wall, Sho with his knees almost up to his chin. He watched the old man coach.

He'd watched a hundred coaches on tape in the other life. He'd never watched one teach 8-year-olds. The old man was good. Under the shouting he was the real thing. He saw everything those children did. He saw the girl in the yellow shirt who flinched every time the ball came near her face, and put her at the back, in the corner, where it would only come at her arms. He saw the boy called Kota, who was fast and wild and wanted every ball, and gave him every ball, until Kota's arms were red and he was laughing and didn't care. He coughed, sometimes, a dry cough into the towel round his neck, and kept going.

Sho had been 20, with a ruined knee and everything else still working, and he'd gone and found a room with no windows and stayed in it for 9 years.

He looked down at his own hands, folded on the notebook. Then up at the old man, shouting at a 7-year-old about her feet.

At 7, the parents came. The children ran off to them shrieking about who'd won, and nobody had won, because it hadn't been a game. A mother bowed to the old man and he waved her off like a fly. The girl in the yellow shirt came back from the door to give him a sweet from her pocket, and he took it and told her to go home, and put it in his own pocket, carefully.

Then it was quiet. Just the 3 of them, and the low net, and the lights humming, and the sound of cicadas through the open doors.

Ikkei turned his chair round to face them.

"Keishin," he said. "Get a ball. Go over there. Hit at him."

Ukai looked at him. "I thought you were going to talk to him."

"I'm going to look at him. Then I'll know if he's worth talking to." Ikkei pointed at the court with his chin. "You. Boy. Go and stand there. Middle of the back. Like a receiver. Do what you do."

Sho went and stood there. The net came barely over his head; he could have blocked over it flat-footed. On the other side Ukai picked up a children's ball, soft and light, and stood up on a folding chair so he'd be hitting down from roughly a spiker's height, and looked at his grandfather as if to say *happy now?*

"Hit," said Ikkei.

Ukai threw it up and hit it.

It was an ordinary hit, a coach's hit, cross, at Sho's left. Sho watched Ukai's shoulder and hips and the way his arm came round, and his heels came down, and he went, and the ball came to his arms, and he passed it high back into the middle.

"Again," said Ikkei.

Line. Sho's heels came down; he went; he got it.

"Again."

Short, a tip, off the fingers. Heels; go; he got there with a step to spare.

"Again. Again." Sho, glancing at him between hits, saw where the old man's eyes were: low, on Sho's feet, the whole time.

After 10, Ikkei held up his hand.

"Stop."

Ukai got down off the chair.

Ikkei folded his hands on the blanket. Then he said, to Sho, "What are you looking at?"

"His shoulder, sir. And his hips. Where his arm comes round."

"No. Before that."

"You moved before he hit it," said Ikkei. "3 times out of 10, you were going before his hand touched the ball. Keishin's a terrible hitter, and you were still early on him." He coughed into the towel. "So what were you looking at?"

Sho stood in the middle of the children's court with his hands at his sides, and felt his mouth begin to do the thing that Mio had written down as number 1.

He stopped it.

"I don't know how to say it," he said. "It's true. I don't know."

Ikkei looked at him with those deep sharp eyes, and Sho let himself be looked at.

"Hm," said Ikkei. Then he put his hand out, palm up, toward the notebook on the bench.

"Keishin says you wrote something. Show me."

---

Ikkei read the whole notebook.

He started at the 1st page and turned them over 1 by 1 with a dry thumb, the April pages and the May pages, Nekoma in May with the box drawn round *Kenma*, the pages from Seijoh in June in 2 different handwritings, Suga's round and careful where Sho had been holding a cold pack on his face. He didn't comment. Sometimes he grunted. Once he went back a page to check something. Ukai sat on the bench with his arms folded and the cigarette back behind his ear and looked at the ceiling.

It took a long time. The cicadas went on outside. Sho sat on the bench and kept still.

In the middle of June, Ikkei stopped.

It was the page Sho had written on the train home from the clinic, the 1st page of the new part, with the date at the top and 5 lines under it in his own careful hand:

*Hand up at the burning.*

*Tell Coach 1st.*

*Build the map so I don't need the eye.*

*Listen.*

*Play all 3 sets.*

Ikkei read it, and stayed on it. He didn't turn the page.

Sho found that his left hand had gone to his left knee. It was resting there, on the kneecap, the way Mio had drawn it in her notebook with *LEFT* in capitals. He felt it there and left it, because taking it away now would be worse.

Ikkei's eyes went once from the page to Sho's hand on his knee, and back to the page. Then he turned it over and went on.

At last he got to Sunday's page, and the bottom of it: *So keep it. Then what?*, and under that, smaller, *11:04. Mio said 11.*

He read it twice. Then he laughed.

It was a short, rough laugh, and it turned into a cough halfway through, and he coughed into the towel, and Ukai half stood up, and Ikkei waved him back down.

"Who's Mio?" said Ikkei.

"My sister, sir. She's 10."

"She said 11?"

"She said stop at 11."

"Did you?"

"No," said Sho. "4 minutes late."

Ikkei grunted, and looked at him over the top of the notebook for a beat longer than he'd looked at anything else in it. Then he tapped *Then what?* with 1 finger. "Who read you? Nekomata's setter? The pudding-head Keishin told me about?"

"Kozume-san. Yes, sir."

"Nekomata always did love a boy who watches." Ikkei closed the notebook on his finger, keeping the page. "When I was coaching against him, I scratched my ear before I called a timeout. Here." He touched his right ear. "Every time. Nekomata knew it by our 2nd match. He called his own timeout 1 second before mine, every time, for 40 years, and grinned at me across the gym."

He held up the closed notebook. "So. You stop when you know where the ball's going. You found that out. Good. And you tried hiding it, and it got worse. And you tried being careful, and you got boring." He looked at the page through the closed cover, as if he could still read it. "And you think the problem's the stopping."

Sho waited.

"The stopping's fine," said Ikkei. "Everybody good stops. You can't move right from a wobble. The problem is what you've decided when you stop."

"Where the ball's going."

"Where the ball's going." Ikkei nodded. "1 place. You pick 1 place and you go there, and you're right most of the time, because you're a clever boy who looks hard, and when you're wrong, you're 2 steps the wrong way with your arms out like a scarecrow, and Nekomata's setter has his ball in the floor." Ikkei put the notebook down on his blanket. "Listen. You're looking at where the ball's going to go. Stop that. Look at what can't happen."

Sho looked at him.

"Keishin. Up on the chair." Ikkei didn't look round. "Same as before. Don't hit it. Just get ready to."

Ukai sighed, and climbed back up on the folding chair, and held the children's ball up in his left hand at the top, where a toss would be, and drew his right arm back, and held that too, looking extremely tired of his grandfather.

"Look at him," said Ikkei to Sho. "Tell me where he can't hit it."

Sho looked.

It was a strange thing to be asked. He'd crossed things out for 9 years, on paper: *never sets the pipe from rotation 4*, *never serves short to the libero*. He'd never once crossed anything out with his feet, standing on a court, with a ball about to come. He looked at Ukai's shoulder, turned toward the left; at his hips, square to the net; at his elbow, low; at the ball, a little behind his head.

"He can't hit line," said Sho slowly. "Not hard. His shoulder's turned too far. And he can't hit deep cross, he's too close under it, it'd go out."

"What else."

"He can't tip. His arm's all the way back and his hand's open. He's going to swing."

"So?"

Sho looked at the court. The children's court, small and low, with the line taped on the floor. In his head, without his choosing it, bits of it went dark. The line went dark. The deep cross went dark. The strip just over the net went dark. What was left was a shape, a lopsided strip across the middle of the court, short cross to the middle of the back.

"It's somewhere in there," said Sho.

"So stand in there," said Ikkei. "Keishin. Hit it."

Ukai hit it, short cross. Sho was already in the strip, and he took a half step, and the ball came to him, and he passed it, and his heels had come down, he felt them, but they'd come down somewhere that was right for 3 different balls instead of 1.

"Again," said Ikkei. "And this time do it in your head before he's at the top."

They did it 10 more times. It was slow. It was terribly slow. Sho could feel his head working like an old computer, crossing out 1 thing and then another while the ball was already on its way, and twice he hadn't finished crossing out before Ukai hit it, and he went for a ball he'd already ruled out and missed it. Once he crossed out the wrong thing entirely, and the ball went straight into the place he'd decided it couldn't, and he stood there looking at it.

"Ha," said Ikkei, and clapped his hands once.

"He couldn't hit that from there," said Sho.

"He's a terrible hitter. Terrible hitters do things that can't happen. So do good ones, sometimes." Ikkei pulled the blanket up on his knees. "It'll be slow for a month. Maybe 2. Then it won't be. Then you'll do it without words, the way you do the other thing, whatever that is."

"And stop looking at everything like you're counting change in a shop," said Ikkei. "I watched you watch my class. Your eyes went everywhere. Flick, flick, flick. Look at the middle. The setter, the middle of the net, something. Let the edges come to you. They'll come. You don't have to go and fetch them."

Sho stood in the middle of the children's court, and tried it, there and then: his eyes on the middle of the low net, on the tape, and the edges of the gym lying round it, Ukai on his chair, the bench, the open door, all there at once without his going to look at any of them. It made him dizzy. It made the edges blurry and wide.

"Slow," said Ikkei. "Also for a month. Go home. You're tiring me out."

---

They were at the door, Ukai jingling the van keys, when Ikkei spoke again behind them.

"Keishin."

Ukai turned round.

"That one sees the hit before the hitter does." Ikkei was sitting in his folding chair with the blanket over his knees, unwrapping the sweet the little girl had given him, with his eyes on the wrapper. "Bring him back after the camp. I want to see if he's done it."

Ukai looked at his grandfather. Then at Sho.

"Yeah," he said. "Okay."

Sho bowed from the door, low, the full bow he used for Mom's parents at New Year.

"Thank you very much, sir."

Ikkei put the sweet in his mouth and flapped a hand at him.

---

It was dark by the time the van got back onto the main road. The rice had gone black on both sides, and the headlights of the cars coming the other way came and went across the windscreen.

Ukai drove back with both hands on the wheel, which he never did, and the cigarette stayed behind his ear.

"*Look at what can't happen,*" he said, at the next junction. "He said that to me when I was 12. About blocking. I've been working out what it means for 14 years."

"Do you know now?"

"Some of it." Ukai turned off toward Karasuno. He glanced at Sho in the dark, a short sideways look, and back at the road. "*Sees the hit before the hitter.* What's that about?"

Sho looked out of the windscreen.

"I see things early," he said. "It's IQ."

Ukai's head turned. "You heard that?"

"You said it to Takeda-sensei. In the gym. Before exams."

"Hm." A car passed them, and then another. "Annoying old man," said Ukai, to the road.

The car in front of them was a small blue one, in the left lane, with its indicator off.

Sho looked at it. Before he'd decided to, his head did the new thing, slowly. It can't turn right: wrong lane, no signal. It can't stop: there's nobody behind it, the light's green. It's going straight, or left into the petrol station.

It went left, into the petrol station.

He tried it on the next car. A white van, a delivery van like Ukai's. It can't turn, it's too big for that road. It can't speed up, there's a bend. It went straight, slowly.

He did it all the way back to Karasuno, car after car, at every junction, while Ukai drove with both hands and the radio off. He got most of them. It was slow, and his head ached a little behind the forehead the way it did after Classical homework, and it was the most fun he'd had all week.

At the last set of lights before the school a taxi in front of them did a complete U-turn across 2 lanes, without signaling, in a place where no car could possibly turn.

Sho laughed out loud.

"What?" said Ukai.

"Taxi," said Sho.

Ukai looked at the taxi, and at Sho, and shook his head, and the light went green.

He pulled up at the school gate at 8:20, by the dead lamp post, and Sho turned on the little light in the van's roof and opened the notebook on his knee to Sunday's page. Under *So keep it. Then what?* he wrote:

*Look at what can't happen. Stand in what's left.*

*Slow for a month. (He said maybe 2.)*

Then, under that, because Takeda had pinned the camp letter to the club room door that morning and he'd counted the days on the way out:

*Saitama: 16 days.*

Ukai leaned over and read it upside down, and grunted, and turned the roof light off.

**Continuity (ch 35)**

- Sho POV, Tuesday to Thursday of the week after trip 1. No eye. No status card. Seeds: **ruling options out** ("Look at what can't happen") and **wide gaze** (taught, not working yet). Both "slow for a month."
- **Hinata (canon, his):** left practice early on Tuesday with Keishin in the shop's white van (to Ikkei's kids' class); came back Wednesday saying "Tempo" ("I don't know yet"); jumps a little later in spike drills; mostly misses. Kageyama also left early Tuesday, separately, 10 minutes after the van. Hinata and Kageyama still not speaking; both at every other practice.
- **Kageyama, Thursday water break:** met Oikawa on Tuesday at Ikkei's kids' class (canon: Oikawa picking up his nephew; their talk is Kageyama's, offstage). **"He asked if you still squint." "I said I'd never noticed. He said, 'Typical.'"** "Do you?" "No. Not anymore." (squinted after Seijoh till the Wednesday clinic.) Kageyama: "When he jumps like that... With his eyes open... What do you see?" **Sho: "Too much."** (guardrail line) "Too much what?" Sho drank. "Tch." Yachi's submarine thumbs up.
- **6:10 Ukai pulled Sho out of a serve drill ("Kiyose. Get changed.")**, like Hinata on Tuesday. ("I said after practice... He finishes at 7. So I lied.")
- **The van:** cigarettes, cardboard, fake-lemon air freshener, instant noodles, bottled tea, the summer-sale banner. "He was rude to the doctor who saved his life." "Just don't argue with him... then you're there till 10." **Ikkei (invented details): collapsed in the gym mid-practice at 71; told to rest; grows tomatoes; coaches 8-year-olds twice a week ("his idea of rest").** Keishin was "1 of the ones who wasn't easy on it."
