Heron in the rice. "Because you wrote *Then what?*... and I don't know the answer. And he might."
- **Community gym (canon: Ikkei teaches a kids' class; details invented):** green roof by a primary school; children's net barely over Sho's head; about 15 kids aged 7 to 10. Ikkei: thin, blanket over his knees in July, towel round his neck, dry cough, voice bigger than his body ("5 of you after 1 ball! Is it a hamster?... Receivers stay home! STAY HOME!"); Kota (wild, gets every ball); the girl in the yellow shirt (flinches; put in the back corner; gave him a sweet). Keishin calls him "Gramps." Sho: never watched a coach teach 8-year-olds; Satoshi at 20 chose "a room with no windows" for 9 years.
- **The test:** Keishin on a folding chair hitting a children's ball from his left; Ikkei watched Sho's feet. 10 balls (cross, line, tip...). "What are you looking at?" "His shoulder, sir. And his hips." "No. Before that." "You moved before he hit it... 3 times out of 10... Keishin's a terrible hitter, and you were still early on him." Sho's mouth started to lie (Mio's number 1); he stopped it: "I don't know how to say it. It's true. I don't know." Ikkei: "Hm."
- **Ikkei read the whole notebook** page by page; **stopped on the June page** (*Hand up at the burning. / Tell Coach 1st. / Build the map so I don't need the eye. / Listen. / Play all 3 sets.*); **Sho's left hand went to his left knee (Mio's number 3) and he left it there; Ikkei's eyes went once to the hand.** Then *So keep it. Then what? / 11:04. Mio said 11.*: Ikkei laughed and coughed. "Who's Mio?" "My sister, sir. She's 10." "Did you?" "No. 4 minutes late." "Nekomata's setter? The pudding-head Keishin told me about?" **Ikkei's tell (invented): scratched his right ear before calling a timeout; Nekomata knew by their 2nd match and called his own timeout 1 second earlier for 40 years.**
- **The lesson:** "The stopping's fine. Everybody good stops. You can't move right from a wobble. The problem is what you've decided when you stop." "Look at what can't happen." Keishin frozen on the chair (ball held up in his left hand, right arm back): "Tell me where he can't hit it." Sho (crossed things out on paper for 9 years, never with his feet): no hard line (shoulder turned), no deep cross (too far under), no tip (arm all the way back, hand open); **those parts go dark; what's left is a shape ("It's somewhere in there")**; "So stand in there." Heels come down somewhere right for 3 balls. 10 more: slow; 2 misses from not finishing in time; 1 ball into a spot he'd ruled out ("Terrible hitters do things that can't happen. So do good ones, sometimes."). "It'll be slow for a month. Maybe 2... Then you'll do it without words, the way you do the other thing, whatever that is."
- **Wide gaze:** "I watched you watch my class. Your eyes went everywhere. Flick, flick, flick. Look at the middle... Let the edges come to you." Dizzy, edges blurry. "Slow. Also for a month."
- **At the door: "Keishin. That one sees the hit before the hitter does. Bring him back after the camp. I want to see if he's done it."** Sho's full New Year bow.
- **Drive back:** Ukai drove with both hands. "He said that to me when I was 12. About blocking. I've been working out what it means for 14 years." (Keishin 26.) "*Sees the hit before the hitter.* What's that about?" "I see things early. It's IQ." "You heard that?" "You said it to Takeda-sensei. In the gym. Before exams." (ch 30) "Annoying old man." Sho practiced ruling out on traffic (blue car, delivery van); slow; a little ache like after Classical homework; "the most fun he'd had all week." A taxi U-turned where nothing could turn: "Taxi."
- **8:20 at the gate, roof light:** under *So keep it. Then what?*: *Look at what can't happen. Stand in what's left. / Slow for a month. (He said maybe 2.) / Saitama: 16 days.* (Takeda pinned the camp letter to the club room door.) Ukai read it upside down, grunted, turned the light off.
- Next: ch 36 "Saitama" (Sho): the doctor's check-up before leaving ("adapted, like any muscle"); midnight bus to Shinzen; the week-long camp; begs Yaku for reps; Reception D to C (status card); serve targeting on Ubugawa; the eye holds 2 rallies without soreness for the 1st time.

## Chapter 36: Saitama

For 16 days Sho crossed things out.

He did it in the receive line, where it was easiest, because a server only has so many choices. Tanaka's toss went up a little behind his head: so it couldn't be short. Ennoshita stood square: so it wasn't going to the seam on the left. Asahi, who'd started jump serving that week on his own, 20 a day at the end of practice, with his jaw set and most of them going into the net or the ceiling, threw the ball up so high and so far in front of himself that half of the court went dark before he'd even left the ground. Wherever an Asahi jump serve went, it wasn't going to be soft.

Sho stood in what was left. It was slow. It stayed slow through the 1st week, and then 1 evening in the 2nd week it was a little less slow, and he couldn't have said which rep it changed on.

Yachi filmed it. She stood on the bench in the corner with her elbows tucked in, every evening, and afterward they sat on the concrete step by the side door with the torch and counted. His heels still came down. They came down every time; Ikkei had said they would. But they came down in a wider place now, a place that was right for 2 balls, sometimes 3, and on Yachi's little screen it looked like somebody stepping into a doorway.

Hinata and Kageyama still weren't speaking. Hinata went somewhere with the shop van twice more. Kageyama stayed late every night and set balls to an empty court, alone, high and then low and then high again, with his face screwed up, as if he was trying to stop the ball in the air with his hands and couldn't work out how. Nobody asked him what he was doing. Sho kept out of it, and kept crossing things out.

Mio's notebook grew. He saw it on the kitchen table some mornings, open, with new lines in it in her forward-leaning pencil. *6. Says "it's fine" in his normal voice when it IS fine. (Check.)* *7. Talks to the cat next door.* He didn't know which was more embarrassing. He didn't ask her to stop.

---

The clinic was the same one as in June, up the stairs above the pharmacy, with the same television in the corner of the waiting room showing high school baseball with the sound turned down.

Mom took the afternoon off. She sat beside Sho on the vinyl bench in her office clothes with her ID still on its lanyard and a magazine she didn't open. Mio had wanted to come and had been told she couldn't, and had made Sho promise, at breakfast, to ask the doctor 2 questions she'd written on a sticky note and stuck to the back of his hand.

He read the sticky note while they waited. *1. Is it better. 2. How much better (number).*

"Kiyose Sho-kun."

The doctor was the same woman as in June, small and quick, with her reading glasses pushed up into her hair. She did the chart, which he read to the bottom line without trying. She did the pen, bringing it in toward his nose, and his eyes followed it in together, both of them, all the way, until it nearly touched him. She did the card with the fly on it, and the fly stood up off the page the way it had in June, and she didn't need to ask him whether it had.

Then she did a test he hadn't had before, with a small machine and lights that came on and went off at the edges while he kept his eyes on a dot in the middle and clicked a button for every one he saw. He found himself doing what Ikkei had said without meaning to: looking at the middle and letting the edges come. He clicked a lot.

"Full fields," said the doctor, reading the printout. "Nothing missing. That's the 1 I wanted, after June." She put it down and picked up the other sheet, the one with the June numbers on it, and laid them side by side on the desk. "And this."

She turned them round so Mom could see. Sho could see too. The June column was the one with the circles round it.

"The muscles that turn his eyes in," said the doctor. "In June they'd been worked too hard, too fast, and they gave out. Since then he's used them every day, a little at a time, the way I told him. And they've done what muscles do." She tapped the new column. "They've adapted. Like any muscle you train."

Mom looked at the 2 columns, the circled one and the new one. Her thumb moved once along the edge of the magazine.

"So he can play," she said. "At the camp. All week."

"He can play. Same rule: if they hurt, he stops." The doctor looked at Sho over the top of her glasses. "You can have 2 hours of screens now. Still not before bed."

"Thank you very much," said Sho. Then, because he'd promised, he held up the back of his hand with the sticky note on it. "My sister asked me to ask. Is it better? And how much better, as a number?"

The doctor read the sticky note. Her mouth twitched. She looked at the 2 columns.

"Tell her yes," she said. "And tell her: if June was 100, today's about 120."

Sho wrote *120* on the sticky note with the doctor's pen, under Mio's questions, and stuck it back on his hand.

In the stairwell going down, Mom walked beside him with her bag over her shoulder, her eyes on the steps. On the last flight, she reached over and took hold of his sleeve, the cuff of it, between her finger and thumb, the way she'd held it when he was 4 and they crossed roads, and held it to the bottom. Then she let go and walked out ahead of him to the vending machine by the pharmacy door, and bought a can of cold green tea, and drank half of it standing on the pavement, and gave him the rest.

---

The bus for Saitama left the school gate at midnight.

Dad walked him there, carrying the bag. Mio came too, in her pajamas with a cardigan over them and her school shoes on with no socks, holding Dad's other hand, because she'd said that if she wasn't allowed to come she'd stay awake anyway and then she'd be tired *and* angry, and Mom, who'd seen her do both at once, had let her.

The street was hot and black and full of cicadas even at that hour. The bus was waiting under the lamp post that worked, engine running, lights on inside, and half the team was already standing round it in tracksuits, yawning. Hinata was there, bouncing. Kageyama was there, 10 meters from Hinata, not bouncing.

Mio let go of Dad's hand when they got to the bus. She stood in front of Sho in the light from the bus door and looked at him: knees, then arms. She did it right there in front of him, under the lamp post, with half the volleyball club watching.

Then she patted her cardigan pocket, where the sticky note was.

"9:30," she said.

"9:30."

She nodded, once, the way Daichi nodded at the end of a team talk. Then she turned round and took Dad's hand again and they walked off up the street, a tall man in shirtsleeves and a small girl in pajamas and a cardigan, and she didn't turn round, and Sho stood by the bus door and watched them go until they turned the corner by the shrine.

"Your sister's scary," said Tanaka, beside him, with deep respect.

---

Shinzen High School was in the hills west of the city, and the bus came up to it at 5:40 in the morning, in a white mist that was already turning warm. There were trees everywhere, and behind the school a road went straight up a hill with no bends in it at all. Tanaka looked at it through the window and said, "What's that for?" and Ennoshita said, "Don't ask," and Tanaka said, "Why not?" and Ennoshita said, "Because they'll tell you."

Kuroo and Kenma were at the school gate when the bus pulled in.

Kuroo was leaning on the gatepost with his hands in his pockets and his hair worse than ever in the damp, grinning. Kenma was beside him with his console. They'd come out to meet the bus, which was a thing Kuroo would do and Kenma would only do if Kuroo made him.

"Karasuno!" said Kuroo. "Welcome to Saitama. It's very hot and there's a hill." His eyes went along them as they came off the bus with their bags, Daichi, Suga, Asahi, and found Sho. "Notebook."

"Good morning, Kuroo-san."

"Still writing?"

"Every day."

"Good. I'd hate to be left out." He jerked his head at the road up the hill. "That's for losing, by the way. Every game you lose, up and back. Welcome."

Kenma looked up from his console. His eyes went to Sho's face, and then down, the way they always did, to Sho's feet.

Sho was standing on the tarmac by the bus with his bag on his shoulder, his weight easy, his heels where they always were.

Kenma looked at them. Then back at his console.

"Hello," said Kenma.

---

Ukai gave his camp talk in the gym at 8, with every team at the camp warming up around them.

"Look round," he said. "Go on. Look."

They looked. Fukurodani's ace was hitting balls so hard they bounced into the stands. Nekoma's libero was passing jump serves as if they were thrown underarm. Ubugawa's whole team was serving, 1 after another, every 1 of them going up into the air.

"Every team in this gym does something better than we do," said Ukai. "Not 1 thing. Lots of things. Serves, blocks, receive, the way they talk on court. You've got a week." He folded his arms. "Eat it. All of it. Every trick, every habit, every technique you see, eat it and take it home. I don't care if it's from Nekoma or Fukurodani or the 3rd-string kid on Ubugawa who can only do 1 thing. You're omnivores this week. Got it?"

"YES!"

After that, while the others went to warm up, he caught Sho at the side of the court.

"Same rule," he said. "Hand up if it burns, and you come off."

"It hasn't burned since Tokyo, Coach."

"Then it won't cost you anything to keep the rule." Ukai looked at him. "Doctor?"

"120," said Sho. "If June was 100."

Ukai's eyebrows went up. "Who's measuring?"

"My sister."

Ukai snorted, and went to shout at Noya.

---

The 1st day was games from 8:30 to 6, single sets to 25, 1 after another, and Karasuno lost all of them.

Half the team was trying something new, and nothing new was working yet. Hinata jumped with his eyes open. Kageyama tried to set a ball that stopped at the top, and it kept not stopping. Asahi served 1 jump serve per rotation, and 2 out of 3 went out. Ukai let all of it happen, standing at the side with his arms folded, and every time they lost, they went up the hill.

It was 300 meters long and as steep as a staircase. You ran up it, and walked down. By the 4th time Tanaka had stopped yelling on the way up, which nobody had ever seen.

Sho played in pieces: a rotation in the back row for Asahi, against Fukurodani, in the middle of the afternoon.

At 14-19 Bokuto came in from the left, big and loud, and Sho did what Ikkei had told him. He looked at the shoulder, turned toward the cross. He looked at the hips, open. He crossed out the line: Bokuto's shoulder was wrong for it, he'd have to turn his whole body at the top, and nobody could do that. He crossed out the tip: the arm was all the way back. He stood in what was left, the cross, short and deep. His heels came down.

At the top of his jump, with the ball already in front of him, Bokuto turned.

He turned his whole body in the air, the way nobody could, from the hips, and his arm came through on a completely different line, and he hit it straight down the line, hard, into the back right corner of Karasuno's court, into the exact place Sho had crossed out 1 second before.

It hit the floor 3 meters from Sho. He hadn't moved.

"HEY HEY HEY!" said Bokuto, landing.

Sho stood in his strip, in what was left, and looked at the ball rolling away into the wall. *Good ones do things that can't happen,* Ikkei had said. *Sometimes.*

Bokuto did it twice more before the rotation came round. Sho crossed him out right both times, by every rule there was, and both times Bokuto decided in the air to be somebody else.

They lost 25-17. Sho went up the hill 6 times that day. On the 3rd he laughed, halfway up, out of nowhere, and nobody noticed, because everybody was too busy trying not to be sick.

---

At dinner on the 1st night Lev stood up at Nekoma's table with a bowl of rice in his hand and announced to the whole hall, in a voice of tremendous injury, "Yaku-san says I have to be in the GYM at 6 o'clock TOMORROW. In the MORNING. To RECEIVE."

"Sit down, Lev," said Yaku, without raising his voice, and Lev sat down.

Sho set his alarm for 5:30.

The gym was empty at 6 except for 2 people. Yaku was standing at the service line with a basket of balls, in a T-shirt and shorts, very small and very awake. Lev was in the middle of the far court, 194 centimeters of him, with his arms out in front of him and his face full of suffering.

"Again," said Yaku, and served, a sharp topspin serve straight at Lev's chest.

Lev got it on his face, sort of, and his arms, partly, and it went off into the stage.

"Arms, Lev! Arms! Your face isn't a platform!"

"It's 6 in the morning, Yaku-san!"

"The ball doesn't know what time it is!"

Sho stood in the doorway and watched 4 more serves. Yaku served the way he did everything, with no wasted motion at all, and every serve went exactly where he wanted it, at Lev's chest, at Lev's left hip, short in front of Lev's feet, and every time, before Lev moved, Yaku was already shouting the correction.

Sho walked across the gym and stopped beside the basket and bowed.

"Good morning, Yaku-san. Please bully me too. The same as Lev." He straightened up. "I'll be quieter than him."

Yaku looked up at him. A long way up. Yaku's eyes narrowed in a way that Sho, who'd watched Yaku play in May and in Tokyo, recognized as the face of a man deciding whether somebody was making fun of his height.

"Why?" said Yaku.

"Because you're the best receiver at this camp," said Sho. "And I'm not very good."

Yaku went on looking at him, checking. Then his shoulders came down a little.

"You're Karasuno's 13," he said. "The one who goes still."

"Yes."

"Nekoma's whole team knows about the still thing. Kenma told Kuroo on the train, and Kuroo told everybody." Yaku picked up a ball. "Get on the court. Next to Lev. Not too close, he flails."

"I do not FLAIL!"

Sho went and stood on the far court, 3 meters to Lev's left. Lev looked down at him with interest.

"You came here at 6 in the morning," said Lev. "On purpose."

"Yes."

"For THIS?"

"Yes."

"Are you sure you're a 1st year?" said Lev. "You play like an old man."

Sho thought about it.

"Thank you," he said, and Yaku served at him.

---

Yaku had a test. He told them what it was after the 1st 20 serves, when he'd seen enough to be disappointed in both of them.

"10 serves," he said. "In a row. Each 1 passed to within 1 step of the setter's spot." He pointed at a strip of white tape he'd stuck on the floor by the net, a little right of center. "If the pass lands more than 1 step from the tape, it doesn't count, and you start again from 1. Lev's record is 2."

"It's 3!"

"The 3rd 1 hit the tape and bounced off your knee. It's 2." Yaku looked at Sho. "Go."

Sho went. Yaku served the 1st at his right hip, topspin, and Sho saw it, the toss, the shoulder, the line of the arm, and crossed out short and crossed out left and his heels came down and he went, and passed it, and it landed on the tape.

"1," said Yaku.

The 2nd landed half a step to the left. "2."

The 3rd he saw, and crossed out, and stopped, and went, and the ball was already on him, and it came off his wrists and went into the stage after Lev's.

"Back to 1," said Yaku.

He did it 6 more times that morning. His best was 3 in a row.

At 6:50 Yaku put the last ball in the basket and walked across the court to Sho, and stood in front of him, and looked up.

"You see it fine," said Yaku. "You see it before I've hit it, half the time. I've been watching your eyes. You know where it's going." He pointed down. "Your feet are late."

Sho looked down at his feet.

"You look," said Yaku, "and then you stop where you're standing, and then you go. Everybody stops. Liberos stop. But we stop *there*." He pointed at a spot on the floor a step and a half from Sho's right foot. "Where it's going to land. You go while you're still looking. Feet first. You stop when you get there. And then, when you're there, you look at it again. Once. A long look. All the way onto your arms."

"Stop where it lands," said Sho slowly. "Then look at it again."

"That's what I said." Yaku turned back to the basket. "Same time tomorrow."

Sho stayed on the empty court after they'd gone, with his feet flat on the floor where Yaku had pointed at them.

*You see it fine. Your feet are late.*

He'd written that, in the other life. *Good reader. Feet late.* About 100 receivers. He'd paused them on tape and drawn the red arrow from where their feet were to where their feet should have been, and thought, every time, a little impatiently: *just move your feet.* Then he'd drawn the arrow and left it there.

He went to breakfast and ate 3 bowls of rice, and thought about feet.

---

That afternoon Karasuno played Ubugawa.

Ubugawa were the jump serve school. You could tell from the sound of their warm-up, the long run-ups, the crack of the ball off the hand, the way every 1 of their players, even the small ones, went up into the air to serve. Their captain said it at lunch, loudly, like a threat, to Tanaka, who'd asked: 100 serves each after practice. Every day.

Sho watched their warm-up from the bench with his eyes half shut.

He couldn't hear everything in Shinzen's gym. It was big and loud, like Fukurodani's, with 2 courts going at once. But he'd learned in Tokyo how to pick out 1 sound if he sat still and wanted it enough. Ubugawa's passers, taking each other's jump serves in warm-up, all made the same sound when the ball came off their arms: a short flat *tok*, clean, the sound of a platform that knew what it was doing.

All of them except 1.

Their #9 was a 2nd year, by his face, a little stocky. When the ball came off his arms it made a thicker sound, a *thud*, with something soft in it, as if it had hit him a fraction too far down on the wrists, a fraction late. About 1 in 3. The other 5 passers never made that sound at all.

Sho opened his eyes and watched #9. His arms looked fine. His feet looked fine. With your eyes alone you'd never have picked him out.

He wrote, on the bench, in pencil: *U #9. Sound wrong. 1 in 3.*

Ukai sent him in at 14-17, for Tanaka, who'd come back from the morning's hill with his legs shaking. The rotation had just brought Tanaka's spot round to serve.

Sho walked to the end line with the ball in his hands. His own float serve wasn't much: flat, no spin, a little slow, nothing like Yamaguchi's, which wobbled in the air like a leaf and was getting better every week. Sho's went where he pointed it about half the time. The other half it went into the net or a meter long.

He bounced the ball twice. He looked at #9, in the middle of Ubugawa's back row, standing easy, waiting.

He tossed it and hit it at #9's left hip.

It went over the net flat and slow, a ball a good passer would have eaten for breakfast, and #9 put his arms out and the ball came off his wrists with the soft wrong *thud* and went sideways, low and fast, straight into the bench.

Ace. 15-17.

The Karasuno bench went up. Hinata yelled. Tanaka yelled, from the bench, louder than Hinata. Sho walked back to the end line to serve again and caught Ukai's eye on the way. Ukai was looking at him with his arms folded and his head on 1 side.

Sho served at #9 again. It went a meter long. 15-18.

Ubugawa served, and Sho found out what it was like to be #9.

They'd been watching too. Ubugawa's server, a 3rd year with a huge jump, looked straight at Sho before he tossed: the new boy, the sub, the 1 who'd just come on. The serve came at Sho like something fired out of a pipe. He saw it, and crossed out, and his heels came down where he was standing, and he went, a step late, Yaku's step, and the ball came off his arms and went straight into the net.

15-19.

The next serve came at him too. He went earlier, feet first, before he'd finished looking, and got it up, but high and off the net, and Kageyama had to chase it, and Asahi hit a ball he didn't like into the block. 15-20.

"Kiyose!" said Noya, beside him, low and fierce. "Feet!"

"I know."

The 3rd serve came at him as well. He moved while he was still looking. He stopped where it was going to land. He looked at it again, all the way onto his arms. It went up, not to the tape, not to anywhere Yaku would have counted, but up, to Kageyama, who set the middle, and Hinata, eyes open, hit it off the block and out.

16-20. Karasuno's ball. Sho let out a breath he'd been holding since the ace.

---

It happened 4 rallies later, with Sho in the middle of the back row.

Ubugawa's ace was their #1, a 3rd year with a long, clean, loose arm, who'd been hitting cross into the deep corner all game. Sho had watched him from the bench, and on the court, and in warm-up, and crossed him out in his head so many times that the deep corner had a kind of shape to it now, a shape he could almost see when #1 started his approach.

At 19-22, #1 came in from the left. Sho crossed out the line, the shoulder was wrong for it, and the tip, the arm was all the way back. He stood in the strip that was left. His heels came down.

And the gym went quiet.

It came the way it always came, without asking. The noise of both courts went far away, and #1 hung over the net in points of light, shoulder and elbow and wrist and hip, and the brightest of them was his hand. The ghost peeled off it a blink early and swung across, cross, and from that long loose arm and that height Sho knew it would come down short of the deep corner, just inside the strip he was standing in.

He moved 1 step. The ball came onto his arms and went up, high and soft, to the middle, and Kageyama set Asahi on the right, and Asahi hit it into the floor. 20-22.

The noise came back. Sho stood in his spot and waited for what always came after, the warm sting behind his eyes, the dull hot weight of having looked at something too bright. It didn't come.

Karasuno served. Ubugawa passed it, and set #1 again, from the left.

And the ghost was still there.

It hadn't gone. It had been there all through the serve and the pass and the set, faint, sitting over #1 like something left on a screen after you've turned it off, without Sho doing anything to keep it. When #1 went up, it peeled off his hand again, a blink early, and swung down the line this time, the line Sho hadn't crossed out because #1 had turned his shoulder for it, and Sho was already moving, 2 steps to the right, and down.

He dug it. It went up wild, toward the bench, and Noya ran it down and scooped it back over, and Ubugawa put it away. 20-23.

Sho got up off the floor and stood in his spot and waited again.

Nothing. His eyes felt the way his legs felt after a good warm-up: used, and warm, and ready for more. The ghost had faded off #1 now, the way it always faded in the end, but it had held for 2 whole rallies, on its own, and there was nothing behind his eyes at all.

Ubugawa won it 25-21. The rotation came round and Tanaka came back on for the last 2 points; Sho went to the bench, and Ukai, with his eyes still on the game, said, "Who told you to serve at 9?"

"His platform sounds wrong in warm-up, Coach."

Ukai turned his head.

"Sounds," he said.

"The ball sounds dead off his arms. Some of the time. The others don't."

Ukai looked at him, and then back across the court, at #9, who was shaking his arms out at his sides as if they'd been stung.

"Hm," said Ukai. "Keep doing it. And get your feet sorted. They found you in 2 serves."

Karasuno went up the hill. Halfway up, with his lungs on fire and his legs full of sand, Sho noticed he was smiling, with no reason he could have given Tanaka, who was beside him, gasping, and looking at him as if he'd gone mad.

---

At 9:30 he called home from the bench outside the dining hall, under a light with moths round it, the same as Tokyo.

"Who'd you play," said Mio.

He told her. 5 games, 5 lost. 5 times up the hill. He told her about the hill, and Yaku, and Lev, and what Lev had said about old men, and she laughed so hard she had to put the phone down.

"Okay," she said, when she'd picked it up. "Eyes."

"Once. Against Ubugawa." He looked at the moths. "It stayed for 2 rallies. On its own. It's never done 2 on its own."

"Where did it hurt?"

"Nowhere," said Sho.

The line went quiet.

"Nowhere?"

"Nowhere. Not after the 1st 1, and not after the 2nd."

He heard her pencil. It went on longer than *nowhere* needed.

"What did you write?"

"Eyes: 2 rallies," Mio read out. "Hurt: 0." A pause. "And I drew a circle round the 0. A big circle."

---

On the 3rd morning, at 6:05, in the empty gym, with Lev groaning 3 meters to his right and the mist still white in the high windows, Yaku served his 1st ball of the day at Sho's right hip.

Sho went before he'd finished looking. He'd been doing it in his head since the 1st morning, at every water break, in the bath, on the hill: feet first. Go while you're still looking. Stop where it's going to land. Then look at it again, once, all the way onto your arms.

His heels came down when he was already there.

The ball landed on the tape. "1," said Yaku.

Short, in front of his feet. He was moving before Yaku's hand finished the swing. Tape. "2."

Left hip, hard. "3."

At 6 he stopped hearing Yaku count. At 8 Lev stopped groaning and started watching. At 9 Yaku served the hardest 1 he'd served all week, a flat, mean, fast serve straight at Sho's chest, the kind that made you flinch, and Sho's feet took him half a step back and to the side before the ball was over the net, and he looked at it all the way in, the long still look, and it came off his arms soft and dead and dropped onto the tape.

"9," said Yaku.

The 10th went at his left, high, at the seam. He was there. He looked. The ball came down onto his platform, and went up, and fell on the tape, and rolled off it, half a step, and stopped.

Within 1 step.

Yaku stood at the service line with his hand on the basket.

"10," he said.

Lev said, "WHAT? In a ROW?"

Yaku picked up another ball and looked at it, and then at Sho. His face didn't do much. It was a libero's face; it gave nothing away it didn't have to. He kept looking at Sho, and then he nodded, once.

"Again tomorrow," said Yaku. "Or it was luck."

---

That night, after the bath, the old gym on the far side of the school was lit up.

It was the 3rd gymnasium, at the edge of the campus, by the trees. Kuroo had mentioned it at the gate on the 1st morning, the way he mentioned everything, like bait: Fukurodani's captain practiced there every night after dinner until somebody dragged him out. You could hear it from the corridor, the long roaring approach, the crack of the hand on the ball, and a voice, enormous, delighted, shouting into the dark.

Sho was coming back from the bathhouse with his towel round his neck when the side door of the 3rd gym banged open and a figure filled it, backlit, hair sticking up in 2 gray and black points like horns.

"HEY!" shouted Bokuto, across 30 meters of dark grass. "HEY! YOU! 13! Karasuno's 13! The 1 who got my best 1 in Tokyo!"

Sho stopped.

"Come here! Come here! Akaashi, it's him! The digger!" Bokuto was waving both arms over his head as if he was flagging down a plane. "We need a digger! Kuroo's useless at it and your tall blond 1 won't stay! Come and dig! You stood in the wrong place 3 times on the 1st day, I saw! I'll give you my best 1 again! I'll give you 50 of them!"

Behind Bokuto, in the light of the doorway, a calm dark head appeared at his shoulder. Akaashi looked out across the grass at Sho with the same short plain look as in Tokyo.

"Bokuto-san," said Akaashi. "He's just had a bath."

"He can have another 1!"

Sho stood on the path in the dark with his towel round his neck. He looked at the lit doorway, at Bokuto's arm, already swinging, already impatient, the arm that had turned in the air and done the thing that couldn't happen, 3 times, and left him standing in what was left.

He went.

```
[Kiyose Sho]
Age: 15 | Height: 182cm | Karasuno #13
Power: C   Jump: B   Speed: C   Stamina: B
Reception: C (from D)   Serve: D   Block: E   Set: E
Mentality: B   Shamelessness: S
Emperor's Eye: Partial awakening
Meta Vision: Scanning
```

**Continuity (ch 36)**

- Sho POV. The 16 days before camp, the check-up, the midnight bus, Shinzen days 1 to 3. **Status card: Reception D to C (Yaku's 10).** Emperor's Eye stays *Partial awakening* on the card (the bible's "Adapting" stage is prose-only; the card jumps to Conditioned at ch 45). Meta Vision still Scanning on the card.
- **16 days:** ruling out in the receive line (Tanaka's toss, Ennoshita square, Asahi's jump serve: Asahi started it on his own that week, 20 a day, mostly net or ceiling; his). Slow, then a little less slow in the 2nd week. Yachi films every evening; heels still come down, "in a wider place now... right for 2 balls, sometimes 3"; on tape "like somebody stepping into a doorway." Hinata went with the shop van twice more; **Kageyama stays late setting to an empty court, trying to stop the ball at the top (canon stopping toss; his)**. Mio's notebook: *6. Says "it's fine" in his normal voice when it IS fine. (Check.)* *7. Talks to the cat next door.*
- **Clinic (same as June: up the stairs above the pharmacy, TV with high school baseball; same doctor, glasses in her hair):** Mom took the afternoon off. Mio's sticky note on his hand: *1. Is it better. 2. How much better (number).* Chart to the bottom; pen all the way in; the fly stood up; visual field test ("Full fields. Nothing missing. That's the 1 I wanted, after June."). June numbers vs today side by side. **"They've adapted. Like any muscle you train."** Same rule (hurts = stop); **screens now 2 hours, still not before bed.** "If June was 100, today's about 120." In the stairwell Mom held his cuff (as when he was 4); cold green tea, half each.
- **Midnight bus:** Dad walked (bag; shirtsleeves); Mio in pajamas and cardigan, school shoes, no socks. **Mio did the door check (knees, then arms) in front of him and the team.** Patted the sticky note in her pocket. "9:30." "9:30." Didn't turn round. Tanaka: "Your sister's scary."
- **Shinzen (hills west of the city; penalty hill: 300 m, steep as a staircase, up once and walk down per lost game):** arrived 5:40, warm mist. Tanaka/Ennoshita: "What's that for?" "Don't ask... Because they'll tell you." **Kuroo and Kenma met the bus (canon).** Kuroo: "Notebook... Still writing?" "Every day." "Good. I'd hate to be left out... That's for losing." Kenma looked at Sho's feet: "Hello."
- **Ukai's omnivores talk (canon theme; wording invented):** "Look round... Every team in this gym does something better than we do... Eat it. All of it... You're omnivores this week." Rule: "Hand up if it burns, and you come off." "It hasn't burned since Tokyo." "Then it won't cost you anything to keep the rule." "120... If June was 100." "Who's measuring?" "My sister."
- **Day 1: lost every game** (Hinata eyes open, Kageyama's set won't stop, Asahi 1 jump serve per rotation, 2 of 3 out); up the hill 6 times; Tanaka stopped yelling by the 4th; Sho laughed on the 3rd. **Vs Fukurodani at 14-19: Sho ruled out Bokuto's line correctly; Bokuto turned his whole body in the air and hit line into the crossed-out corner (3 m from Sho, no move). 3 times in the rotation.** ("Good ones do things that can't happen. Sometimes.") Lost 17-25.
- **Day 1 dinner:** Lev: "Yaku-san says I have to be in the GYM at 6 o'clock TOMORROW." Yaku: "Sit down, Lev."
- **Day 2, 6 a.m.:** "Please bully me too. The same as Lev. I'll be quieter than him." "Why?" "Because you're the best receiver at this camp. And I'm not very good." **Yaku: "You're Karasuno's 13. The one who goes still... Kenma told Kuroo on the train, and Kuroo told everybody."** **Lev: "Are you sure you're a 1st year? You play like an old man." "Thank you."** (Lev's line, used once.)
- **Yaku's 10** (tape a little right of center; a miss = back to 1; Lev's record 2, "It's 3!"). Day 2 best 3. **Yaku: "You see it fine... Your feet are late... Everybody stops. Liberos stop. But we stop *there*. Where it's going to land. You go while you're still looking. Feet first. You stop when you get there... look at it again. Once. A long look."** (Squares with Ikkei: the stop is fine; where you stop matters.) Satoshi's reports: *Good reader. Feet late.* about 100 receivers; drew the arrow and left it there.
- **Ubugawa, day 2 afternoon (canon: jump-serve school; captain: 100 serves each a day):** Sho heard #9 (2nd year, stocky) "thud" about 1 in 3. **In at 14-17 for Tanaka (legs gone from the hill), serving on entry (Sogeking debut): ace off #9 (15-17); 2nd serve a meter long (15-18).** **Counter: Ubugawa served the new sub: shank into the net (15-19), high off the net (15-20; Noya: "Feet!"), then a pass to Kageyama (16-20).** **The eye: 19-22, #1 (3rd year ace) cross; Sho ruled out line and tip; the ghost swung cross and Sho read the depth from the arm; dug; Asahi scored 20-22; no sting. Karasuno served; Ubugawa set #1 again; the ghost was still there ("like something left on a screen after you've turned it off"); line; Sho dug it wild; Noya kept it; Ubugawa scored 20-23. No sting: "used, and warm, and ready for more."** Ubugawa won 25-21; Tanaka back for the last points. Ukai: "Who told you to serve at 9?" "His platform sounds wrong in warm-up." "Sounds." "Hm. Keep doing it. And get your feet sorted. They found you in 2 serves." Sho smiling up the hill.
- **Day 2 call (9:30, bench outside the dining hall, moths):** 5 games, 5 lost, 5 hills. Lev's old-man line made Mio laugh. **"It stayed for 2 rallies. On its own. It's never done 2 on its own."** (ch 22's forced 11 rallies were forced.) "Where did it hurt?" **"Nowhere."** Mio: *Eyes: 2 rallies. Hurt: 0.* (a big circle round the 0).
- **Day 3, 6:05: Yaku's 10 complete** (feet first; heels down when already there; the 9th the hardest serve of the week at his chest; the 10th rolled half a step off the tape). Lev: "WHAT? In a ROW?" **Yaku: "Again tomorrow. Or it was luck."**
- **Night 3, the 3rd gym (canon venue; Kuroo mentioned it at the gate as bait):** Bokuto: "HEY! YOU! 13!... The 1 who got my best 1 in Tokyo!... We need a digger! Kuroo's useless at it and your tall blond 1 won't stay!... You stood in the wrong place 3 times on the 1st day, I saw!... I'll give you 50 of them!" Akaashi: "Bokuto-san. He's just had a bath." "He can have another 1!" **Sho went.**
- Bible note: Kagemane (Kuroo's "stand in my shadow") now debuts in ch 37.
- Next: ch 37 "Moonrise" (Sho): nights 3 to 5 in the 3rd gym; Akaashi sets, Bokuto hits, Kuroo blocks; **Sho invites the eye for the 1st time** (on Bokuto) and it comes; digs Bokuto 1, then 3, then 6 a night; Kuroo: "Stand in my shadow." The moonrise night (Tsukishima/Yamaguchi, Tsukishima's question, Bokuto's "the moment") stays theirs; Sho leaves before it.

## Chapter 37: Moonrise

The 3rd gymnasium was the oldest building at Shinzen. It had a wooden floor gone gray at the edges and gold in the middle where 100,000 shoes had polished it, and windows that didn't close properly, and a stage at 1 end with a dusty red curtain nobody had drawn in years. The lights hung on long wires from the roof, and moths went round every 1 of them. Through the open windows came the cicadas, and the smell of cut grass, and the dark.

There were 4 people in it when Sho came in with his towel still round his neck.

Bokuto, already at the net, bouncing. Akaashi at the ball basket, sorting the soft balls to 1 side. Kuroo at the net on the other side, rolling his shoulders, with Lev beside him looking enormous and doubtful. Behind Kuroo and Lev, nobody. The whole back court on their side was empty: 9 meters by 9 of gray-gold wood, with the lights swinging slightly overhead.

"Notebook!" said Kuroo. "You came. He's been begging for a digger for 3 days."

"HEY HEY HEY!" Bokuto came round the end of the net at a jog and stopped in front of Sho, close, beaming. Close up he was barely taller than Sho, and about 3 times as loud. "13! You came! What's your name?"

"Kiyose. Kiyose Sho."

"Kiyose! You got my best 1, in Tokyo. My best cross." He held up 1 finger. "Nobody gets that 1."

"It was line, Bokuto-san," said Akaashi, from the basket, still sorting. "The 1 he got."

Bokuto thought about this. "My best line," he said. "Nobody gets that 1 either." He pointed at the empty back court. "Go and stand there. I'm going to hit everything at you."

---

Akaashi made the rule plain before the 1st ball, in the same voice he used for everything. A dig counted if it went up to the setter's spot on Sho's own side of the net, high enough that somebody could have set it. Anything else was a touch. Kuroo, coming down from the block, would catch the good ones and roll them back under the net.

Bokuto didn't count digs. Bokuto counted, out loud, with tremendous satisfaction, every ball that hit the floor.

The 1st hit the floor before Sho had moved.

The 2nd hit him on the shoulder and went into the stage.

The 3rd, he crossed out the line, and Bokuto turned in the air, the way he had on the 1st day, and hit the line anyway, and it went past Sho's right hand so close he felt the air of it.

"3!" said Bokuto. "HEY! 4!"

"You're counting the wrong thing, Bokuto-san," said Akaashi.

"I'm counting the GOOD thing!"

By 20 Sho had touched 4 and dug none. His forearms were already red. Bokuto's ball was heavier than anybody's he'd stood in front of in this life, heavier than Iwaizumi's, heavier than anything Satoshi had dug at 19. It arrived all at once, with the weight of a whole body behind it.

He tried Ikkei's way, crossing out, and it was too slow. He tried Yaku's way, feet first, and it was better, and still too slow. Bokuto decided in the air. That was the trouble with him. A spiker who decides in the air gives you nothing to cross out until he's chosen, and by then the ball is on the floor.

On the 38th ball, Sho crossed out nothing at all. He just went where Bokuto's shoulder pointed at the last instant, and threw his arms at it. It came off them and went up, high and wild, but up, near enough to the 3-meter line that Kuroo, landing, took 2 steps and caught it.

"1!" said Bokuto. He spun round to the whole empty gym. "AKAASHI! HE DUG 1!"

"I saw, Bokuto-san."

Sho was flat on his back on the gray-gold floor with his arms out, looking up at the lights on their long wires and the moths going round them, and laughing. It had come out of him the moment the ball went up.

"Why is he laughing?" said Lev.

"He's from Karasuno," said Kuroo, and rolled the ball under the net to Akaashi. But he was watching Sho over the tape with his head on 1 side, the way he'd watched him at the gate.

At 9:20, after the 50th ball, Sho got up and bowed to all of them and said thank you, and that he had to go.

"GO?" said Bokuto. "We've only done 50!"

"I'm sorry. I promised somebody. 9:30."

Bokuto looked at him, and then at the clock over the stage, and then back at him, with deep suspicion.

"Tomorrow," said Bokuto. "Same time. And you dig 2."

---

On the 4th morning, at 6:05, he passed Yaku's 10 again. It took him 2 tries. Yaku counted it out loud to the end, and said, "Hm," and turned round and served at Lev.

The games that day went badly, though less badly than the 1st day. Hinata and Kageyama still weren't speaking. Hinata still jumped with his eyes open, and still got up off the floor every time with his jaw clenched and went again. Kageyama still couldn't make the ball stop at the top. Sho played his pieces, a rotation here and a rotation there, and passed Ubugawa's servers, and some of his passes went to the setter now. When Ubugawa's #9 came on to serve, he looked at Sho first, and then away.

At 7:40 Sho went back to the 3rd gym.

---

It was Kuroo who saw it.

They'd been going about 20 minutes, and Sho had touched 6 and dug none. He was reading Bokuto with everything he had: approach, shoulder, hips, hand. He crossed out what he could and stood in what was left, and Bokuto kept turning into somebody else at the top.

After the 23rd ball Kuroo came down off his jump and didn't go back to his spot. He ducked under the net and walked across the back court to Sho with his hands in his pockets.

"Notebook," he said. "Can I tell you something?"

"Please."

"You're reading Bokuto."

"Yes."

"Don't." Kuroo jerked his head back at the net. "Read me."

Sho looked at him.

"I'm the block," said Kuroo. "When Bokuto comes in from the left, I take his line away. I put my hands there." He held up his right hand, flat, angled. "Lev's supposed to take the middle, and he doesn't, because he's Lev, but he's getting there. So between the 2 of us there's a big piece of your court Bokuto can't hit. He can try. He'll hit my hands." He let his hand drop. "Behind my hands, that's dead. Nothing lands there. The edge of it, just off my outside hand, that's where the ball comes when he tries to go round me. Stand on the edge of my shadow. Let it come to you."

"So you're crossing things out," said Sho slowly. "For me."

Kuroo's eyebrows went up.

"Is that what it's called? Sounds clever." He grinned. "It's just blocking."

He went back under the net and up to his spot without waiting to see whether Sho understood.

Sho stood in the back court and looked at Kuroo's hands.

They were the best hands on the court. He'd written it in May, on the bench, in the notebook: *Kuroo's hands at the net, open early, steering instead of stopping.* He'd never once, in May, thought of standing behind them.

Akaashi set. Bokuto came in. Kuroo went up, and his hands went out over the net, angled to take the line, and behind them, on the floor, a piece of the court went dark. Kuroo had crossed it out for him.

Sho stood on the edge of it.

Bokuto hit at Kuroo's outside hand, trying to go round, and the ball came off the edge of the block, and Sho was standing in front of it, and it came onto his arms and went up in a soft arc to the setter's spot.

Kuroo landed, caught it, and rolled it under the net.

"1!" said Bokuto.

Kuroo looked over his shoulder at Sho, and grinned, and went back to his spot.

The 2nd came 10 balls later. Kuroo's hands. The edge of the shadow. Up.

"2!" said Bokuto. "AKAASHI! 2! He wants 50!"

"He doesn't want 50, Bokuto-san."

"EVERYBODY wants 50!"

The 3rd came on the very next ball, and Bokuto was still turning round to celebrate the 2nd when Kuroo caught it.

"3!"

---

And then Bokuto rushed.

He came in on the next set before Akaashi's hands had finished it, too early, and hit it into the net. Then he hit 1 a meter long. Then 1 into Lev's flailing hands, which surprised Lev more than anybody. And Bokuto came down from that 1 and stood at the net with his arms hanging, and his hair, which had been sticking up in 2 proud points all evening, seemed to sag.

Sho had seen this before, from the other side of a net in Tokyo: 3 rallies of sulking after a block.

"Don't set me," said Bokuto, to the floor. "I'm no good today."

Kuroo sighed and sat down on the floor where he was, like a man waiting for a train he knew would come eventually.

Akaashi picked up a ball from the basket and turned it over in his hands as if he were checking the seams. Then he walked to his spot under the net and stood there with it, and waited. He didn't call Bokuto. He stood there, quite still, until Bokuto turned his head and saw him standing there.

Then Akaashi tossed the ball up to himself, and set it.

Sho saw it from 9 meters away, because he knew how to read Akaashi's sets now. In Tokyo he'd worked it out: tight to the net meant Akaashi wanted cross; out toward the antenna meant line. This set was neither. It was a fraction higher than any all evening, and a fraction slower, and a fraction off the net, and it hung at the top in the place where Bokuto's hand would be if Bokuto went now. It didn't tell Bokuto where to hit. It only told him to come.

Bokuto's head came up. His feet moved before he'd decided anything, 1 step, 2, and then he was running, and he went up into that ball and hit it with everything, straight down into Kuroo's side of the court, so hard it bounced up and hit a light, and the light swung on its wire, and the moths scattered.

"HEY HEY HEY!" said Bokuto, and his hair was back up.

"I set it, Bokuto-san," said Akaashi, and picked up another ball.

Sho had been that, once. At 19, in a white shirt with a navy 5 on it, after a ball he should have killed. Somebody had set him the next 1 a fraction higher than usual. He'd never asked who decided that, or why.

Kuroo got up off the floor, dusting his shorts, and went back to the net.

At 9:20 Sho bowed and went to call his sister, and Bokuto shouted after him across the dark grass, "4! Tomorrow you dig 4!"

---

The 5th night was hot and still. The moths were thicker round the lights than ever, and somewhere beyond the trees a dog was barking at nothing.

At dinner Tsukishima wasn't at Karasuno's table. Yamaguchi was, in his usual place, with his chopsticks in his hand and his rice untouched, looking at the empty place beside him, and then at the door, and then at the empty place again.

Sho saw it. He ate his rice. At 7:40 he went to the 3rd gym.

---

He dug 2 in the 1st 15 minutes, both off the edge of Kuroo's shadow. Bokuto counted them at the top of his voice. Lev, who was beginning to understand what Kuroo was doing with his hands, put his own hands somewhere useful for once, and Kuroo said "There!" and Lev nearly fell over with pride, and the dead piece of the floor behind them grew a little wider.

Then, on the 16th ball, Bokuto went up, and Kuroo's hands went out, and Sho stood on the edge of the shadow, and Bokuto turned in the air.

Bokuto had learned too. He turned his whole body and hit the ball high off the very tips of Kuroo's fingers, so it went up and back, deep, over Sho's head, into the corner by the stage. Nobody's shadow covered that.

Sho ran it down and got a hand to it, flat out on the floor, and it went into the curtain.

He lay with his cheek on the floor, looking at the dusty red curtain with a volleyball-shaped dent in it, and breathing. Then he got up and went back.

After that there wasn't much for him to do. Lev's hands kept being useful. Kuroo's hands were always useful. Between them the court behind the net was mostly dark now, and what was left was small enough that Sho could stand at its edge and wait, with his weight easy and his breathing slow, and let the edges of the gym come to him the way Ikkei had said. The net, the tape, the space over Kuroo's hands. The moths. The swinging lights. It was quiet inside him in a way it hadn't been all week.

He'd asked before. In April, in May, on courts in Miyagi, he'd stood with his jaw clenched and begged it to come, and it never had. It came when it wanted.

Tonight he asked differently. He stood on the edge of Kuroo's shadow with nothing in particular riding on the answer, and thought, at nothing, *If you'd like to come, now would be a good time.* And if it didn't, he'd dig what he could, and that would be fine.

Akaashi set.

Bokuto came in from the left, the big slapping approach, and went up, and Kuroo and Lev went up with him, and Bokuto's arm went back.

The gym went quiet.

He felt it arrive the way you feel somebody sit down next to you on a bench.

Bokuto hung over the net in points of light. The ghost peeled off his hand, a blink early, and Sho saw the turn in it, the whole body twisting at the top, the wrist going high, aimed at the very tips of Kuroo's outside fingers. That was all the ghost showed. The rest Sho already knew. He'd lain on his face 10 minutes ago looking at the dent that ball had made in the curtain.

He was already going, 3 steps back and to the right, toward the stage. He got there with time to turn round and wait, and look up, and watch the ball come off Kuroo's fingertips exactly where it had come off before, and look at it all the way down onto his arms, the long still look, Yaku's look. It fell into his arms and he gave with it, soft, and it went up.

High. Slow. To the setter's spot.

Kuroo, coming down, didn't have to move. He caught it at chest height, and stood holding it, and turned round to look at Sho.

On the other side of the net, Akaashi had gone very still. Before Kuroo had rolled the ball back under the net, before Bokuto had started shouting, Akaashi was looking across the court at Sho, and he went on looking while Kuroo rolled the ball to his feet, and he picked it up without looking at it.

"3!" Bokuto roared. "THAT WAS MY BEST FINGERTIP 1! AKAASHI, HE GOT MY BEST FINGERTIP 1!"

The noise came back. Sho stood by the stage with the ball's warmth still on his forearms. His eyes felt clear and wide, the way the court looks in the morning before anybody has come in.

He didn't ask again. He dug 3 more that night off the edge of Kuroo's shadow, the ordinary way, with his feet and his eyes and somebody else's hands. 6 in all.

"6!" said Bokuto. "Tomorrow he'll get 10! The day after, 50!"

"Bokuto-san, the camp ends in 3 days."

"Then he'll have to dig fast!"

---

At 9:20 Sho bowed.

"No!" said Bokuto. "You can't go now! You're good now! 10 more!"

"9:30, Bokuto-san."

"Who is it, anyway?" said Kuroo, from the net. "Every night. Girlfriend?"

"My sister."

"How old?"

"10."

Kuroo laughed. "And you're scared of her."

"Yes," said Sho.

Kuroo stopped laughing, and looked at him, and nodded slowly. "Fair," he said. "Go on."

Akaashi, at the ball basket, straightened up and gave Sho a small bow, and said, "Thank you for the digs, Kiyose-kun," and Sho bowed back and went out of the side door into the dark.

---

The moon was coming up over the hill.

It was huge, low and orange, the way the moon only is when it's just come up behind something, and it was coming up over the penalty hill, so that the road going straight up seemed to go into it. The grass was silver on 1 side of the path and black on the other. Behind him, through the open windows, he could hear the crack of Bokuto's hand on the ball, and Bokuto's voice, and Kuroo laughing.

He'd gone about 30 meters along the path when he saw somebody coming the other way.

Tall. Thin. Hands in his pockets. Walking slowly, as if he hadn't quite decided to walk in this direction and was waiting to find out whether he had.

Tsukishima stopped when he saw Sho. They stood on the path, 3 meters apart, in the light of the moon.

Tsukishima's face was doing nothing. It always did nothing. His shoulders were a little high and his chin a little low, and Sho, who had watched people's shoulders for a living, made himself stop reading them.

"Are they still in there?" said Tsukishima. "Bokuto-san. And Kuroo-san."

"Yes."

Tsukishima looked past him at the lit doorway, at the light coming out of it onto the grass, and didn't move.

Sho stepped off the path onto the grass, to give him room, and went on.

When he'd gone another 20 meters he looked back, once.

Tsukishima was standing in the lit doorway of the 3rd gym, a long dark shape against the light, with his hands in his pockets. Then he went in.

Sho walked on toward the dining hall with the moon on his right.

---

"Who'd you play," said Mio.

"Bokuto-san. And Kuroo-san's hands."

"Whose hands?"

"Kuroo-san. The tall 1 with the bad hair. He blocks." He was on the bench outside the dining hall, with the moon a little higher now over the hill and going from orange to gold. "If you stand at the edge of where his hands are, the ball comes to you."

"That doesn't make sense."

"It does when you do it."

"How many?"

"6. Out of about 60."

"That's still bad."

"Still bad," he said. "Better."

"Eyes."

He looked at the moon.

"Once," he said. "And it was different."

"Different how?"

He thought about how to say it to a 10-year-old sitting on the stairs at home in her pajamas, with a pencil and a notebook with *SHO* on the cover, so that it would be true.

"It always just comes when it wants," he said. "I can't make it. I've tried. Tonight I asked. The way you'd ask somebody to come over. And it came."

The line went quiet.

"Did it hurt?"

"No."

He heard her pencil. Then it stopped.

"Then ask it again tomorrow," said Mio.

"It might not come."

"Then ask it nicely."

She hung up. He sat on the bench with the phone in his hand and the moon going gold over the hill.

A lighter clicked in the dark, near the vending machines. Ukai came along the path from the staff building with Takeda beside him, Takeda carrying a clipboard against his chest and talking quietly about tomorrow's games, Ukai with the cigarette lit for once and held low. They were nearly past the bench when Ukai's eyes went to Sho's forearms, purple to the elbow under the light.

He stopped.

"3rd gym?" said Ukai.

"Yes, Coach."

Ukai looked at the forearms a little longer. Then at Sho's face. Then he said to Takeda, without taking his eyes off Sho, "Tomorrow. 1st game. He starts. Whole set."

Takeda wrote it on the clipboard.

"Hm," said Ukai, to nobody, and walked on.

**Continuity (ch 37)**

- Sho POV, nights 3 to 5 of the Shinzen camp (day 4 in brief). The eye: **invited on night 5, on Bokuto, and it came; no cost.** (Bible "Conditioned": "he can invite it in calm moments" begins here, early and once.) No status card.
- **The 3rd gym (canon venue; details invented):** oldest building; gray-gold wooden floor; windows that don't close; stage with a dusty red curtain; lights on long wires; moths; cicadas. **Present nights 3 to 5: Bokuto, Akaashi, Kuroo, Lev** (Kuroo coaching Lev's blocking, canon). Tsukishima arrives only at the end of night 5. Hinata not there yet.
- **Rules (Akaashi):** a dig counts if it goes up to the setter's spot on Sho's own side, settable; anything else is a touch. **Kuroo, landing, catches good digs and rolls them under the net.** Bokuto counts the floor ones ("You're counting the wrong thing, Bokuto-san." "I'm counting the GOOD thing!").
- **Night 3:** "Kiyose. Kiyose Sho." Bokuto: "My best cross." Akaashi: "It was line, Bokuto-san." Bokuto barely taller than Sho (185 vs 182), 3 times as loud. Line passed Sho's right hand. 4 touches in 20. Bokuto decides in the air. **1st dig on ball 38** (went where the shoulder pointed at the last instant); "1!... AKAASHI! HE DUG 1!" Laughing on his back. Lev: "Why is he laughing?" Kuroo: "He's from Karasuno." **9:20 he leaves every night to call Mio at 9:30.** "Tomorrow... And you dig 2."
- **Day 4:** Yaku's 10 again in 2 tries ("Hm"). Games lost, less badly. Hinata eyes open; Kageyama's set won't stop (theirs). Ubugawa's #9 looks at Sho and away.
- **Night 4, Kuroo's shadow (Kagemane debut):** "You're reading Bokuto... Don't. Read me... I take his line away... Behind my hands, that's dead... The edge of it, just off my outside hand, that's where the ball comes when he tries to go round me. Stand on the edge of my shadow." "So you're crossing things out. For me." "Is that what it's called? Sounds clever. It's just blocking." (May notebook: *Kuroo's hands at the net, open early, steering instead of stopping.*) **Digs 1, 2, 3** off Kuroo's outside hand. "He wants 50!" "EVERYBODY wants 50!"
- **Bokuto's sulk (canon trait; this instance invented, short):** rushed 3 balls (net, long, into Lev's hands); "Don't set me. I'm no good today." Kuroo sat down to wait. **Akaashi waited with the ball until Bokuto looked, then set a ball that was neither tight (cross) nor out (line): higher, slower, off the net, hanging; "It didn't tell Bokuto where to hit. It only told him to come."** Bokuto killed it into a light. "I set it, Bokuto-san." Satoshi at 19 (white shirt, navy 5) had been set "the next 1 a fraction higher" after a ball he should have killed; never asked who decided.
- **Night 5 (moonrise night):** at dinner Tsukishima absent; Yamaguchi's rice untouched, looking at the empty place and the door (theirs). In the gym: 2 digs; Lev's hands useful ("There!"). Ball 16: Bokuto turned and hit high off Kuroo's fingertips, deep to the stage corner (dent in the curtain). Calm: little left to do; wide gaze. **He'd asked before (April, May) with his jaw clenched, never answered. Tonight: "*If you'd like to come, now would be a good time.*" It came "the way you feel somebody sit down next to you on a bench."** The ghost showed only Bokuto's turn and the high wrist at Kuroo's outside fingertips; **Sho's memory of ball 16 supplied the corner.** 3 steps, waited, Yaku's long look, soft give, **high and slow to the setter's spot; Kuroo caught it at chest height without moving.** Akaashi went still and kept looking at Sho. "THAT WAS MY BEST FINGERTIP 1!" Eyes "clear and wide." He didn't ask again. **6 digs in all.** "Bokuto-san, the camp ends in 3 days."
- **9:20:** "9:30, Bokuto-san." Kuroo: "Girlfriend?" "My sister." "How old?" "10." "And you're scared of her." "Yes." "Fair. Go on." Akaashi: "Thank you for the digs, Kiyose-kun."
- **Moonrise:** huge orange moon over the penalty hill. **Tsukishima on the path: "Are they still in there? Bokuto-san. And Kuroo-san." "Yes."** Sho stepped onto the grass; looked back once; Tsukishima went in. **The night inside is Tsukishima's (canon: his question, Bokuto's "the moment", Kuroo's blocking advice).**
- **Night 5 call:** "Bokuto-san. And Kuroo-san's hands." "If you stand at the edge of where his hands are, the ball comes to you." "That doesn't make sense." "It does when you do it." "6. Out of about 60." "Still bad. Better." **"Tonight I asked... And it came." "Did it hurt?" "No." "Then ask it again tomorrow." "It might not come." "Then ask it nicely."**
- **Ukai and Takeda pass (lighter, clipboard):** Ukai sees Sho's forearms purple to the elbow. "3rd gym?" "Yes, Coach." **"Tomorrow. 1st game. He starts. Whole set."** Takeda wrote it down. "Hm."
- Next: ch 38 "Hand up" (**surprise Ukai POV 2**): day 6 from the bench; Ukai watches Sho play a whole set and sees a 1st year become an all-rounder (the dig, the block that waits, a 2nd-ball set, the serve at the right man, standing at the edge of the block's shadow); once, out of habit, Sho's hand twitches toward the rule: "Burning?" "...No." "Then stay on." Ukai's word: IQ. Hinata and Kageyama "back in action" around here (canon ch 90; theirs).

## Chapter 38: Hand up (Ukai)

Ukai Keishin couldn't sleep on a futon. He never had. At home he slept on a proper bed above the shop, with the delivery trucks going past at 4 and the fridge compressor downstairs going on and off all night, and that was fine. On a classroom floor in Saitama, with 13 teenagers snoring and Takeda talking in his sleep about lesson plans, he lay awake from 3 and gave up at 5:30.

By 6 he was standing at the vending machines outside Shinzen's main gym with a can of black coffee and a cigarette he hadn't lit. He held it between 2 fingers anyway. It gave his hand something to do.

The mist was still lying in the trees. Through the high windows of the gym he could see 3 figures and hear the thump of balls. The small 1 at the service line was Nekoma's libero, Yaku. The enormous 1 in the middle of the court, arms everywhere, was Nekoma's new middle, Lev. And the 3rd, standing a little apart from Lev with his weight easy and his arms out, was his own 1st year.

Kiyose.

Ukai watched him pass 6 in a row onto a strip of tape by the net. Then a 7th. On the 8th the ball came off his arms half a step wide, and the little libero said something short, and Kiyose nodded and went back to his spot and started again from 1 without any face about it at all.

He'd been doing that every morning. Nobody had told him to. Ukai had found out on the 2nd day from Takeda, who'd found out from Nekomata, who'd mentioned it the way you'd mention that somebody's dog had been sleeping in your garden: *Your tall 1 turned up at 6 and begged Yaku to bully him.*

Ukai drank his coffee.

He'd been coaching since spring. Most days, if he was honest, and at 6 in the morning outside somebody else's gym he could be, it felt like exactly what it was: a man of 26 who ran a convenience store, standing in other people's gyms in a tracksuit, pretending. His grandfather had taken Karasuno to the nationals. His grandfather was a name other coaches said in a particular voice. *Old Ukai's grandson,* people called him at tournaments, before they called him anything else.

And now there was a 1st year who saw things on a court before he did.

He'd known it since May, really. Since the practice match, when the kid had muttered on the bench about Nekoma's back row shifting a step every time their setter touched the ball, and Ukai had looked, and it was true, and he hadn't seen it. His pride had wanted to be annoyed about that. He'd let it, for a week or so. Then he'd started reading the notebook.

Inside the gym Kiyose passed 10 in a row onto the tape, and the little libero stood at the service line looking at him, and said something Ukai couldn't hear, and Kiyose bowed.

Ukai put the cigarette back behind his ear and went to wake his team.

---

At breakfast Hinata and Kageyama were shouting at each other.

Ukai stopped in the doorway of the dining hall with his tray, and at 1st he didn't understand why the sound made his shoulders come down. Then he did. It was the 1st time in 3 weeks.

"...you said you'd SET it! You said if I came early you'd SET it!"

"I'm SETTING it! You're not HITTING it!"

"Because it's not THERE!"

"It IS there! You're looking at the wrong PLACE!"

The whole Karasuno table was eating with their heads down, trying not to grin. Tanaka had his face in his rice bowl. Suga was looking at the ceiling with wet eyes. Daichi caught Ukai's eye across the hall and nodded, once.

Ukai sat down at the end of the table and ate his miso soup. They were talking. He didn't care what about.

---

Before the 1st game he took them through hitting lines.

He stood at the net beside Kageyama with his arms folded and watched them come in, 1 after another, the way he'd watched hitters come in for 3 years at Karasuno, when he'd been the setter and his grandfather had stood at the net exactly like this, saying nothing and seeing everything. Ukai had set 1000s of balls in those 3 years. He knew what a hitter looked like from 2 meters away. He knew it in his hands.

Asahi came in, big and clean and, this morning, not scared of anything, which was new and still made Ukai want to cross his fingers. Tanaka came in all noise and elbows. Daichi, neat. Hinata, who went up so early the ball had to come and find him.

Kiyose came in.

Ukai had watched him hit before. In May, in June, at Seijoh. He'd watched him hit as a sub, as a back-row passer, as a kid who got put on to receive and was allowed to swing if the ball happened to come his way. He'd been watching for the eyes. Everybody had been watching for the eyes.

He watched the arm this time.

The approach was raw, a hair slow on the last 2 steps, so he had to reach a little for Kageyama's set. But the plant was clean, both feet down together, and the left knee bent straight over the toe, which most boys his age didn't manage. He went up. Both arms came up high together, and then the left stayed up, pointing, while the right went back, elbow high, shoulders turned, a bow drawn all the way. Then the hips came round, and the trunk, and the shoulder, and the arm last, in that order, and he met the ball at the very top of his reach in front of his right shoulder and snapped it.

Cross. Into the floor. A sound like a door slamming in an empty house.

Kiyose landed softly on both feet and jogged round to the back of the line.

Ukai stood at the net with his arms folded and watched 4 more. 3 of them were the same. 1 went into the net because he'd run under the ball.

The kid had been hitting at about 70 percent all week. Ukai could see that now: the careful swing of a boy who'd been put on to pass. And under it, the whole time, there'd been this, and Ukai had been too busy looking at what the kid could see to look at what he could do. The kid had let him, because he was 15 and polite, and a 1st year doesn't walk up to his coach and say *put me on the wing*.

Ukai had never even asked what position he'd played in middle school. He suspected the answer was none. He suspected that didn't matter.

Asahi was graduating in March.

He walked to the bench, where Takeda was sitting with the clipboard.

"Sensei. Is there a weight room at Karasuno?"

Takeda blinked. "There's a room with some weights in it. Behind the 1st gym. The baseball club used it, I think, before they moved. I don't know who has the key."

"Find out," said Ukai. "When we get home."

Takeda looked at him, and then at the hitting line, where Kiyose was jogging round again, and nodded.

---

"Lineup," said Ukai, in the huddle, reading it off the clipboard. "Kageyama, Hinata, Tsukishima, Daichi, Asahi. Nishinoya. Kiyose on the wing. Tanaka's spot. Whole set."

The huddle went quiet.

Tanaka looked at him. Then at Kiyose. Then back.

Ukai had known Tanaka would be the 1 who'd have to take this. He'd thought about it at 3:40 that morning, on the classroom floor. Tanaka had started every game since May. Tanaka had worked for it like a dog.

Tanaka was quiet for about 2 seconds, the longest Ukai had ever known him be quiet in a huddle.

Then he grinned, a big ugly generous grin, and hit Kiyose on the back so hard the kid took a step forward.

"Don't be bad," said Tanaka.

"I'll try not to, senpai."

"If you're bad I'm going in."

"Same rule as always," said Ukai, to Kiyose. "Hand up, you come off."

"Yes, Coach."

They went out. Ubugawa again, the jump serve school. Ukai sat on the bench next to Takeda with his arms folded, and Tanaka sat on Takeda's other side with his elbows on his knees and his eyes on the court.

---

Ubugawa had learned.

On the 2nd day the kid had aced their #9 by listening to his passes in warm-up, which Ukai still didn't entirely believe, though he'd watched it happen. Today #9 wasn't in the passing line at all. They'd tucked him up near the net and put their libero and 2 strong passers across the back. Weak man, hidden.

Kiyose's 1st serve came round in the 3rd rotation.

He walked to the end line and bounced the ball twice and looked at Ubugawa's passing line. He'd already seen #9 was gone. His eyes went along the line and stopped on their #1, the 3rd year ace with the long loose arm, standing deep on the left of the passing line.

He served at him.

A plain float, flat and slow, at #1's chest. #1 passed it fine. And then #1 had to turn and run out to the left antenna and start his approach from a stand, half a step behind, and Ubugawa's setter saw he was late and set the middle instead, and Tsukishima was standing there.

Block. Karasuno's point.

"He served their ace," said Takeda, beside him. "Their best passer. Why?"

"Because then the ace has to pass," said Ukai. "If the ace passes, he's late to hit, or he's tired when he hits. Either way their setter's got 1 less good option. It's an old trick. Your 1st serve at a team you don't know, you go at the guy who's going to hit next." He watched the kid walk back to the end line with his face doing nothing at all. "I didn't teach him that."

He'd have liked to have taught him that.

---

He watched the whole set the way he'd never let himself watch the kid before, with the eyes left out of it and everything else in.

In the back row Kiyose stood just off the outside edge of Tsukishima's block. Ukai didn't know where he'd picked that up. Last week he'd stood in the hitter's line, the way readers did. This week, every time Tsukishima went up, the kid moved half a step until he was behind the outside of Tsukishima's hands, and twice in the set a ball came off those hands and came straight to him.

In the front row he blocked Ubugawa's right side. The 1st time, he went up with the setter's hands, early, the way he always had, and the hitter waited for him to come down and hit over him. Ukai opened his mouth.

The next time he didn't need to. The kid waited. He stood at the net with his hands up and his knees bent and waited, a beat and a half, longer than it looked possible to wait, and went up late and soft with his hands angled back. The ball came off them and went up high into his own court, straight to Nishinoya.

"NICE TOUCH!" Noya shouted, and passed it, and Hinata hit it down, and it went in.

On the bench Tanaka said, "Huh," under his breath.

At 11-13 Kageyama dug a ball flat out on the floor, and it went up off his arms, and Karasuno had no setter. Ukai was on his feet. The ball was coming down between Daichi and Kiyose.

"MINE!" said Kiyose.

He got under it. His hands came up square and steady, a passer's hands doing a setter's job, and he put the ball up high and outside to the right, to Asahi, where Ubugawa's block was smallest. High enough that Asahi had all the time in the world.

Asahi used it. 12-13.

Ukai sat down. Takeda was looking at him.

"I didn't know he could set," said Takeda.

"Neither did I," said Ukai.

---

At 15-18 came the ball nobody wanted.

Ubugawa's serve went at Daichi, hard, and Daichi got it up but sideways, off the court toward the bench, and Nishinoya ran it down with his whole body and threw it back over his own shoulder, blind and high, toward the left antenna. No setter near it. Most kids would have put it over soft and let Ubugawa have it.

Kiyose was already out wide. He'd turned and run to the left the moment Daichi's pass went off, before Noya had reached it. He took the ball at the antenna, a bad ball, too tight to the net and drifting, with 3 Ubugawa blockers lining up in front of him.

He swung at it. Full swing, the whole bow, into the middle of the triple block.

It came straight back down off their hands onto Karasuno's side, and nobody could cover it.

15-19.

Ukai didn't move. Beside Takeda, Tanaka sat up.

Kiyose got up off the floor and looked at the 3 blockers for a second, the way he'd looked at the tape on Yaku's floor at 6 in the morning. Then he went back to his spot.

Ukai had seen aces get blocked like that and go small. He'd watched Asahi go small for a whole year. A boy stuffed at 15-19 usually tapped the next 1, played safe, asked for nothing.

3 rallies later Kageyama set him again, a good ball this time, high on the left, with 2 Ubugawa blockers coming across to meet it. Kiyose went up for it with the same full swing. At the top his wrist turned out, and his hand went across the ball from the inside, and the ball went off the outside hand of the end blocker, off the edge of the little finger, and out toward the stands, so fast Ubugawa's libero didn't turn his head.

Karasuno's point.

"YEAAAAAHHH!" Tanaka was on his feet with both fists up, louder than the whole court. "THAT'S MY SPOT YOU'RE BEING GOOD IN!"

Ukai sat still.

He'd seen the kid tool a block before. Against Date Tech, after Aone had stuffed him twice. Off Oikawa's hand, at 24-23 at Seijoh. He'd thought of it then as clever. This was the same hand, 3 rallies after a triple block had put him on the floor, and the kid had gone up for it with a full swing and no fear in his face at all.

---

At 21-22, Ubugawa's #1 came in from the left. Kiyose was in the back row, in the middle, just off the edge of Tsukishima's block. Ukai, who'd been watching him all set, saw him stop.

The kid went completely still for a piece of a second, heels down, the way the notebook said he did, the way Nekoma's setter had read in Tokyo. Then he went, before #1 hit it, 2 full steps back and to the right, and #1's arm came through and hit the ball exactly where the kid was already standing, and it came up off his arms to Kageyama, perfect.

The rally went on. Kageyama set Asahi. Asahi was blocked; it came back up and went over and came back, a long ugly rally with the ball crossing the net 4 times, and Ubugawa finally put it away in the corner. 21-23.

And Kiyose's right hand came up.

It came up off his side, flat, the way the rule said, and stopped about halfway to his shoulder, and hung there.

Ukai was on his feet before he'd decided to be.

He'd made that rule in June, standing under the awning by the drainpipe at the dinner after Seijoh, in the rain, with a boy whose eyes had stopped working together. He'd made it because of 21 all in the 2nd set, when he'd seen the kid miss a free ball he'd have eaten in his sleep, and blink at it, and he'd left him on for the rest of the set anyway, because they were winning and Ukai had wanted it. *It was mine,* he'd told the kid, *and I got it wrong.* He'd had a teammate once, under his grandfather, who'd played the last 2 sets of a match on an ankle that turned out to be broken, and nobody had said a word, because under the old man you didn't. Ukai had made a different rule. His own.

*Right hand, flat, to shoulder height. You come off. No argument. And I don't ask you anything in front of anybody.*

He'd been ready for it all week. At set point, at deuce, at the worst possible moment. He'd decided he'd keep it even then. Especially then.

Tanaka was already up off the bench, already at the substitution zone with the paddle in his hand, his face set. He'd said, back in June, *I'll be watching for it.* He had been.

But halfway wasn't the rule. The rule was shoulder height.

Ukai went to the line and stood there, close to the court, and said it low, so only the kid could hear.

"Oi. Kiyose."

The kid looked at him. His hand was still halfway up. His face was doing that polite nothing it did, but his eyes were on Ukai's, and Ukai looked at them hard. Clear. Dry. Steady.

"Burning?" said Ukai.

The kid didn't answer straight away. Ukai watched him check. He actually checked. His eyes went a little inward, out of focus, the way Ukai's own did at the end of the night when he was counting the till and wanted to be sure. Ukai had never seen anybody check so honestly whether something hurt.

"...No," said Kiyose.

He looked surprised, and the surprise was for his own hand, halfway up in the air, as if it had gone up without asking him.

"Then stay on," said Ukai.

The hand came down. At the substitution zone Tanaka let out a breath and sat back down on the bench with the paddle across his knees.

Ukai went back to the bench too. Takeda was looking at him. Ukai kept his eyes on the court. He found the cigarette was in his fingers, though he couldn't remember taking it from behind his ear, and that his hand wasn't entirely steady, and he put it back.

---

Ubugawa got the next point off a jump serve that Daichi couldn't hold. 21-24.
